Consideration of funding proposals – 
Addendum XVI 
Funding proposal package for FP241 
Summary 
This addendum contains the following six parts: 
a)
A funding proposal summary titled “Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP)
in Indian MSMEs” submitted by Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI);
b)
No-objection letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
c)
Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;
e)
Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's
assessment; and
f)
Gender documentation of the funding proposal.
These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated authority(ies) or 
focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy of the Fund, the funding 
proposal titled “Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in Indian MSMEs” submitted by Small 
Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) is being circulated on a limited distribution basis only to Board 
Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or 
commercially sensitive information of the entity.
 Disclaimer: 
The boundaries and names shown and the designations used including maps, references and citations in this 
Funding Proposal do not imply any official endorsement or acceptance by the Green Climate Fund. The final  
status of Jammu and Kashmir is yet to be decided.

NOTE TO ACCREDITED ENTITIES ON THE USE OF THE FUNDING PROPOSAL TEMPLATE 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

=== PAGE 7 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 3 
 
Climate change problem 
 
India is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and ranks 7th on the Climate Risk Index and 31st on the INFORM 
Risk Index, incurring losses worth $68 billion, annually, due to extreme weather events. 
1. In 2016 India’s total GHG emissions, excluding Land Use Land-Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) were 2,838.89 
million tonne CO2e and 2,531.07 million tonne CO2e with the inclusion of LULUCF. Carbon dioxide continues to 
remain the most emitted GHG. Increased burning of fossil fuels, methane emissions from livestock and increasing 
aluminium and cement production are attributed to this. MSMEs generate around 110 million tonnes of carbon 
dioxide, equivalent to the energy usage of up to 50 million metric tonnes of oil per year from 200 energy-intensive 
manufacturing clusters in India, according to a 2018 report4. MSMEs also face several barriers to become climate 
resilient owing to reasons such as usage of conventional processes and technologies with unskilled / semi-skilled 
manpower, leading to higher resource consumption and associated GHG emissions. The technology advancement 
in these units is usually low due to resource constraints including availability of finance, access to technology, and 
skilled manpower. This provides an opportunity to reduce their resource consumption including electricity, fossil 
fuels, water, etc. in a cost-effective manner.  
Proposed interventions  
 
2. Considering the above, the proposed project is an attempt to sensitise the MSMEs (towards adopting climate 
/cleaner production technologies and develop/deploy adaptation technologies) and to sensitize the banks/FIs 
towards having dedicated climate finance instruments and to lend with increased confidence to meet the demand 
from MSMEs to adopt/develop the necessary climate technologies for mitigation and adaptation interventions. 
Through, SIDBI’s various climate initiatives and the learnings gained thereof, it is strongly felt that MSMEs in India 
can be an important contributor to both the mitigation and adaptation efforts of the Government of India from the 
demand side as well as supply side. The project titled ‘Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Project in Indian MSMEs’ 
is targeted at the following:  
1. Develop and deploy an ecosystem to promote Climate Financing to MSMEs- Utilizing the past learning of 
SIDBI, the programme will set up a blended finance facility targeting the reduction of carbon emissions and 
enhancing the resilience to the impacts of climate change, in line with the requirements of MSMEs. The aim is 
to provide loans at concessional terms to MSMEs either directly or through Participating Financial Institutions 
(PFIs) to develop and deploy technologies for mitigation and adaptation interventions. 
2. Undertake Technology Need Assessments for MSMEs- To facilitate the MSMEs to undertake mitigation 
interventions within their systems and operations, it is imperative to provide handholding support for technology 
needs assessment.  
3. Awareness Generation and Capacity Building of MSMEs- The technology assessment will help to build 
awareness on the climate technologies and create niche market demand for its adoption among the MSMEs. 
Further, the technology need assessment would pave the way for awareness raising and training activities for 
the MSMEs to disseminate the information about low carbon and climate-resilient technologies, as well as 
available financing amongst MSMEs. 
4. Awareness Generation and Capacity Building of Participating Financial Institutions- SIDBI will sensitise 
India's financial sector to the importance and benefits of climate finance with special emphasis on capacity 
building and creating awareness of climate risk assessment to tackle the challenges posed by climate change.  
5. Implement knowledge management and sharing on climate change adaptation and mitigation for 
MSMEs- The learning of the program will be disseminated amongst stakeholders through knowledge products 
to enable large scale replication of technologies for mitigation and adaptation interventions within MSME sector.  
6. Enabling environment for a low-emission and climate-resilient MSME sector- The project seeks to support 
MSMEs operating in the ecosystem of climate adaptation technologies in the various sectors of economy 
including agriculture, agroforestry, water resources, disaster response services, etc.  
7. Develop and implement MSME sectoral adaptation and mitigation benchmarking tools- The program will 
contribute to the upgrading and further development of the online assessment / benchmarking tools for MSMEs.  
 
Climate results/benefits  
• 
Reduction of GHG emissions- The programme will reduce 35.34 million tCO2eq of GHG emissions over 
equipment lifecycle with the adoption of low carbon climate technologies  
• 
Promoting Industrial Decarbonization- The exclusive investment in climate friendly Clean Technologies in 
MSMEs will accelerate industrial decarbonization trajectory of the country through (a) New innovations in climate 
change domain; and (b) Acceleration of the market for climate technologies.  
• 
Promotion of climate financing- A total of ~10000 climate loans will be provided to the eligible MSMEs.

=== PAGE 8 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 4 
 
• 
Energy Saving- The Project will enable saving of more than 27 lakhs MWh energy. Studies indicate that the 
value of the productivity and operational benefits derived can be up to 2.5 times the value of the energy savings.  
• 
Impact on beneficiaries- The programme will impact a total of 10.80 million beneficiaries. Additionally, the 
project will create ~ 420,000 new climate jobs directly related to climate change Mitigation. 
• 
Leveraging Private Investment in climate financing- GCF fund of USD 215.6 million as loan and TA grant will 
potentially leverage USD 3577 million (Co finance of USD 800 million mobilization of 2400 million plus MSME 
promoter’s contribution of USD 377 million) it is associated public and private sector sources, hence making a 
total programme outlay of USD 3792.6 million. The fund will be utilized towards a dedicated investments in MSME 
sector for carbon emission reduction and promoting MSMEs for accelerating adaptation actions. Higher 
concessional finance would help MSMEs in overcoming the barriers of high capital cost as well as increased 
compliance cost.  
• 
Promoting Innovation- The support from the programme to new and young companies and entrepreneurs who 
often exploit technological or commercial opportunities that have been neglected by more established enterprises 
or even challenge the business models of existing companies will unleash climate innovations, and thereby 
facilitate climate low carbon industrialization. This will be done especially in the case of adaptation interventions 
which have to be locally suited and context specific  
• 
Promoting women-based entrepreneurship- Various TA activities including capacity building and financial 
literacy of women workers/civil society representatives will be conducted so that they can become an independent 
entrepreneur. These initiatives will help in increasing the existing women employment ratio in MSMEs.

PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Country context and climate profile 
 
The Indian subcontinent encompasses varied climatic zones including the north covered by Himalayan tundra, the North-west 
covered by arid desert and the north-west, central and North-eastern parts covered by humid climate. The monsoons are crucial 
for the Indian economy and are often regarded as the backbone of the agricultural sector, which employs over half of India’s 
population. They also play a pivotal role in the management of water resources and the generation of hydro-based clean energy. 
However, the variability of the Indian monsoons and the associated wet and dry conditions have direct impacts on socio-economic 
aspects of the country, including the gross domestic product (GDP) (Gadgil and Gadgil 2006; Wang, Gadgil, and Kumar 2006; 
Gulati, Saini, and Jain 2013).  
 
Observed and projected climate change 
 
Temperature 
Observed: 
 
1. India’s average annual mean temperature during 1901-2020 showed an increasing trend of 0.62°C/100 years, with 
significant increasing trend in maximum temperature (0.99°C/100 years) and relatively lower increasing trend 
(0.24°C/100 years) in minimum temperature. Considerably greater warming of 0.14–0.25°C per decade has been 
experienced over the last 30 years. This warming trend has been highest during the post-monsoon season (0.88°C/100 
years) followed by winter season (0.68°C/100 years). India’s average temperature has risen by around 0.7°C during 
1901–2018.5 
Future: 
2. Temperatures in India are projected to increase by ~4°C by 2080-2099 under the RCP8.5 emissions scenario, and 
~1.1°C under the RCP2.6 emissions scenario. By mid-century, projections indicate increases in annual average 
temperatures of 1.5–2.5°C in subtropical regions and 1.2–2.2°C in tropical regions in the country. Warming is stronger 
in annual minimum and maximum temperatures than in the average. Under all emissions scenarios, the rise in annual 
minimum temperatures is 18-21% higher than the rise in average temperatures (Figure 1). Such temperature increases 
are also supported by regional models used for the development of the IPCC’s Sixth Assessment Report (AR6)6 Under 
the SSP2 4.5 scenario, mean temperature in the South Asia region is expected to increase by 0.6–1.3°C by 2040, relative 
to a baseline period of 1980–2010 (Figure 2). Warming on higher emissions pathways is strongly biased towards the 
winter and pre-monsoon months. Such conditions will see an increase in the duration of heat waves and ‘hot’ (when 
temperatures exceed the current average maximums and minimums by 10%) days and nights.

=== PAGE 10 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 6 
 
 
 
7 https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/sites/default/files/country-profiles/15503-WB_India%20Country%20Profile-WEB.pdf  
 
Figure 1: Projected mean temperature for India for all emissions scenarios relative to a reference period of 1986 to 2005 
 
3. As per a World Bank study, unusual and unprecedented spells of hot weather are expected to occur far more frequently 
and cover much larger areas.  Long-term predictions show that the heatwaves will affect more than 300 million people 
by 2050 and lower the quality of life for almost 600 million Indians by 2100. Under 4°C warming, the west coast and 
southern India are projected to shift to new, high-temperature climatic regimes with significant impacts on agriculture.  
Precipitation 
Observed: 
4. While the Indian monsoons, by nature, show high variability through space and time, current climate change trends 
indicate that we are likely to breach global warming records earlier than expected (WMO 2023). A decline in monsoon 
rainfall since the 1950s has already been observed. The frequency of heavy rainfall events has also increased. A very 
recent study by Council for Energy, Environment and Water a think tank based in India has highlighted the following: 
 
Figure 2:  Annual Mean Rainfall (mm) over the period 1991-20207

=== PAGE 11 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 7 
 
 
5. In the past 40 years during the southwest monsoon, it was observed that India as a whole experienced 29 ‘normal’, 8 
‘above-normal’, and 3 ‘below-normal’ monsoon years. However, analysis of these trends at the district level showed that 
approximately 30 per cent of India’s districts witnessed a high number of deficient rainfall years and 38 per cent witnessed 
a high number of excessive rainfall years.. 
6. 55 per cent of tehsils witnessed an increase in southwest monsoon rainfall in the past decade (2012-2022), by more than 
10 per cent compared to the climatic baseline (1982–2011) 
7. While the decreasing trends in southwest monsoon were not statistically significant continuously over forty years, we 
found that nearly 11 per cent of the Indian tehsils (blocks) witnessed a decrease particularly in the past decade (2012-
2022), by more than 10 per cent compared to the climatic baseline (1982– 2011). These are in the Indo-Gangetic plains, 
which contribute to more than half of India’s agricultural production, northeastern India, and the Indian Himalayan region. 
These regions also host fragile but highly diverse ecosystems.  
8. In our study of localised wet rainfall extremes, we found that nearly 64 per cent of Indian tehsils experienced an increase 
in the frequency of heavy rainfall days by 1-15 days per year in the past decade during the southwest monsoon. This 
pattern is prominent in the tehsils of states with the highest GDPs.  
Future: 
 
 
 
 
Figure 3:  Projected Change in Annual Precipitation by 2040-2059 (left) and by 2080-2090 (right) vs 1986-2005  
 
9. A 2°C rise in the world’s average temperatures will make India’s summer monsoon highly unpredictable. At 4°C warming, 
an extremely wet monsoon that currently has a chance of occurring only once in 100 years is projected to occur every 
10 years by the end of the century. An abrupt change in the monsoon could precipitate a major crisis, triggering more 
frequent droughts as well as greater flooding in large parts of India. India’s northwest coast to the south-eastern coastal 
region could see higher than average rainfall.  
Climate hazards and impacts 
10. In 2022 alone, Asia witnessed over 81 natural hazards, with 83 per cent of them being hydro-meteorological. India, in 
particular, incurred significant losses, primarily due to floods caused by the monsoons (WMO 2023).  
Droughts 
11. In monsoon-dominated regions like India, droughts have been recurrent and caused major famines in the 19th and 20th 
centuries The southwest monsoon rainfall in India is the primary source of agricultural wate and groundwater recharge, 
accounting for 80% of the total annual rainfall. Droughts in India due to the weakening of the southwest monsoon are

=== PAGE 12 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 8 
 
 
 
8 https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-023-02856-y  
9 https://www.nature.com/articles/s41597-023-02856-y 
closely linked to Indian Ocean warming and El Nino/Southern Oscillation (ENSO)8.  India is highly vulnerable to drought 
with about two-thirds of its area prone to drought Being an agricultural-dominant country and home to 1.4 billion people, 
droughts in India profoundly impact agricultural productivity, water resource management, and socio-economic well-
being. India has witnessed a rise in the frequency, severity, and duration of droughts over the recent decades, which is 
projected to be further exacerbated by climate cha. With the increasing food demand due to rising population and 
urbanization the impact of droughts is expected to become more severe in the future. 
 
Figure 4: Regions in India expose to extreme drought events and those exposed to compounded drought events 
 
12. Evidence indicates that parts of South Asia have become drier since the 1970s with an increase in the number of 
droughts. Droughts have major consequences. In 1987 and 2002-2003, droughts affected more than half of India’s crop 
area and led to a huge fall in crop production. Droughts are expected to be more frequent in some areas, especially in 
north-western India. Crop yields are expected to fall significantly because of extreme heat by the 2040s.9 
Floods 
13. Extreme precipitation events in India in the past caused flooding, affected economy, and resulted in the loss of lives 
(Wallemacq et al., 2015). Several extreme precipitation events occurred during the recent decades are noteworthy for 
their impacts on society and infrastructure. For instance, extreme precipitation (940 mm in 18 h) event in one of the south-
western states (July 2005) was a disaster that led to flooding affecting 20 million people and causing around 1200 deaths

B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 9 
 
 
 
10 https://economictimes.indiatimes.com/news/new-updates/himalayan-glaciers-risk-losing-75-of-their-volume-by-2100-due-to-global-
warming/articleshow/101130703.cms?from=mdr  
11 https://indianexpress.com/article/technology/science/climate-change-frequent-intense-cyclones-india-west-coast-8969486/  
12 https://www.downtoearth.org.in/news/climate-change/cyclones-getting-fiercer-account-for-nearly-half-of-indias-deaths-due-to-climate-
related-disasters-85587  
(Gupta and Nair, 2011). Similarly, the same state experienced flooding in September 2017 due to extreme precipitation 
(330 mm in 24 h), which largely affected road transportation. (Boyaj et al., 2017; van Oldenborgh et al., 2016). 
 
14. Other than localized flooding, which is mostly caused by extreme precipitation, the river basin scale flooding can be due 
to extreme precipitation and other factors associated with catchment characteristics (Shah and Mishra, 2016; Mishra and 
Lilhare, 2016; Sharma et al., 2018). For instance, extreme precipitation resulted in a large flooding in north India in June 
2013 claiming more than 6000 lives and an estimated loss of $3.8 billion (Kumar, 2013). More recently, extreme 
precipitation in August 2018 in south India caused a widespread flooding and death of more than 440 people (Mishra 
and Shah, 2018; Mishra et al., 2018a). A preliminary estimate of economic loss due to this flood exceeds over $3 billion 
(Gulf News, 30 August 2018). 
 
15. Both flooding and extreme precipitation events are likely to increase under the warmer climate in India (Mukherjee et al., 
2018) posing more risk of economic loss and damage to infrastructure. Dottori et al. (2018) showed that 1.5 °C increase 
in global mean temperature from the pre-industrial level will lead to an increase in human losses from flooding by 70–
80% with a higher risk in South Asia. Rapid and unplanned urbanization further increases the risks of sea water intrusion. 
Melting of Glaciers  
16. According to a recent report, the glaciers in Asia's Hindu Kush Himalaya region are melting at an alarming rate due to 
global warming, which causes both dangerous flooding and water shortage. According to the assessment conducted by 
the International Centre for Integrated Mountain Development (ICIMOD), the glaciers in the region shed ice about 65% 
faster during the 2010s compared to the preceding decade10.The study predicts that by the end of the century, these 
glaciers could lose up to 7 5% of their volume, leading to significant flooding and water shortages for the approximately 
240 million people residing in the mountainous area. Glaciers in the northwestern Himalayas and in the Karakoram range 
- where westerly winter winds are the major source of moisture - have remained stable or even advanced. On the other 
hand, most Himalayan glaciers - where a substantial part of the moisture is supplied by the summer monsoon - have 
been retreating over the past century.  
 
17. At 2.5°C warming, melting glaciers and the loss of snow cover over the Himalayas are expected to threaten the stability 
and reliability of northern India’s primarily glacier-fed rivers, particularly the Indus and the Brahmaputra.  The Ganges 
will be less dependent on melt water due to high annual rainfall downstream during the monsoon season. The Indus and 
Brahmaputra are expected to see increased flows in spring when the snows melt, with flows reducing subsequently in 
late spring and summer. Alterations in the flows of the Indus, Ganges, and Brahmaputra rivers could significantly impact 
irrigation, affecting the amount of food that can be produced in their basins as well as the livelihoods of millions of people 
(209 million in the Indus basin, 478 million in the Ganges basin, and 62 million in the Brahmaputra basin in the year 
2005). 
Cyclones 
18. Since the middle of the 20th century (1951-2018), the frequency of very severe cyclonic storms during the post-monsoon 
season has increased significantly. During 2000-2018, the frequency of severe cyclones has been increasing at the rate 
of 1 event per decade. A zone wise analysis of cyclone hotspots conducted by Council for Energy, Environment and 
Water Resources highlights that the eastern part of India is highly exposed to extreme cyclone events. Further, the 
southern and western areas are more exposed than the northern and north-eastern zones. Various studies show that 
the warming of oceans causes sea levels to rise, causing thermal-hydro expansion, which intensifies the strength and 
frequency of cyclones in the coastal regions of India (Mimura N., 2013). A total of 283 cyclones hit the Indian coastline 
between 1877 and 2005; as many as 106 of these were extreme cyclonic events that affected a 50 km–long strip on the 
east coast of India, and 35 hit the west coast (ADRC, 2012).  Human-induced climate change is causing more frequent 
and intense cyclones on India's west coast, finds a new study11. 
 
A study by National Disaster Management Institute (NDMI)12 highlights the following:

19. Cyclones affect 11 states and Union territories, found an NDMI study Mapping Climatic and Biological Disasters in 
India. There are 96 districts along the coasts officially declared cyclone-prone. 
20. “Cyclones accounted for 48% of India’s overall human life loss due to climate-related disasters, followed by heat waves 
(26 per cent), 18 per cent due to floods and 8 per cent due to cold waves,” - NDMI study. 
21. Due to increase in sea surface temperature and ocean heat content, intensity of cyclones is increasing along Indian 
coasts. “Annual frequency of cyclones significantly reduced from 1951-2018; however, frequency of Very Severe 
Cyclonic Storms during post-monsoon season increased significantly,” according to the study by the researchers from 
the University Of Central Lancashire, UK, the Central University of Tamil Nadu and the University of the West of England, 
UK. 
22. Frequency is rising in Arabian Sea and reducing in the Bay of Bengal, overall. Pre-monsoon frequency is coming 
down on both sides.  
 
Heat waves 
23. In parts of Asia, there is moderate evidence of a rising trend in everyday temperature extremes13. India is amongst the 
most affected countries in Asia. In summer 2019, half of the country was hit by severe heat waves, with temperatures 
reaching above 48 degrees Celsius in numerous regions and 6,100 individuals reported to have died since 201014. Over 
the last 50 years, the length of heat waves in central and northwestern India has increased by about five days15. 
According to the Ministry of Earth Science, the occurrence of heat waves in northwest India has increased by one per 
20 years. The average total span of heat waves grew by two days every ten years, whereas the maximum duration 
climbed by roughly 1.5 days. By the end of the 21st century, the heat waves are projected to be 3 to 4 times more 
frequent, under the RCP8.5 scenario, as compared to 1976-2005 levels16. 
Climate change vulnerability and impacts 
24. In 2019, India was the 7th most affected country17 due to climate change led extreme weather events – both in terms of 
the fatalities (2,267 people) as well as the economic losses (USD 66,182 million). As per a study by Council for Energy, 
Environment and Water, extreme weather events due to climate change has led to 17 out of 20 people in India being 
vulnerable to extreme hydrological and meteorological (or “hydromet”) disasters like flood, drought and cyclone. The 
same study highlights that 75% of Indian states and Union Territories are vulnerable to extreme weather events.  
 
Figure 5: Region wise climate vulnerability in India

=== PAGE 15 ===

25. India ranks 31st on the INFORM Risk Index, incurring losses worth $68 billion, annually, due to extreme weather events. 
The country has a very high absolute exposure to flooding (ranking joint 8th), including riverine, flash and coastal. 
Exposure to tropical cyclones and related hazards (ranking 13th), along with drought is also high (ranking 13th). India’s 
disaster risk is also driven by its vulnerability (ranking joint 31st), which is driven by high levels of social deprivation.  
 
26. According to ND Global Adaptation Initiative Index, the key components comprising vulnerability in India are: Ecosystem 
services (18% of vulnerability), food (19% of vulnerability), human habitat (15% of vulnerability), health (20% of 
vulnerability), infrastructure (10% of vulnerability) and water (17% of vulnerability). Further, India’s socio-economic 
characteristics further add to its vulnerabilities. For example, the country accounts for approximately one third of the 
global poor with ~2 million people being without shelter, ~92 million people lacking access to safe drinking water, and 
approximately one third of the population depending on biomass for their cooking and heating requirements. Accordingly, 
a large proportion of the country is dependent on climate-sensitive agricultural and ecosystem-based livelihoods. 
According to the World Bank, it is estimated that climate change is likely to impact 600 million people across India if the 
growth in GHG emissions is not contained. Therefore, adapting to impacts of climate change has been a high priority for 
India, as rightly outlined in India’s NDCs submitted to the UNFCCC. 
 
Climate house Gas (GHG) emissions profile  
27. As per the Third National Communication (TNC) and Initial Adaptation Communication submitted by India to the United 
Nation’s Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in December 2023, India’s net national emissions in 
2019 stood at 2.6 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e), marking a 4.56 per cent increase from 2016 levels 
and a 115 per cent increase since 1994. Carbon dioxide continues to remain the most emitted GHG, comprising 79 per 
cent of total emissions in 2019. Increased burning of fossil fuels, methane emissions from livestock and increasing 
aluminium and cement production are attributed to this. 
 
 
 
Figure 6: Contribution of various sectors to GHG emissions of India. 
 
28. As per India’s GHG inventory 2019, the contribution of energy sector towards the overall GHG emission stands at 75.80 
per cent, followed by Agriculture sector (13.44 per cent), Industrial Process and Product Use (IPPU) sector accounts for 
0.25 per cent and waste sector accounts for 2.33 per cent.  
 
29. The Energy sector, which includes the consumption of fossil fuels in the Country and their associated fugitive emissions, 
is the main GHG emitting sector in the Country, contributing to 75.81 per cent of the total GHG emissions in 2019 
(excluding LULUCF). The total emissions from the energy sector were 23,74,330 Gg CO2e in 2019, increasing by 11.5 
per cent since 2016, mainly due to the steady increase in the Country’s energy consumption, including the consumption 
of coal and natural gas for power generation and the consumption of liquid fuels for transportation.

30. The IPPU sector, which includes GHG emissions produced by a variety of industrial activities that transform raw materials 
by chemical or physical means, represented 8.41 percent of GHG emissions (without LULUCF) in 2019. The same year, 
their emissions reached 2,63,540 GgCO2e, increasing by 16 per cent since 2016 mainly due to the increase in the 
production of cement, aluminium, and lime.  
 
31. The main GHG emissions from the agriculture sector are methane from livestock’s enteric fermentation and rice 
cultivation and nitrous oxide from manure management and agriculture soil. The agriculture sector represented 13.44 
percent of the total GHG emissions (4,20,968 GgCO2e) in 2019, a decrease of 1 percent compared to 2016. Even though 
in absolute terms, the emissions from agriculture sector increased by 3.22 percent over 2016, the share of emissions 
from agriculture sector is progressively reducing over the years.   
 
32. Land Use and Land-Use Change and Forestry Sector removed 20 per cent of the Country’s carbon dioxide emissions in 
2019. In the same year, its emissions reached -4,85,472 GgCO2e, increasing its status as a sink by 58 per cent since 
2016. 5.3.10 In 2019, the waste sector, which includes GHG emissions accounted for only 2.34 per cent of total GHG 
emissions.  
 
Groundwater  
 
Climate change influences groundwater systems in several ways. In terms of the hydrological cycle, climate change can affect 
the amounts of soil infiltration, deeper percolation, and hence groundwater recharge. Also, rising temperature increases 
evaporative demand over land, which limits the amount of water to replenish groundwater. More than 60% of India’s agriculture 
is rain-fed, making the country highly dependent on groundwater. Even without climate change, 15% of India’s groundwater 
resources are overexploited. The comparison of groundwater extraction between the years 2020 and 2022 (as assessed by the 
Central Ground Water Board (CGWB) and States) indicates decrease in extraction (on an average for the entire country) from 
around 244.92 Billion Cubic Meter (BCM) to 239.16 BCM.  
According to the Central Groundwater Board of India, 17% of groundwater blocks are overexploited, meaning that the rate at 
which water is extracted is higher than the rate at which the aquifer can recharge. Groundwater resources, which support over 
60% of irrigated agriculture and around 85% of domestic water supply in rural areas and 45% of it in urban areas, are already 
under pressure from groundwater pollution and the effects of climate change. 
Impact on Food Security 
The World Food Summit in 1996 defined food security thus: “Food security exists when all people, at all times, have physical, 
social and economic access to sufficient, safe and nutritious food which meets their dietary needs and food preferences for an 
active and healthy life.” According to this definition, there are three main dimensions to food security: food availability, access to 
food, and food absorption.  Food security is one of the leading concerns associated with climate change. Climate change affects 
food security in complex ways. It impacts crops, livestock, forestry, fisheries and aquaculture, and can cause grave social and 
economic consequences in the form of reduced incomes, eroded livelihoods, trade disruption and adverse health impacts. 
However, it is important to note that the net impact of climate change depends not only on the extent of the climatic shock but 
also on the underlying vulnerabilities. According to the Food and Agriculture Organization (2016), both biophysical and social 
vulnerabilities determine the net impact of climate change on food security.18Even without climate change, world food prices are 
expected to increase due to growing populations and rising incomes, as well as a greater demand for biofuels. While overall rice 
yields have increased, rising temperatures with lower rainfall at the end of the growing season have caused a significant loss in 
India’s rice production. Without climate change, average rice yields could have been almost 6% higher (75 million tons in absolute 
terms). Recent studies shows that wheat yields peaked in India and Bangladesh around 2001 and have not increased since 
despite increasing fertilizer applications. Observations show that extremely high temperatures in northern India - above 34°C - 
have had a substantial negative effect on wheat yields, and rising temperatures can only aggravate the situation. Seasonal water 
scarcity, rising temperatures, and intrusion of sea water would threaten crop yields, jeopardizing the country’s food security. 
Should current trends persist, substantial yield reductions in both rice and wheat can be expected in the near and medium term. 
Under 2°C warming by the 2050s, the country may need to import more than twice the amount of food-grain than would be 
required without climate change.

Impact on Energy Security 
Climate-related impacts on water resources can undermine the two dominant forms of power generation in India - hydropower 
and thermal power generation - both of which depend on adequate water supplies to function effectively. To function at full 
efficiency, thermal power plants need a constant supply of fresh cool water to maintain their cooling systems. Climate-related 
impacts on water resources can undermine the two dominant forms of power generation in India - hydropower and thermal power 
generation - both of which depend on adequate water supplies to function effectively. To function at full efficiency, thermal power 
plants need a constant supply of fresh cool water to maintain their cooling systems. The increasing variability and long-term 
decreases in river flows can pose a major challenge to hydropower plants and increase the risk of physical damage from 
landslides, flash floods, glacial lake outbursts, and other climate-related natural disasters. Decreases in the availability of water 
and increases in temperature will pose major risk factors to thermal power generation. 
 
Climate Change and Impact on Health  
Climate change is expected to have major health impacts in India- increasing malnutrition and related health disorders such as 
child stunting - with the poor likely to be affected most severely. Child stunting is projected to increase by 35% by 2050 compared 
to a scenario without climate change. Malaria and other vector-borne diseases, along with and diarrheal infections which are a 
major cause of child mortality, are likely to spread into areas where colder temperatures had previously limited transmission. 
Heat waves are likely to result in a very substantial rise in mortality and death, and injuries from extreme weather events are 
likely to increase. Heatwaves could slow or reverse India’s progress towards Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) without 
heat impact assessments Deadly heatwaves fuelled by climate change in 2022 made almost 90 percent of Indians more 
vulnerable to public health issues, food shortages and increased risks of death19.

rainfall events more accurately, providing crucial lead time for

Policy and Regulation: Implementing policies that integrate 
climate change adaptation into development planning is critical. 
This includes enforcing building codes that require flood 
resilience and managing land use to prevent deforestation, which 
exacerbates flood risk (Maharana et al., 2020).

Infrastructure Enhancement and Protection: Strengthening

exacerbated by rising sea levels. This also includes elevating

(Nicholls, 2011).

Ecosystem-Based Adaptation: Preserving and restoring 
mangroves, coral reefs, and other natural buffers that absorb the 
impact of storm surges and high waves. These ecosystems not 
only protect the coastline but also provide habitat for biodiversity 
and support fisheries (Byravan, Rajan, & Rangarajan, 2010). 
• 
Relocation and Retreat: Implementing planned relocation for 
communities most at risk from sea level rise, ensuring that new 
settlements are built in safer areas. This strategy is often 
considered as a last resort but is becoming increasingly 
necessary in highly vulnerable areas (Khristodas et al., 2023). 
• 
Policy and Regulation Enhancements: Updating coastal zone 
management policies to restrict development in high-risk areas, 
promoting sustainable land use practices, and ensuring that all 
new constructions are resilient to future sea level rise. Policies 
should also ensure that adaptation efforts are integrated into 
broader urban and regional planning frameworks (Almeida & 
Mostafavi, 2016). 
• 
Community Engagement and Capacity Building: Educating 
and involving local communities in adaptation planning to ensure 
they have the knowledge and resources to cope with the impacts 
of sea level rise. Community-driven approaches can lead to more 
sustainable and accepted solutions (Natesan & Parthasarathy, 
2010).

Rainwater Harvesting and Storage: Implementing rainwater 
harvesting systems to capture and store rainfall for use during dry 
periods. This approach includes building tanks, reservoirs, and 
other storage facilities to collect rainwater from rooftops and land 
surfaces, which can be used for agriculture, drinking, and other 
domestic purposes (Roll & Andrade, 2016). 
• 
Improving Irrigation Efficiency: Adopting modern irrigation 
technologies such as drip and sprinkler systems, which 
significantly reduce water waste and increase water use 
efficiency in agricultural practices. These systems deliver water 
directly to the plant roots, minimizing evaporation and runoff 
(Kumar et al., 2023). 
• 
Grey Water Recycling: Implementing grey water treatment 
systems for recycling and reusing wastewater from domestic 
activities (except toilet flushing) for irrigation and industrial 
processes. This strategy reduces freshwater demand and 
pressure on existing water resources (Sushmitha, Chanakya, & 
Khuntia, 2019). 
• 
Community-Based Water Management: Engaging local 
communities in managing water resources through participatory 
governance models. This involves training and empowering 
community members to manage local water resources 
sustainably, including decisions on water allocation and 
conservation practices (Varma et al., 2021). 
• 
Policy and Institutional Support: Developing and implementing 
policies that support sustainable water management, including 
incentives for water-saving technologies, regulations on water 
use, and investments in water infrastructure to enhance supply 
and distribution systems (Basu & Shaw, 2013).

Risk Assessment and Monitoring: Extensive use of remote 
sensing and GIS technologies aids in monitoring glacial lakes and 
assessing their burst potential. This allows for timely updates and 
risk assessments of vulnerable areas (Dubey & Goyal, 2020). 
• 
Infrastructure Adaptation: Strengthening and designing 
infrastructure to withstand potential floods, including the 
reinforcement of existing dams and the strategic placement of 
new ones. This includes the integration of GLOF considerations 
into the design of hydroelectric projects, ensuring they account for 
possible outburst floods (Jain et al., 2012). 
• 
Early Warning Systems: Implementation of early warning 
systems that can provide immediate alerts to downstream 
communities in the event of a GLOF, enhancing community 
response and evacuation procedures. 
• 
Community Engagement and Preparedness: Educating and 
involving local communities in disaster risk reduction strategies, 
including evacuation drills and the development of local response 
plans. This also involves enhancing local capacities to respond to 
emergencies. 
• 
Artificial Lowering of Lake Levels: Techniques such as 
siphoning or controlled drainage have been proposed to lower the 
water levels in lakes deemed to be at high risk of causing GLOFs, 
thus reducing the pressure on natural or moraine dam barriers 
and lowering the risk of sudden breaches (Kattelmann & 
Watanabe, 1996).

Infrastructure Resilience: Strengthening buildings, 
communication systems, and transportation networks to 
withstand cyclonic conditions. This includes constructing cyclone-

ensure timely evacuation and response (Kakumanu et al., 2018).

ensure that adaptation strategies are comprehensive and 
inclusive (Winchester, 2000).

Financial Implication of Climate Change  
33. A report revealed that in 2022, India suffered a loss of 8% in GDP compared to a hypothetical world without climate 
change22. Further, a latest report by The Reserve Bank of India predicts that up to 4.5 per cent of India’s GDP could be 
at risk by 2030 due to climate change related impacts23.   
 
34. The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) has focused on climate change as the primary theme in its Report on Currency and 
Finance (RCF) for 2022-23, a theme-based annual report on contemporary issues concerning central banking and 
macroeconomic issues in India. This year’s report emphasizes climate goals as a policy priority and analyses the potential 
macro-financial consequences of climate change for India. 
 
35. The theme for this report, published on May 3, 2023 is “Towards a Greener Cleaner India” and it claims that fiscal policy 
has a prominent role in driving the green transition, from fossil fuels to renewables, and highlights the urgent need for a 
green taxonomy, a classification system that determines whether an investment is sustainable or not.  
 
36. The report looks into the physical indicators of climate change and policy action globally and in India. It goes on to discuss 
the macroeconomic effect of climate change in India, climate change and the financial sector, and policy options to 
mitigate climate risks.“ India’s goal of achieving the net zero target by 2070 would require an accelerated reduction in 
the energy intensity of GDP by around 5% annually and a significant improvement in its energy mix in favour of 
renewables to around 80% by 2070-71. India’s climate financing requirement is estimated to be at least 2.5% of GDP 
annually till 2030,” says the report, quantifying the scale of the effort needed towards climate action. Financial stability, 
the core mandate of central banks around the world, can face risks due to climate change, says the RCF report, as it 
can affect the valuation of financial assets by influencing investors’ risk perceptions. 
 
37. The pressure of currency depreciation in countries frequently affected by climate disasters can cause financial instability, 
higher import costs, and negative terms of trade. In this context, central banks around the globe are gradually broadening 
their objectives beyond the original scope or focus, the report says. 
 
38. Giving other examples of how climate change can affect inflation and financial assets, the report says that climate change 
can affect price stability through supply shocks such as food and energy shortages. 
 
39. Additionally, demand shocks can arise when firms and households lose wealth on account of frequent natural disasters. 
Physical and transition risks can affect the balance sheets of financial institutions and banks, limiting the flow of credit to

purposes, bringing down the real equilibrium interest rate, which is the interest rate at which the demand for money 
matches the supply. 
 
 
MSMEs 
40. The MSME sector plays a pivotal role from various economic and social perspectives in India. The share of gross value 
added (GVA) from the MSME sector in India’s GDP was 30.0%24 and the share of exports was 49.8% in 2019-20. The 
MSME sector provides the highest employment among the non-agriculture sectors in India, and over 90% of MSMEs in 
the country are micro-units. The informal sector (excluding the agriculture sector) employs 68.4%25 of the employable 
workforce in India as per the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) for 2018-19. 
41. Definition and Categorisation of MSMEs in India:  
Enterprise 
Category 
Criteria based on investment in plant & machinery / Equipment & turnover 
Micro 
Investment up to INR 1 crore (~USD 125,000) and turnover up to INR  
5 crore (~USD 625,000) 
Small 
Investment up to INR 10 crore (~USD 1.25 million) and turnover up to INR 50 
crore (~USD 6.25 million) 
Medium 
Investment up to INR 50 crore (~USD 6.25 million) and turnover up to INR 250 
crore (~USD 31.25 million) 
 
42. The MSME sector in India comprising of around 63.88 million units engaged in either manufacturing or services or trading 
activities, creating employment for about 110 million people, contributing about 45% to manufacturing output and about 
40% of exports, is an energy intensive sector, which can contribute to achievement of NDC targets. There are over 7,000 
products, ranging from traditional to high-tech, that are manufactured by Indian MSMEs today. The sector is the biggest 
employer in India outside the agriculture sector. Currently, MSME sector is characterized by resource intensive 
technology profile. The sector consumes about 25% of the total energy consumed by the industrial sector in India. Trend 
analysis in the sector from 2012-13 to 2019-20 indicates that 6.8% year on year growth has been observed in energy 
consumption including both thermal and electrical energy consumption. The projected energy consumption shows a 
steep increase (by more than 100%) from 30 million toe in 2016-17 to 72.17 million toe in 2029-30. This sector faces 
barriers like limited access to technology, capacity to adopt new technologies and financing. The barriers in MSME sector 
towards adopting climate resilient technologies is also highlighted in the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) 
submitted by Government of India to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

43. The major energy intensive MSME subsectors include: food processing, textile, pulp and paper, chemicals, glass and 
ceramics, brick kilns, foundry, sponge iron, steel re-rolling, forging, pharmaceuticals, rubber and plastic, leather, cement, 
etc.. The energy intensive MSME sub-sectors contribute significantly to India’s economy.   
 
44. Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises and the informal sectors will require considerable financial support, technology 
transfer and capacity building support to achieve a just transition to low-carbon growth and to be climate resilient. Thus, 
the proposed project aims to bridge the gap in investment and technical support for upscaling climate mitigation and 
adaptation technologies in MSME landscape by providing low-cost targeted capital to prepare the MSME sector to the 
challenges and opportunities posed by the India’s low carbon and net-zero development trajectory. Although Indian 
MSMEs are a diverse and heterogeneous group, they face common problems related to, inter alia, lack of awareness, 
limited technical know-how, low technology levels, limited access to modern technologies, and high cost of capital.  
45. Most Indian companies are yet to completely grasp the implications of climate change and how it will affect them. This is 
particularly true for the Indian MSME sector where managing the immediate challenges of everyday business remains a 
higher priority than addressing long term risks. At the same time, the MSME sector is also most vulnerable to climate 
risks since most within the sector lack the financial resources to endure even a single rupture in supply or demand, 
caused for instance by an extreme weather event or a new environmental or climate regulation. 
46. The MSMEs located in vulnerable regions, prone to climate hazards are either not aware of the adaptation technologies 
that could make their units resilient or are perceived as riskier by the PFIs, thus restricting flow of capital to finance 
climate resilient interventions.  
 
MSMEs role in low carbon transition  
 
47. MSMEs in energy intensive manufacturing sectors have high potential for emission reduction by adopting renewable 
energy sources, low carbon alternative sources of energy, energy and resource efficiency measures in the production 
value chain, battery storage, and other low carbon technologies. Adoption of clean energy sources in MSMEs will be 
critical for India’s transition towards net-zero economy, in line with the announcement made by India at UNFCCC COP26. 
Thus, there has been momentum for accelerating decarbonisation of Indian industries of which MSME sector has been 
a key stakeholder by adopting appropriate technologies and solutions such as cleaner fuel, common combustion facilities 
and energy efficiency technologies.

27 These values exclude land use emissions. The 1.5°C fair share ranges for 2030 and 2050 are drawn from the Climate Action Tracker

such strong reductions by domestic emissions reductions, supplemented by contributions to global emissions reduction efforts via, for

Mitigation Needs 
48. India will need to reduce its emissions to below 4,500 MtCO₂e by 2030 and to below 3,200 MtCO₂e by 2050 to be within 
its fair-share range compatible with global 1.5°C IPCC scenarios27. Through its Long-Term Low-Carbon Development 
Strategy, India has committed to a 45% reduction in the GHG emissions per unit of GDP by 2030 as compared to 2005 
levels, with the country aiming to reach net zero by 207028. It is estimated that USD 2.5 Trillion (at 2014-15 prices) will 
be required for meeting India’s climate targets between 2015 and 2030. This implies USD 170 Billion per year for climate 
action, however the average climate finance flow was USD 19 Bn per annum from 2016-1829. In the country’s energy 
sector, coal has continued to lead electricity generation, with the country having the second-most coal intensive electricity 
sector among the G20 in 2020. At sub-national level, the State Action Plan on Climate Change includes strict regulations 
toward reducing coal footprints. However, other government policies such as more than doubling the coal consumption 
limit and the one billion tonne coal production target could increase power sector emissions by an estimated 1,427 
MtCO2e. Renewable energy is being promoted, and in 2015 India set a target to install 175 GW from renewable energy 
sources by 2022, of which 100 GW will be from solar photovoltaic (PV), 60 GW from wind, 10 GW from biomass, and 5 
GW from small hydropower station30. However, the renewable energy sector added less than 60% of its targeted capacity 
in 2018-2019, highlighting the needs to strengthen policy framework and manufacturing capacity to ensure long-term 
growth of the sector. Furthermore, the cost of the solar PVs has been reduced drastically in the past decade and the 
generation cost is lesser than thermal power plants generation. The investment in large scale renewable projects is also 
slowly flowing in. However, investments for MSME sector in renewables are not forthcoming due to perceived investment 
risks, smaller scale investments, regulations for RE integrations31. Considering both aspects, banks and financing 
institution in India follows a restrictive and conservative approach. Thus, addressing investment gaps in renewables in 
MSME sector is critical for achieving the NDC targets of the country and facilitating the sector towards low carbon 
pathways. Civil society experts have noted that the next focus of India’s emission mitigation policy, cantered largely on 
electricity generation, must be expanded to the industrial sector and particularly in the MSME sector and that it is critical 
to devise ways for the sector to reduce dependence on fossil energy while ensuring rapid growth in manufacturing and 
associated jobs and enhancing international competitiveness32. In the MSME sector, emissions per unit of production is 
generally higher than that of the corresponding large enterprises partly due to use of obsolete technologies and poor 
management practices. The sector faces further barriers like non-availability of technology or limited adaptability to 
changing technology. Moreover, MSMEs often find it hard to invest in latest technology due to various technical and non-
technical limitations leading to limited priority for sustainability issues. As a result, the MSME sector consumes ~25% of 
the total energy utilised by the industrial sector while generating significant amount of total industrial emissions in India33.  
 
49. Energy consumption (thermal and electrical) in the MSME sector has been projected to increase by more than 100% 
from 30 million tons of oil equivalents (mtoe) in 2016-17 to 72.17 mtoe in 2029-2030, necessitating the application of 
resource efficient technologies and practices in the sector to reduce correlated increases in GHG emissions. Detailed 
project reports and energy audits conducted under several SIDBI / BEE programs identify low-cost technologies, which 
have demonstrated significant energy savings and the potential to reduce both direct and indirect GHG emissions. Some 
of these Low cost technologies include: installation of APFC (for PF); energy efficient lighting; VFD motors and pumps; 
energy efficient pump and motors; energy efficient compressors; compressed air leakages; fan less cooling towers;

MSME role in adoption of adaptation technologies  
 
50. Adaptation actions have enormous potential for investment from private sector tapping through potential business 
opportunities.  Adaptation actions have enormous potential for investment from private sector tapping through potential 
business opportunities. Although MSMEs are themselves vulnerable to climate change, they still have important roles to 
play in building community resilience. First, they can develop climate-resilient goods and services that are affordable to 
vulnerable populations. MSMEs are closely integrated into their communities, and as a result, have local knowledge of 
consumer demand and supply. This level of understanding can help MSMEs identify areas of need and respond 
accordingly. This may be especially useful for adaptation, since climate change impacts vary depending on where place-
specific climatic, environmental, and socio-economic factors intersect. Second, MSMEs can upgrade their current 
products. In general, small and medium firms don’t have the same governance structure as larger corporations, allowing 
them to adapt their products more rapidly to changes in the climate. Third, MSMEs can directly engage with community 
members to build adaptive practices. All of these actions can go a long way toward helping communities prepare for 
current and future climate change risks. MSMEs, with wide reaching presence across the market and employing up to 
78 percent of the population, are in a better position to get products and services to hard-to-reach populations and 
contribute to making vulnerable populations more climate-resilient 35. MSMEs being locally connected are better 
positioned to develop customized and innovative products and services in areas of agriculture and livestock sector 
including crop diversification, product value chains, and improved micro irrigation and pest management, afforestation, 
clean and affordable drinking water, water harvesting, recycling and Municipal solid waste management, etc. However, 
start-ups, innovators and MSMEs developing such climate-resilient affordable and customized goods and services with 
innovative business models require customized and dedicated financial support to meet their investment needs, which 
hitherto is not available in India.  
Adaptation Needs  
51. A study by Climate Policy Initiative highlights the following barriers to adaptation financing in India (a).  Limited knowledge 
of investment opportunities (b). Lack of capacity to develop commercially feasible projects (c) Unavailability of bankable 
projects and business models36. Also, the adaptation technologies need to be appropriate solution that are locally suited 
and in most of India the technology/service providers are usually start-ups that comes under MSMEs. Hence, the 
objective of this activity is to create a debt instrument tailored to adaptation needs for improved access to finance in 
selected MSMEs. This programme will further focus on regions in India with high climate vulnerability: coastal, eastern 
belt and Indian Himalayan regions and the focus sectors would be agriculture, water resources, agroforestry, disasters 
and services. 
52. The potential impacts of climate change on businesses can be classified as either direct or indirect: Direct impacts of 
climate change affect the physical assets of a business, such as buildings, machinery and equipment or other vital 
infrastructure and Indirect impacts of climate change affect business operations through changes in the business 
environment, such as new regulations and market demand, changes in prices and availability of inputs and financing or 
effects on stakeholders.  Companies that are not prepared to manage the direct and indirect impacts of climate change 
may face competitive disadvantages, especially if their competitors are better prepared or less affected by climate 
impacts.  This is even more true for India’s MSMEs: For most of them it is a higher priority to manage immediate 
challenges instead of long or term risks. At the same time, MSMEs are highly vulnerable to climate risks as they often 
lack the financial resources to endure even a single rupture in supply or demand, caused for instance by an extreme 
weather event or a new environmental or climate regulation37.  
Impact of Climate Change on businesses including MSMEs:  
• 
Rising temperatures: Average temperatures as well as temperature extremes have been increasing and 
projected to further increase across India. For business, temperature rises are likely to have an indirect impact 
such as reduced productivity of workers or increased energy use, as more cooling is required in manufacturing 
processes. Rising temperatures can also negatively affect crop yield and harvests.

=== PAGE 28 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 24 
 
 
 
38 https://www.ifc.org/en/insights-reports/2023/blended-finance-for-climate-investments-in-india  
39 https://rbidocs.rbi.org.in/rdocs/Publications/PDFs/CLIMATERISK46CEE62999A4424BB731066765009961.PDF  
40 GCF categorizes co-benefits into six areas which are: environmental, social, economic, gender, adaptation (relevant for pure mitigation 
projects) and mitigation (relevant for pure adaptation projects). Further guidance is available in the funding proposal (FP) guidance note. 
• 
Precipitation changes: Higher precipitation in some regions, and less rainfall in others have indirect impacts 
on yield, buildings, sewerage and ground water availability  
• 
Loss of freshwater: A report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) states that water and 
its availability and quality will be the main pressures on and issues for societies and the environment under 
climate change, The industry demand for water in India is estimated to more than triple between 2000 and 2025. 
Decreasing availability, declining quality and growing demand for water are likely to have serious repercussions 
for the cost and productivity of companies. Furthermore, water being typically one of the most contentious issues 
with local stakeholder, reputational damages especially for highly water intensive industries are likely.  
• 
Price hike of energy: Climate change affects both supply and demand of electricity: while higher temperatures 
lead to an increased demand for energy, e.g., for air conditioning and cooling, it also affects supply, e.g. by 
damaging business operations of energy providers. At the same time, energy needs in India are expected to 
triple between 2006 and 2025. For all these reasons, energy prices are likely to show greater volatility and 
gradually increase over the next decades. 
• 
Regulatory requirements: Both the need to reduce greenhouse gas emissions for mitigating climate change 
and the need to adapt to the impacts of climate change has already triggered policy action, changing the 
framework conditions for business. More stringent regulation affecting business can be expected in the future, 
especially in the fields of emission reduction and resource consumption. Besides, national and state level 
regulation relevant to all the Indian business, exporting MSMEs need to also anticipate the policies of foreign 
countries.  
Leveraging Private Sector Investment for financing MSMEs  
53. India needs substantial amounts of climate finance by 2050 to achieve its ambitious sustainability goals. An estimated 
USD 10.1 trillion will be required to get to net-zero by 2070. Public investment alone will be insufficient to tackle India’s 
adaptation and mitigation targets, so financing that can bring in private sector investment is vital to scaling climate 
solutions with urgency. As per a recent consultation by SIDBI, the consulted stakeholders responded that Non-Banking 
Financial Companies (NBFCs) do not prominently incorporate the financing landscape for climate technologies into their 
stated objectives. Given the nascent nature of climate technologies, a considerable segment of Large and Medium 
NBFCs adopt a cautious "wait and watch" stance, refraining from explicit commitments to climate financing. As a result, 
the funding of climate initiatives by these NBFCs often takes place incidentally rather than through deliberate strategic 
planning38.  As per the findings of the survey by RBI vide its notification dated July 27, 2022 and also as indicated in 
RBI’s Report on Currency and Finance 2022-23 issued on May 03, 2023, it is observed that the financing flows from the 
banking sector towards climate financing  are presently quite low and there is a need for training, sensitization and 
capacity building of the banking officials in this regard as also putting in place proper systems and processes towards 
climate risk assessments and integrating suitable framework for M&E, ESG & gender, etc. Further, RBI vide its draft 
disclosure framework on climate related financial risk, 2024 vide its notification dated February 28, 2024 has indicated 
glide path with timelines for putting in place suitable systems and processes for climate related risk disclosures by the 
banks and other financial institutions39. Moreover, in the very recent G20 Delhi declaration, the leaders unanimously 
accepted the recommendation of the Sustainable Finance Working Group (SFWG) to scale up climate financing through 
innovating finance models such as blended finance, to leverage private finance and multilateral climate funds. SIDBI has 
been historically working with the PFIs under its institutional finance vertical and realizes the importance to tapping these 
to scale up climate financing for MSMEs. Further, many of these PFIs have better outreach, especially in rural areas, 
thus ensuring that units located in the rural parts of the country are able to maximize from the current intervention.

54. The objective of the programme is to develop and demonstrate an ecosystem to promote Climate Financing to MSMEs 
in mitigation and adaptation interventions. To achieve this objective, GCF funding is sought in the form of non-
reimbursable funds (grant) of up to USD 15.6 million for technical assistance, and in the form of reimbursable funds 
(loan) in the amount of up to USD 200 million GCF reimbursable funds will be blended with co-finance from SIDBI of up 
to USD 800 million.  Out of total financing, SIDBI will on-lend (i) USD 200 million directly to MSMEs, and (ii) USD 800 
million to NBFC/FIs as on lending / refinance support. To achieve results at a scale, the project has potential to leverage 
private sector investment worth USD 2400 million. The concessional funds are expected to trigger the market towards 
climate investments.

57. The project’s outcomes will contribute to the achievement of the overarching goal of the programme: IF MSME mitigation 
and adaptation technologies are financed and implemented, MSMEs gain improved access to finance, knowledge 
management is improved, capacities of selected MSMEs are enhanced to upscale their business and climate impact, 
and an enabling environment is created for a low-emission climate-resilient MSME sector; THEN investment for climate 
change projects in India’s MSME sector will be unlocked and the country’s industrial decarbonization trajectory will be 
accelerated; BECAUSE MSMEs will have access to finance, technology and capacity building support to become 
resilient and developing products promoting adaptation measures. The project’s Theory of Change is described below, 
followed by a diagram summarising its causal pathways and results chains.  
 
Key barriers and challenges 
 
58. Despite the urgent imperative and scope for low carbon transition of sector, MSMEs have limited financial capacity to bear 
the transition cost associated with replacing high emitting machineries and infrastructure and adopting climate mitigation 
technologies. The current incentive and financing schemes are limited from the perspective of MSMEs especially given the 
inherent challenges and barriers of financing the MSME sector in itself. As per the study of International Finance 
Corporation41, only 16 percent of MSMEs in India are financed through formal banking systems and the credit gap to MSMEs 
is estimated to be about USD 240 billion (about INR 16.66 trillion as per 2018 estimates). In addition to inadequate access to 
finance and high cost of capital, MSMEs also lack access to markets, insurance, climate-smart inputs and services, and 
knowledge about mitigation and adaptation technologies and options. A recent International Energy Agency study42 indicates 
that technologies are readily available today to achieve most of the carbon emissions through 2030. However, MSMEs often 
use outdated technologies which are energy intensive due to inadequate access to finance and lack of technological expertise 
and knowledge. For example, small and medium sized boilers used in many MSMEs are powered by coal and are significantly 
inefficient, i.e., by up to 30-35 percent less efficient43. Due to the inherent small size and lack of skilled human resources and 
awareness of technical know-how of climate technologies, MSMEs also have limited adoption of climate resilient and low 
carbon technologies and practices. A summary of the key barriers and the corresponding programme activities that addresses 
the barriers are detailed below:

59. A series of stakeholder consultations conducted as a part of this programme, highlighted the key barriers across this segment 
on adopting climate technologies across adaptation and mitigation as indicated below:  
Sector  
Key Barriers  
Solar- Open Access  
• 
Lack of partnership opportunities for large developers with MSMEs: Low 
corporate ratings & small-scale/disaggregated opportunity leads to higher risk 
preventing large developers to partner with MSMEs. 
• 
Opportunities available only to stable firms: Due to the risky nature of the 
segment, financing is only limited to small/medium sized businesses with large 
revenues (>INR 10-20 Cr). Micro enterprises aren’t able to leverage such 
financing. 
 
Roof Top Solar 
• 
Limited awareness (>50% MSMEs across clusters weren't aware of RTS) 
• 
Technical Challenges i.e., limited by availability of rooftop space 
• 
Financing Challenge (Limited by ability to put capital upfront) 
• 
RESCO model not developed given low offtaker rating and challenges in 
collection at disaggregated level 
 
Energy Efficiency  
• 
Inherent inability to access capex easily limits the degree to which they can 
adopt these interventions within their operations

=== PAGE 33 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 29 
 
 
• 
Low uptake given lack of after sales support ecosystem: Installation of novel 
technology requires strong after sales support. However, the ecosystem 
currently lacks these services. 
• 
High transaction cost: One-time cost of shift to energy efficient 
technology/processes is high and difficult for MSMEs to bear. With a lack of 
collateral, a lot of them aren’t able to finance this transition. 
• 
Information failure: Inadequate data on unit level energy consumption, energy 
savings achieved till date from different interventions, and inadequate 
dissemination of knowledge make the decision of investing in energy saving 
technology difficult. 
Electric Vehicle  
• 
Economic: The price of electric vehicles is more than their ICE counterparts. 
In the case of electric buses and trucks, the initial cost is almost two to three 
times higher than their ICE counterparts. The higher upfront cost will translate 
into owning of lesser vehicles than ICE variants thereby reducing the earning 
potential of the operators.  
• 
Access to finance: At present, the financing terms are not viable for most of 
the operators willing to deploy electric variants. The scheduled commercial 
banks with better financing terms in terms of high loan-to-value ratio (LTV), 
longer tenure, and lower interest rates are reluctant to fund the operators due 
to many perceived risks. The low LTV is translating into higher upfront capital 
required for the purchase of e-vehicles especially in the case of e-bus and 
trucks, and shorter tenure are resulting in high monthly payments making the 
operations unviable.  
• 
Technical: At present, there are few products available in the market in 
comparison to diesel models. The adequate product is available in the two-
wheeler segment and three-wheeler cargo segment but are missing from the 
other product categories. The buses are used for different buses such as 
school buses, employee transport, intracity, intercity etc.  and requires models 
of different specifications with suitable price to match the business model. The 
same exists in the freight sector as well.  
• 
Regulatory: At present, the incentives for electric vehicles are available at the 
central government level only with a limited number of vehicles. The demand 
incentives are not available in every state with no clear mandate on the 
transition to electric vehicles.   
• 
Behavioral: The lower total cost of ownership is estimated for electric vehicles 
across different vehicle modes. However, due to the lack of trust in EVs and 
higher upfront cost requirements, entities are reluctant to transition to electric 
mobility. It is accentuated in the case of buses and trucks. 
• 
Structural: The vast network of diesel outlets across the country in 
comparison to meagre public charging stations is one of the major 
apprehensions among potential users. Since batteries come with a limited 
range, it is pertinent to have adequate charging stations to ensure freedom in 
the deployment of electric buses and trucks. The battery warranty available in 
the market is less than the vehicle's operational requirement for the useful life 
of the vehicle. The battery replacement cost is further increasing the capital 
cost of owning buses 
Adaptation 
Narrative and information gaps: 
• 
Information gaps (i.e., poor data and analytics) around climate risks and the 
benefits of timely adaptation, deter investments 
• 
Narrative for action is weak: 'Cost of inaction' not well quantified, existing 
perspectives narrow and mono sectoral 
Lack of/limited Bankability 
• 
Financial return/ROI from A&R projects often perceived as low or non-existent 
• 
Lack of linking A&R projects to socioeconomics returns, thus limited bankability 
• 
Many projects are 'sub scale', have high transaction costs 
• 
Due to a lack of proven effectiveness, many A&R technology solutions have 
garnered limited interest

Consultations with leading global DFIs has revealed a two-fold challenge in disbursing 
adaptive finance in India:  
 
There is a need for credible intermediaries who can act as a bridge between the 
DFIs and the private sector is critical – an intermediary that can bring a strong 
pipeline of investible projects and help unlock 4x-10x private investments will be 
crucial 
 
The biggest challenge to mobilize climate finance is not the availability of capital 
but the lack of innovative financial instruments 
 
 Barriers to climate finance for Indian MSMEs:  
60. India needs substantial amounts of climate finance by 2050 to achieve its ambitious sustainability goals. An estimated 
USD10.1 trillion will be required to get to net-zero by 2070. Public investment alone will be insufficient to tackle India’s 
adaptation and mitigation targets, so financing that can bring in private sector investment is vital to scaling climate solutions 
with urgency. As per a recent consultation by SIDBI, the consulted stakeholders responded that Non-Banking Financial 
Companies (NBFCs) do not prominently incorporate the financing landscape for climate technologies into their stated 
objectives. Given the nascent nature of climate technologies, a considerable segment of Large and Medium NBFCs adopt a 
cautious "wait and watch" stance, refraining from explicit commitments to climate financing. As a result, the funding of climate 
initiatives by these NBFCs often takes place incidentally rather than through deliberate strategic planning44.   
61. As per the findings of the survey by RBI vide its notification dated July 27, 2022 and also as indicated in RBI’s Report on 
Currency and Finance 2022-23 issued on May 03, 2023, it is observed that the financing flows from the banking sector 
towards climate financing  are presently quite low and there is a need for training, sensitization and capacity building of the 
banking officials in this regard as also putting in place proper systems and processes towards climate risk assessments and 
integrating suitable framework for M&E, ESG & gender, etc 
62. Consultations with the wider MSME ecosystem comprising of leading financial institutions (including SIDBI’s on-ground 
branch officers) revealed the need for financing instruments and assistance designed specifically to meet the adaptive needs 
of the Indian MSME sector as shown below:  
 
Role of the financial institutions in climate resilience financing for MSMEs is to provide timely financial support, 
customized financial products, and services, holding workshops on climate resilience for MSMEs, etc. 
 
Financial institutions felt that they will be comfortable to provide climate financing for the MSME sector if they get low-
cost funds/ cheaper sources of finance, non-sovereign loans coupled with technical assistance so that lenders can 
develop climate financing products, forbearance in NPA classification of climate finance loans, blended finance, longer 
tenure loans, default guarantee system, knowledge of reliable/proven technology, etc.  
 
To enhance climate resilience, the financial community should offer solutions which are demanded by the market, 
especially in the area of bringing out low cost MSME focused financial products 
 
FIs also suggested that all types of climate financing be included in priority sector and inclusion of climate finance in 
CSR activities 
 
In future, if financial institutions are mandated to disclose their emissions, including Scope 3 emissions, this could 
result in some difficulties in obtaining finance, which could be overcome with better preparedness and efforts on the 
part of MSMEs in curbing emissions 
 
Most stakeholders advocated for more training, building greater awareness for MSMEs on how they can see the bigger 
picture better, and contribute to arrest adverse climate change - very few MSMEs were found to know what the impact 
of their actions on climate is and they need to be made aware of how to manufacture their products more responsibly 
and without causing harm to environment irrespective of the sectors they are active in

=== PAGE 35 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 31 
 
 
 
MSMEs would require handholding and support through sector-wise intervention so that the specific issues relating to 
climate change can be identified and dealt with carefully
