Programme Description:  
65. SIDBI through the programme ‘Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Project in MSMEs’ aims to develop and demonstrate 
an ecosystem to promote Climate Financing to MSMEs in mitigation and adaptation interventions. To achieve this GCF 
fund is sought in the form of GCF grant (USD 15.6 million) for technical assistance and GCF loan (USD 200 million) 
which will be blended with co-finance from SIDBI of USD 800 million to finance the MSMEs for their green interventions. 
Out of this blended facility, SIDBI will directly lend USD 200 million to the MSMEs whilst USD 800 million will be given to 
various PFIs as on-lending/refinance. To achieve results at a scale, private sector investment worth USD 2400 million 
will be potentially leverage from the market and the total indicative cost of the entire project is USD 3792.6 million.  
66. The proposed activities in the programme emanates from SIDBI’s more than a decade experience of ecosystem 
strengthening aligned to climate activities for MSMEs. Based on our experience, the following elements will be considered 
as part of the programme:  
a. Handholding: Besides financing, MSMEs require technical handholding support in implementing climate 
interventions specific to adaptation and mitigation in their units.  
b. Market Creation: There needs to be a market push for the MSMEs to adopt climate technologies. Since, MSMEs 
in India work on the neighbourhood theory, financing ~10000 loans, at a concessional rate, as a part of this 
programme, would help in demonstrating the climate technologies that MSMEs can adopt within their clusters.    
c. Concessional Finance: Climate financing to MSMEs is a relatively new area, constrained with lack of awareness 
among MSMEs and FIs about the climate interventions required in MSMEs. To boost market demand, 
concessional financing based on innovative financing model will be very helpful. One of the examples of a 
blended facility model that has been very successful is that of the 4E scheme- The 4E scheme was set up with 
USD 5 million corpus (28% financed by The World Bank and 72% co-financed by SIDBI) with blended rates to 
provide concessional funding to MSMEs. The proceeds from this was further used as revolving fund to further 
finance the MSMEs for their climate interventions.  This model was very successful and at present in FY 2024, 
the total disbursement stands at USD 987.9 Million.  Based on numerous stakeholder conversations with several 
PFIs, it is felt that availability of lines of credit at concessional rates is likely to incentivize financial institutions to 
provide loans to MSMEs for adopting climate resilient measures. Adaptation measures suffer heavily from 
information gaps with unquantified risks leading to a higher risk perception for financiers. Further, financial return 
from adaptation projects is often considered to be low/ non-existent leading to their lack of bankability at the 
market rates. Thus, concessional financing can alleviate concerns about commercial viability of sector in given 
market 
d. Ease of Access: Simplified end to end process would boost demand from MSMEs to demand for climate 
financing. For example, in the 4E schemes and in other running schemes of SIDBI, digitisation and seamless 
loan application process has proven to be very helpful in the expansion of the programme on the whole.  
e. Mainstreaming climate finance in Indian Banking: There is a need to mainstream climate finance within the 
Indian Banking system, more focused on MSMEs. Currently, financing green technologies to MSMEs, especially 
those located in climate vulnerable regions is perceived as a riskier venture by the banks and hence there is a 
need to understand the climate space so as to enhance their lending portfolios focused towards climate financing 
to MSMEs.  
f. 
Capacity Building: It was seen that training and capacity building of MSMEs, developing e-modules of the 
training and disseminating these through banking and industrial associations could help manifold in raising 
awareness among not just MSMEs but also the financers. Additionally, stakeholders advocated for more training, 
building greater awareness for MSMEs on how they can see the bigger picture better, and contribute to arrest 
adverse climate change - very few MSMEs were found to know what the impact of their actions on climate is and 
they need to be made aware of how to manufacture their products more responsibly and without causing harm 
to environment irrespective of the sectors they are active in. Utilizing the lessons learnt by SIDBI in running pilot 
scale interventions in the area of energy efficiency as well as a blended finance facility, the proposed programme 
will set up a blended finance facility aimed at encouraging the Lending Institutions to take up financing of 
reduction of carbon emissions and enhancing the resilience to the impacts of climate change, in line with the 
requirements of MSMEs. The programme proposes to cover manufacturing as well as solution / service provider 
MSMEs addressing adoption of clean technologies towards emission reduction and providing solutions to 
enhance climate resilience of communities. The programme will build capacities of the select Lending Institutions 
for climate financing in MSMEs as also to facilitate networking of technology providers and facilitate technology 
customization as per the local needs and priorities of MSMEs.  
67. A substantial component of the programme is focused on leveraging the strength of PFIs to finance the MSMEs for 
their low carbon and climate resilient intervention.  The Accredited Entity, acting as the Executing Entity, will

45 https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/561461577729300064/pdf/India-Financing-Energy-Efficiency-at-MSMEs-Project.pdf 
refinance/on-lend the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-financing, to the PFIs.  The Programme will offer 
refinance against eligible assets created by the empanelled PFIs and originated no longer than six months prior to 
approval from the Accredited Entity for co-financing in accordance with the FAA. SIDBI has been working with banks 
and NBFCs under its institutional finance mandate. SIDBI’s continued partnership with Indian Banking System through 
its institutional finance vertical is highlighted below:  
Refinance to banks: 
SIDBI expands resources of other banks/PFIs through refinancing activities thereby boosting the flow of credit to 
MSMEs. As of March 31, 2023, the outstanding portfolio was USD 35,924 million/INR 2,98,173 crores, the year on year 
growth was a significant 79%. The portfolio is in respect of 49 banks which comprises of 12 Public Sector Banks 
(PSBs), 17 Private Sector Banks (PvSBs), 6 Foreign Banks (FBs), 10 Small Finance Banks (SFBs), 3 Regional Rural 
Banks (RRBs), and 1 Urban Cooperative Bank (UCB).  
            Refinance to Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs):  
At the end of the fiscal year 2023, the Bank’s NBFC portfolio stood at USD 4266.8 million/ INR 33,415 crores across 56 
NBFCs, an increase of 86% in outstanding. During FY 2023, sanctions and disbursements showed a growth by 67% 
and 81%, respectively over FY 2022.  
Refinance to Micro Finance Institutions (MFIs):  
SIDBI extended financial support to MFIs in the form of equity/ quasi-equity, refinance, term loans, grant support, etc., 
enabling smooth flow of credit to MFIs/ Informal Enterprises. The net outstanding portfolio stood at USD 590.36 million/ 
INR 4,900 crore as in FY 2023 as against USD 375.6 million/ INR 3,118 crores during FY 2022, registering growth of 
57% over previous FY. The disbursements during FY 2023 recorded a growth of 32% over the previous FY.  
NBFCs emerging interest towards green/climate financing: 
As per a recent stakeholder consultation conducted for NBFCs, the following are the highlights:  
o 
17 NBFCs participated in the consultation 
o 
92% of the NBFC leaders in the workshop agreed that Green Finance represents a significant opportunity for 
their business. The priority areas overwhelmingly emerged as (a). Electric Mobility (3W and 2W) (b). Solar Rooftops 
(Institutional solar and RESCOs) 
• 
Indian FI Interest in climate financing: 
There is increased interest among PFIs and NBFCs regarding climate financing. Hence, there is a need to sensitise the 
PFIs to increase their lending portfolio towards climate financing of MSMEs. Concessional loans to PFIs will further act 
as a booster to effect this objective. The programme will also build synergy and leverage from the experiences of previous 
projects undertaken on pilot demonstration scale in the country. The programme as catalyst would help scaling up of 
some of the earlier initiatives, taken by Govt of India and SIDBI towards creating momentum in climate financing 
landscape for MSMEs. Through this project, SIDBI will leverage its learning from serving MSMEs through various 
initiatives including successful implementation of GEF funded World Bank project “Financing Energy Efficiency at MSMEs 
in India”45, “Partial Risk Sharing Facility (PRSF) for Energy Efficiency Project”, and other dedicated line of credits from 
Japan International Co-operation Agency (JICA), Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), Agence Française de 
Development (AFD), the World Bank (WB), etc.  
 
68. A joint approach of Mitigation and Adaptation is more effective than the focus only on energy efficiency. The success of 
climate/climate financing project, particularly for MSMEs require both technical handholding support and financial support 
by way of concessional loans. Further, in order to realize low hanging fruits, incentivizing the units at initial stage to create 
climate MSME ambassadors and pool of champion units helps in early investment decision by majority of MSMEs as 
they follow the “Neighbourhood Theory”.  
 
69. ‘Neighbourhood 
theory’ 
refers 
to 
the 
phenomena 
wherein 
new 
technologies 
or 
interventions 
are 
adopted/incorporated/mainstreamed within the larger industry landscape based on showcasing of individual success 
stories. This holds particularly true for the Indian MSME sectors where most constituents are reluctant first movers. In 
this regard, incentivisation of individual units to adopt newer technology/interventions primarily through financial 
mechanism followed by a real world on ground showcase of the technology/intervention’s benefits is seen to catalyse 
the unit’s ‘neighbours’ into adopting the same technology/intervention.

=== PAGE 40 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 36 
 
 
70. Thus, 
utilising 
this 
phenomena 
SIDBI 
intends 
to 
catalyse 
large 
scale 
adoption 
of 
climate 
related 
technologies/interventions within the MSME sector by first focusing on specific set of clusters/industries and technologies  
 
71. While concessional loans as an instrument for climate financing is commonly used, this project has identified several 
other avenues to tackle the risks associated with financing climate focussed projects. It has conducted deep sector level 
analysis, identified targeted technological interventions, and devised numerous financial tools to maximize the impact of 
this financing. 
 
72. The integration of the learnings of SIDBIs previous programme has been at the centre of the design of this proposal. For 
instance, in Annexure 22 regarding the impact calculation we have clearly indicated how the previous lending portfolio 
of SIDBI has helped us to extrapolate cost allocation to each sector, energy savings in each sector and the GHG impact 
of the project on the whole.

Component 1 — Term loans to support climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs. 
 
73. Under this component, ~10000 number of loans will be provided to MSMEs as detailed below: 
a. Executing Entity (SIDBI) will lend around 20% of the loans directly and another 80% of the loans will be financed 
indirectly through PFIs.  SIDBI will provide technical handholding support to the selected Participating Financial 
Institutions (PFIs) i.e. Non-Banking Finance Companies (NBFCs), Small Finance Banks (SFBs), Micro Finance 
Institutions (MFIs) etc. to design and implement climate lending products within their asset portfolio, in line with 
the climate financial instruments of SIDBI. SIDBI will select the PFIs based on their asset portfolio and 
experience.  
b. The MSMEs manufacturing units and service providers on either supply or demand side seeking to adopt low 
carbon measures and promote climate resilient technologies/ products/ services shall be eligible under the 
project. MSMEs executing projects / providing services, related to the activities eligible under the Scheme, to

various government bodies / private entities on BOT, BOOT, BOOM, PPP, etc. models backed by suitable 
contract shall be eligible for finance for their CAPEX and or OPEX requirements. The unit should be in compliance 
with the Environment & Social Management Framework of SIDBI being utilised currently for climate projects 
MSMEs will own, operate, and manage the assets financed under the programme.  
c. The activities under this component will be carried out by engaging technical support staff/experts. 
 
Programme Outputs and Activities: 
Output 1.1: MSME mitigation technologies financed and implemented. Increased access to mitigation finance for 
MSMEs. 
  
Activity 1.1.1: Establish a loan instrument to provide concessional loans for MSME mitigation needs. 
74. Debt instrument tailored to mitigation needs for improved access to finance in selected MSMEs. The key intervention of 
technologies to be financed under FMAP are (a). renewable Energy- PV systems Mostly Captive projects, up to capacity 
of 10 Megawatt and wind turbine up to 5 Megawatt of capacity (b). Electric vehicles and charging infrastructure, all type 
of vehicles 2w,3w and 4w vehicles will be covered under this programme (c). Energy efficiency- Industrial processes and 
appliances, insulation, high performance glass, LED lighting, High-Efficiency Heat Pumps, solar water heater etc. SIDBI 
has established standard operating guidelines for selection of MSMEs involved in the Climate /sustainable projects.  The 
same SOPs would be followed while assessing an intended borrower, i.e., MSMEs for providing debt financing. A detailed 
list of relevant mitigation measures and technologies that will be eligible for finance is attached as Annexure.  
75. The programme has been designed to offer concessional loans to PFIs (NBFCs/SFBs/MFIs) and driving them to further 
leverage 3x the lending amount into climate technologies in order to ensure that results are achieved at a scale. Through 
this, the programme intends to encourage the PFIs to create an ecosystem of climate financing which would allow them 
to lend to the MSMEs with an increased understanding of the associated risks and underwriting capabilities required. 
SIDBIs direct lending to the MSMEs sets an example for the PFIs to follow to successfully lend to the MSMEs for adopting 
low emission climate resilient technologies.  
Other TA elements will include:  
• 
Carry out detailed technical assessment of potential MSME units. 
• 
Carry out credit risk rating evaluation of potential MSME units. 
• 
Selection of MSMEs according to Eligibility Criteria as well as technical and risk assessment results. 
• 
Incentivize the units to create climate MSME ambassadors and a group of champion units. 
• 
Online loan application and speedy appraisal process setup. 
• 
End to end technical and handholding support to MSMEs, PFIs, NBFCs, and other stakeholders. 
• 
Facilitate networking of technology providers. 
 
76. Activity 1.1.1 and 1.2.1 will jointly address the barriers related to: i) lack of private investment at the MSMEs level due to 
immature capital market and high upfront capital costs for climate products; and ii) limited understanding of MSME sector 
by institutional financial players. This will be achieved by (i). Offering concessional finance to MSMEs directly 
encouraging them to adopt relevant climate resilient technologies and interventions (ii). Offering concessional lines of 
capital and blended financial products to FIs which will help in reducing their risk of lending towards MSMEs thus 
encouraging them to offer additional financing  (ii). Handholding by SIDBI helping FIs understand the relevant challenges 
and risks of financing MSMEs and developing the requisite underwriting skills to finance climate technologies within the 
MSME space. Additionally, Activity 1.1.1 will address barriers related to limited climate financing provided by banks and 
the need for training bank officials, enhancing climate risk assessment processes, and integrating monitoring, ESG, and 
gender frameworks. This will be achieved by SIDBI directly lending to MSMEs to demonstrate climate financing, aiming 
to encourage other financial institutions to also engage in climate financing for MSMEs. 
Output 1.2: MSME adaptation technologies financed and implemented. Increased access to adaptation finance for 
MSMEs. 
Activity 1.2.1: Establish a loan instrument to provide concessional loans for MSME adaptation needs. 
77. A study by Climate Policy Initiative highlights the following barriers to adaptation financing in India (a).  Limited knowledge 
of investment opportunities (b). Lack of capacity to develop commercially feasible projects (c) Unavailability of bankable 
projects and business models46. Furthermore, the adaptation interventions are local in nature and within India it has been

=== PAGE 42 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 38 
 
 
observed that a large number of providers of such adaptive technologies/interventions are MSMEs. Hence, the objective 
of this activity is to create a debt instrument tailored for improved access to finance in select MSMEs that offer adaptive 
technologies/interventions for sectors such as agriculture, water resources, agroforestry, disasters and services. This 
programme will further focus on regions in India with high climate vulnerability: coastal, eastern belt and Himalayan 
regions and the focus sectors would be agriculture, water resources, agroforestry, disasters and services. An indicative 
list of relevant adaptation measures and technologies that will be eligible for loan finance is presented in Annexure 22.  
78. The adaptation intervention under this programme attempts to tackle both the demand and supply side concerns. On the 
demand side, the MSMEs located in vulnerable regions are to be targeted where financing for climate resilient 
technologies is considered risky. The concessional loans here would prove to be useful to these vulnerable MSMEs to 
increase their resilience to climate hazards which would have long term direct impact on their operations. At present 
these MSMEs have very limited knowledge of technological solutions to make them climate resilient.  
79. On the supply side, the programme targets financing those units that are value chain members and suppliers to larger 
units manufacturing technologies related to adaptation technology. Herein, focus will also be on financing MSMEs 
working on innovative solutions that manufacture or service locally suited, decentralised adaptation technologies.  
80. Adaptation projects are considered to have limited bankability due to limited financial returns, lack of linkages with socio-
economic returns, etc. In order to address these challenges this programme has been designed to offer concessional 
debt financing to bankable MSMEs who provide relevant adaptive technologies and services to wider adaptation projects.  
81. In addition to lending through concessional debt, SIDBI intends to bring its expertise of different innovative financial 
instruments for adaptation needs.

owing to the increased number of climate induced hazards and alternative measures/technologies will be considered if 
they are deemed fit going forward.

84. In addition to offering concessional finances, a range of TA activities to determine the feasibility of the adaptive 
measures/product and the associated MSMEs will be carried out. As was the case within mitigation, these activities will 
comprise of assessing the health of MSMEs viability of product in question and assisting the wider FI network in financing 
these interventions.   
 
Component 2 — Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs 
  
Output 2.1:  Capacity built and knowledge management improved to support climate change adaptation and mitigation 
in MSMEs and PFIs. Capacities of selected MSMEs and PFIs are enhanced to rapidly scale their business and climate 
impact 
 
85. The programme will extend technical support to MSMEs and PFIs to address knowledge gaps on low carbon and climate 
resilient technologies. MSMEs will also be facilitated to develop bankable business proposals and access finance through 
the Fund of the project.  
86. Capacity building and strengthening of other financial institutions is a continuous process adopted by SIDBI under various 
programmes such as PRSF wherein 15 banks/FIs/NBFCs are empanelled with SIDBI to support financing through 
performance contracting routes. While SIDBI conducts regular knowledge/capacity building programmes for other 
banks/FIs to support them in enhancing their portfolios and to strengthen their capacities to appraise loans for MSMEs, 
focused climate specific capacity building for MSMEs is the need of the hour. Hence, large scale capacity building 
programmes supporting the wider FI ecosystem in India to understand MSME climate financing landscape will be a 
critical part of the TA activities in this programme. Successful implementation of these activities is expected to play a key 
role towards the success of the larger programmes.  
87. The best practices and learning of the programmes will be shared with larger audiences including other MSMEs through 
e-modules on a digital platform. The TA component will support in development of a repository of knowledge and learning 
established under the proposed program in digital form which will be maintained by SIDBI beyond project period and will 
be accessible by all stakeholders. 
 
88. The proposed programme has been designed incorporating and building on learnings acquired by SIDBI over time (as 
showcased in preceding sections). Further, the program will build on the existing knowledge landscape developed by 
SIDBI under its ongoing schemes. 
 
89. The necessity of these activities has been determined through multiple stakeholder conversations with players across 
the climate financing ecosystems including MSMEs, PFIs, local communities etc. Basis their inputs, we have considered 
numerous TA related activities that have been included in our proposal which are designed to address climate concerns 
in the long term. The activities under this component will be carried out by engaging technical support staff/experts. 
 
Activity 2.1.1:  Undertake baseline studies for 500 MSMEs 
90. MSMEs have limited knowledge and understanding of the low carbon and climate resilient technologies and need to be 
persuaded on the potential benefits of adoption of such technologies in terms of energy saving, GHG emission reduction, 
enhanced operational efficiency, long-term monitory benefits, etc. These technologies need assessments will be 
undertaken to: 
• 
Identify the baseline parameters and current consumption of energy, water, etc. 
• 
Identify the applicable low carbon and climate-resilient technologies. 
• 
Demonstrate the savings possibilities in terms of specific energy consumption, GHG emission reductions, cost 
savings, and operational efficiency, etc. 
• 
Encourage MSMEs to adopt/manufacture these low carbon and climate-resilient technologies 
• 
Create an ecosystem for climate related services as part of their routine activity. 
  
91. The purpose of the need assessment exercise has been conceived to address the uncertainties where there is no 
awareness of the baseline and expected environmental impact of technologies to be adopted.  The need assessment 
exercise would enable an MSME to ascertain which climate technology best fits their circumstances.

=== PAGE 45 ===
B

47 Cover Single Pages.cdr (sidbi.in)  
48 Govt. of India. 2010. Enabling Manufacturing Sector to be Competitive through Quality Management Standards and Quality Technology

92. To reach out to MSMEs working innovatively in the space of mitigation and adaptation and having potential for large scale 
impact, one of the interventions under this activity will be to hold a challenge fund to select such projects through a 
competitive process. This would also help in building the pipeline of such innovative projects under FMAP. 
93. The technology assessment studies include feasibility and technical studies of specific technologies identified for adoption 
in a particular MSME, energy audits of MSME units, lifecycle analysis of products or services that help the MSMEs to 
assess their current resource and emission footprints, identify the potential low carbon and climate-resilient technologies 
and their benefits. These baseline studies will help in building awareness on the climate technologies and create niche 
market demand for its adoption among the MSMEs.  
• 
These assessments will be built on SIDBI’s previous work on sectoral technology gap mapping of MSME 
clusters47  
• 
The technology need assessment is imperative for those MSMEs in regions of high climate vulnerability prone 
to climate hazards such as droughts, cyclones, floods etc so as to equip them with the knowledge of those 
technologies that can make their respective units resilient. The specific technologies financed under this 
programme will help the MSMEs to equip themselves against the climate hazards that they are most vulnerable 
to.  
94. Activity 2.1.1 will address barriers related to: i) lack of awareness of relevant climate resilient technologies within MSMEs 
and ii) climate related data scarcity in MSMEs.  
Activity 2.1.2: Undertake awareness raising and trainings on climate change adaptation and mitigation for MSMEs and 
Financial Institutions. 
95. This activity will address the skills and capacity gaps in MSMEs, financial institutions in adopting climate technologies, 
climate risk management and climate investments. Awareness raising and training activities will be informed by the results 
of the baseline studies undertaken through Activity 2.1.1 and will be carried out to create awareness and disseminate the 
information about low carbon and climate-resilient technologies, as well as available financing amongst MSMEs. SIDBI 
will design and operate cluster-specific climate technology pilot demonstration interventions to promote demand for 
adoption of climate technologies (low carbon and climate resilient) invoking the neighbourhood theory. SIDBI through its 
regional and branch offices and associated agencies spread across India will also organise promotional activities on 
climate technologies and their co-benefits periodically to generate demand for adoption of climate technologies. These 
activities will target a range of stakeholders including MSMEs, Industry Associations, Banks / FIs, Equipment 
Manufacturers / Suppliers / Vendors, etc. These trainings will be imparted on various topics, including: 
 
MSMEs / Industry associations: Low carbon, water efficient technologies, climate resilient technologies and their life 
cycle benefits. MSME participants will be sensitized through 16 programmes on various technological options. 
 
Banks/FIs:  Banking / MFIs / NBFCs organisations will be trained on Technical and Financial Appraisals of such proposals 
(Low carbon, water efficient technologies, climate resilient), risk mitigation measures, etc. through 8 training programmes. 
 
Equipment Manufacturers / Suppliers / Vendors: Empanelment of at least 200 Manufacturers / Suppliers / Vendors for 
their different Low carbon and water efficient technologies and connecting them with MSMEs/IAs through a minimum of 8 
technology exhibitions. Quality of equipment provided through the project’s activities will be ensured through  appropriate 
QA/QC criteria as per national quality management and technology standards for the MSME sector48 
 
96. In Indian context, the technology or machine supplier provides guarantee/ warranty of equipment along with annual 
maintenance services to MSMEs. The issues related with QA/QC are usually taken care of by MSME. Machines will be 
financed under the proposed project where QA//QC will be ensured at the supplier level. SIDBI and the empanelled PFIs 
will also ensure that the warranty coverage of the equipment is checked at the level of due diligence conducted by 
MSMEs/PFIs. Further, the technology due diligence exercise shall also ensure that only those OEMs with extensive 
experience in after sales services shall be empanelled to provide after sale services for these technologies. The lenders 
are encouraged to check whether the technology to be funded confers to the stands of the Bureau of Indian Standards 
(BIS). The ISI mark is a standards-compliance mark for industrial products in India since 1950. The mark certifies that a

=== PAGE 46 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 42 
 
 
 
49 https://prsf.sidbi.in/user/pages/viewpage/unnatee 
product conforms to an Indian standard (IS) developed by the Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS), the national standards 
body of India. An equivalent international standard would be followed for the imported machines as well.   
 
97. Activity 2.1.2 will address barriers related to: i) lack of awareness of climate technologies and potential sources of climate 
finance; ii) lack of a strong MSME climate technology OEM ecosystem; and iii) lack in understanding of on ground 
practical benefits of climate resilient technologies. As with other TA activities, Activity 2.1.2 will address the need for 
training bank officials, enhancing climate risk assessment processes, and integrating monitoring, ESG, and gender 
frameworks.  
Activity 2.1.3: Implement knowledge management and sharing on climate change adaptation and mitigation for MSMEs 
 
98. 
The learning of the program will be disseminated amongst stakeholders through knowledge products to enable large 
scale capacity building within MSME sector. SIDBI will document the success stories, best practices and the learning 
of adoption of climate change mitigation and adaptation technologies within the MSMEs through case studies, 
documentaries, assessment studies. These multiple knowledge products including case studies, AV documentaries, 
brochures, flyers, etc. will be published and disseminated for wider circulation among MSMEs, Financial institutions, 
policy makers, and other relevant stakeholders. The learning of the programme will also be disseminated through 
workshops, trainings, information campaigns that further contribute to the uptake of low carbon technologies supported 
by the programme. The knowledge products including the reports of baseline studies developed under the  programme  
will be made available for MSME stakeholders. These Knowledge Products will also be made readily available online 
in public domain to ensure wider dissemination.  
  
Output 2.2: Enabling environment for a low-emission and climate-resilient MSME sector. 
 
99. The ecosystem for innovative and customized products and services for building adaptive resilience of communities and 
vulnerable sectors lacks adequate policy support. The programme aims to strengthen MSMEs understanding of the 
climate ecosystem and shall enable large scale adoption of relevant climate technologies through innovative business 
models, products and services.  
One of the key enabling factors for increased adoption of climate technologies is the availability of a clear taxonomy that 
defines ‘what is’ versus ‘what is not’ a relevant climate mitigating/adapting technology.  
In this regard SIDBI’s experience in financing MSMEs specially for mitigation has resulted in development of robust list 
of 900+ technologies which is attached as annexure in the Operations Manual. Similarly, through this programme, one 
of the objectives is to develop a similar list of adaptation technologies and disseminate the same for wider adoption.   
 
Having a robust database of mitigation and adaptation technologies based on the need assessment of specific MSMEs 
will also enable the PFIs to take an informed decision with regards to financing the MSMEs for adopting low carbon and 
climate resilient interventions 
  
Activity 2.2.1:  Develop and implement MSME sectoral adaptation and mitigation benchmarking tools. 
 
100. The programme will contribute to the upgradation and further development of SIDBI’s existing online MSME 
benchmarking tool (earlier developed by SIDBI under one of the GEF project 49 covering database of almost all sectors 
and 800+ project reports). The tool provides a comparison of existing climate mitigation practices and savings potential 
in a particular type of industry. In this regard, through this exercise the tool shall incorporate climate adaptation practices 
as well going forward. Ultimately, the tool will help the MSME industry make informed decisions on adoption of 
appropriate low carbon and climate-resilient climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies. 
101. The Financial Institutions will also be sensitised to use this benchmarking tool to bridge the gap in their understanding 
of applicable climate technologies within the MSME sector. Currently, lack of understanding among the PFIs about the 
mitigation and adaptation technologies applicable for MSMEs is also a deterrent for the funding of MSMEs to uptake 
these technologies by the PFIs.

=== PAGE 47 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 43 
 
 
Activity 2.2.2: Digital Green Credit Rating Tool (DGCRT) includes integration of Green parameters (Energy and 
Environment) with Credit rating tool 
 
102. This activity will support the digitization of an integrated credit rating tool (to be used across national, sub-national and 
local levels) which includes integration of climate parameters (Energy and Environment) into credit rating tool. This tool 
will provide a fair assessment to a FI of a loan applicant’s potential to invest in low carbon and climate-resilient 
technologies. In doing so, the tool will act as a risk mitigation measure for FIs — the higher the rating, the lower the risk. 
Basis our stakeholder conversations, it is clear that PFIs lack the ability to accurately determine the risk associated with 
climate initiatives within the MSME sector. Thus, such an integrated credit tool will reduce information asymmetry for 
PFIs allowing them to make more informed investment decisions.

Implementation Arrangements 
(a) FAA 
The GCF and the Accredited Entity will enter into a single FAA (Loan and Grant), which will reflect the terms and conditions 
described in the Term Sheet and the AMA.  
(b) Executing Entity 
The Accredited Entity will act as an executing entity for all Activities of the Programme.  
(c) Flow of Funds  
Under the FAA, the GCF will make available to the Accredited Entity the GCF Proceeds, in the form of loan and grant, for the 
implementation of the Programme.  The GCF will disburse the GCF Proceeds into the GCF Account pursuant to the Accredited 
Entity’s Requests for Disbursement, subject to fulfillment of applicable conditions precedent to the Disbursements. 
The GCF Reimbursable Funds shall be provided by the GCF to the Accredited Entity pursuant to the key terms and conditions 
set out in the Term Sheet.  
The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds shall be provided by the GCF to the Accredited Entity pursuant to the key terms and 
conditions set out in the Term Sheet.   
(d) GCF Reimbursable Funds (GCF Loan) 
The Accredited Entity, acting as Executing Entity, will lend the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-financing, to the 
MSMEs via two modalities: (i) loans to MSMEs and (ii) refinance/on-lending to PFIs for subsequent on-lending to the MSMEs.  
 
(i) 
Direct Loans 
The Direct Loans (i.e. loans from SIDBI to MSMEs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in  
the Term Sheet.  
 
(ii) 
Indirect Loans and Sub-Loans

=== PAGE 48 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 44 
 
 
The Indirect Loans (i.e. loans from SIDBI to PFIs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in  the 
Term Sheet.   
The Sub-Loans (i.e. loans from PFIs to MSMEs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in  the 
Term Sheet.  
The Accredited Entity, acting as the Executing Entity, will refinance/on-lend the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-
financing, to the PFIs.  The Programme will offer refinance against eligible assets created by the empaneled PFIs and originated 
no longer than six months prior to approval from the Accredited Entity for financing in accordance with FAA. In addition, on-
lending to PFIs for creating eligible assets within stipulated time from the date of disbursement shall also be permitted on a case-
by-case basis, given compliance with Eligibility Criteria and Investment Criteria as outlined in the Term Sheet.   
It is proposed to follow a three-step approach. First step is to identify and empanel those eligible PFIs which have interest in 
climate financing and are willing to adopt new systems processes. One of the initial conditions for empanelment would be that 
the PFI should agree to leverage 3X of SIDBI’s financing amount.  
Second step is to provide sensitization and handholding support to these empanelled PFIs as also provide sanction of loan 
assistance at concessional terms along with the term sheet which will specify the terms as per the FMAP programme following 
the requirements set forth in the Operations Manual including but not limited to Eligibility Criteria and Investment Criteria as set 
out in the Operations Manual, Annexure 6 ESMS due-diligence and Annexure 22 climate impact.   The term sheet of the Indirect 
Loan Agreements shall also specify that PFIs would need to create 3X (during the first two years of the programme leverage of 
3X may not be insisted. However, leverage of 3X will be ensured on a programme basis) of the portfolio of new MSME loans 
meeting all the conditions of the term sheet under the programme. PFIs would be required to accept the sanction terms and enter 
into loan documentation with SIDBI.  
The third stage would be disbursement wherein PFI would be required to submit (for Refinance) all the details, copy of 
documentation, etc. to satisfy SIDBI that the conditions stipulated in the term sheet at the time of sanction have been fully 
complied with. The disbursement under on-lending could be considered, upfront, based on a declaration/ commitment by the PFI 
borrower for generating eligible portfolio/ Qualifying Assets within an agreed timeline (maximum upto 120 days from the date of 
respective disbursement) and conforming to the end use as per SIDBI’s stipulation. 
For avoidance of doubt, the GCF is taking the credit risk for its portion of the loan, with full recourse to SIDBI.  SIDBI will take on 
the FX risk arising out of GCF loan and the credit risk of sub-projects under the programme. 
SIDBI will be managing the currency risks through appropriate hedging mechanisms and may charge risk premium to the end 
borrowers 
(e) GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds (GCF Grant) 
The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds (GCF Grant) will be used by the Accredited Entity, acting as the Executing Entity, in order 
to conduct baseline/feasibility studies, capacity building and awareness, knowledge management, contribute to policy 
initiatives, M&V, impact evaluation. SIDBI will engage services of established firms for respective activities via service contract. 
The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds shall also cover the expenses of the PMU, which scope includes capacity building and 
monitoring of PFIs, along with the Technical Assistance activities.  
There will be no on-granting of the GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds by the Accredited Entity. 
(f) Eligibility Criteria 
 
The eligibility criteria of all the technologies (mitigation and adaptation) financed under the programme is outlined in the 
Operations Manual.

Operations Manual.  
For Indirect Loans, the Accredited Entity, acting in its capacity as the Executing Entity, will enter into legally binding loan 
agreements with the PFIs, which will pass down the relevant GCF requirements and obligations to the PFIs, including policy

(i) Programme Management Unit 
 
Given the Pan- India expanse of the programme, it is necessary to have a centralised governance and monitoring for smooth 
implementation of the Programme. For the project’s administration and implementation, SIDBI (Executing Entity) has identified a 
multi-vertical core team which will serve as the Programme Management Unit (PMU).   The PMU, headed by the Project Director 
consists of total 15 experts and shall provide sensitisation / capacity building as well as technical hand holding support to EE and 
the PFIs regarding assessment / compliance of climate related aspects, technical eligibility norms, environment, social due 
diligence as per the management framework, monitoring, evaluation and reporting of the climate aspects and environment, social 
parameters of the sub loans. The PMU shall not be involved in origination, credit due diligence, credit appraisal, KYC, credit 
decision of sub- loans, disbursement of sub- loans, loan documentation, follow-up, monitoring, recovery, accounting, etc. The EE 
and PFIs shall utilise their own staff for all these activities. For better clarification, it may be mentioned that the PMU staff shall 
only be supporting/ providing technical hand holding for additional due diligence/compliances required for climate financing which 
are over and above the normal activities which are required to be undertaken for providing normal MSME loans. The PMU 
resources shall have adequate technical as well as fiduciary and safeguards experience. The E&S aspects will be coordinated 
and managed by an expert who shall be the part of the PMU. He/she shall be supported by an E&S specialist (ESS) dedicated 
to the safeguards function.  
The PMU shall be responsible for the execution of day-to-day activities of the programme and report to the AE about the progress 
of the FMAP. The primary responsibility of the PMU includes providing assistance to the Direct Credit Vertical (for direct lending) 
and NBFC vertical (for indirect lending) for the execution of the component 1. The PMU shall be responsible for providing technical 
assistance to the PFIs in line with the GCF guidelines and the FAA. One of the core responsibilities of the PMU includes the 
capacity building of the PFIs & MSMEs and sensitising the sector about low-emission and climate-resilient technologies. The 
team shall engage in TA activities including capacity building and knowledge management etc as well as overall co-ordination 
for the component 1 (including due diligence aspects) in the initial preparatory and loan disbursement phase. In the Second 
phase i.e. the Loan repayment phase, the team shall be responsible for the monitoring and reporting of climate, environment and 
social aspects as per the M&E log frame, technical hand holding and other necessary engagements with the stakeholder. The 
PMU shall regularly report to the AE (as described in FAA) on the progress of the FMAP programme and also of the Grievances 
or issues related to ESS or gender (if any).

Loan Approval Process- 
 
103. The Loan Approval Process is a comprehensive three-stage procedure designed to rigorously evaluate loan applications 
and ensure adherence to the stringent criteria. The first stage, Application and Initial Screening, is conducted at the branch 
level by the Relationship Manager. During this phase, applications are meticulously screened for completeness, including 
the submission of mandatory documentation such as climate-related, environmental, and social safeguards documents. 
Incomplete applications are promptly rejected/ hand holded to be completed, as the case may be, while only those deemed 
complete undergo initial screening against the exclusion list criteria. Applications that meet the criteria are forwarded to the 
second stage, Due Diligence, while those that fall under the exclusion list are rejected. The Due Diligence stage is executed 
in parallel at the branch level and involves several crucial assessments. The Processing Officer conducts evaluations related 
to Know Your Customer (KYC) requirements, financial viability, credit due diligence and checking of ESDD checklist. 
Concurrently, the Climate Expert, Technical Expert and ESS expert at the Project Management Unit (PMU) will perform 
climate-related, technical and ESS due diligence to ensure the soundness of the proposed project from a technical and 
climate perspective. In the third stage, technically sound and financially viable applications proceed to the Credit Appraisal 
Memorandum preparation phase. The Processing team comprising of Maker and Checker compiles a comprehensive Credit 
Appraisal Memorandum, which is then presented to the Loan Sanctioning Committee for final evaluation and decision-
making. To maintain transparency and impartiality, no member involved in the Initial Application Screening and Due 
Diligence processes is permitted to be part of the Loan Sanctioning Committee.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Explain why the project/programme requires GCF funding to address mitigation or adaptation measures, i.e. Why is the project/programme 
not currently being financed by public and/or private sector? Which market failure is being addressed with GCF funding? Are there any other 
domestic or international sources of financing?  
Explain why the proposed financial instruments were selected in light of the proposed activities and the overall financing package. i.e. What is 
the coherence between activities financed by grants and those financed by reimbursable funds? How were co-financing amounts and prices 
determined? How does the concessionality of the GCF financing compare to that of the co-financing? If applicable, provide a short market 
read on the prevailing of the pricing and/or financial markets for similar projects/programmes. 
Justify why the level of concessionality of the GCF financial instrument(s) is the minimum required to make the investment viable.

indebtedness of the recipient. Please reference relevant annexes, such as the feasibility study, economic analysis or financial analysis when 
appropriate. 
Several reports have highlighted the gaps in climate finance as highlighted below:  
(a). India stares at a "funding gap" of $10.1 trillion to achieve its net zero emissions commitment by 207050 
(b). Uneven distribution of climate finance source and need to leverage private investments - Of the $44 billion raised in climate 
financing in 2020, 40 per cent was funded directly or indirectly through public finance51 
(c). Over-reliance on domestic financing, with nearly 80 per cent of funds raised coming from domestic sources52 
As per the findings of the survey by RBI vide its notification dated July 27, 2022 and also as indicated in RBI’s Report on 
Currency and Finance 2022-23 issued on May 03, 2023, it is observed that the financing flows from the banking sector towards 
climate financing  are presently quite low and there is a need for training, sensitisation and capacity building of the banking 
officials in this regard as also putting in place proper systems and processes towards climate risk assessments and integrating 
suitable framework for M&E, ESG & gender, etc. The report highlights the following:  
(a) Only 24% of respondent banks have set their timelines to achieve carbon neutrality  
(b) Few banks have taken initiative to expedite climate lending 
(c) Only 8/34 respondent banks have separate verticals for Sustainable Finance  
(d). 1 in 3 respondent banks- no internal accountability for managing climate change & sustainability 
(e). Only 53% of respondent banks have an ESG strategy in place 
As highlighted by RBI in their survey, there is a need to bring in transformation in the Indian Banking System to boost climate 
financing by PFIs especially targeted at financing low emission, climate resilience interventions in MSMEs. This programme 
aims to bring in this transformation by building the capacities of PFIs to lend to MSMEs with increased confidence. 
Catalyzing climate financing in the MSME space 
107. Today, green lending towards MSMEs are restricted to a significantly small portion of the larger lending portfolios of 
financial institutions in India – limited to ~1% of the total book size  
108. Indian FIs are reluctant to finance MSMEs in the climate space primarily due to their:  
a. Lack of understanding of tools/mechanism to underwrite the risks associated with deployment of climate 
technologies in the MSME space

=== PAGE 52 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 48 
 
 
b. Inability to create scale – individual MSMEs present very small/disaggregated opportunities – for effective 
adoption of various climate technologies (e.g. RE or EE) 
Thus, highlighting a lack of alternative risk management/underwriting tools and innovative financing instruments that are 
more tailored towards the deployment of climate technologies within an MSME‘s operating environment 
109. Under these circumstances, the landscape has witnessed the rise of medium-sized and smaller NBFCs (including 
focused Green NBFCs) that are dedicatedly financing MSMEs in the climate space. As can be observed from the figure 
above, these NBFCs are playing a critical market making role rather than larger commercial banks or general purpose 
NBFCs 
a. The MSME focused climate lending carried out by these new age BBB rated green NBFCs are primarily 
geared towards electric mobility but with increasing lending towards RE deployment particularly solar rooftop 
as well.  
110. However, these NBFCs, particularly Green focused NBFCs are stuck in a vicious cycle, inhibiting their ability to scale, 
as showcased in the figure below: 
  
111. Consultations with Green NBFCs has revealed the need for an ecosystem enabling environment that offers support 
across the following areas:  
a. Co-lending partnerships with medium/small NBFCs to expand last mile reach 
b. On-lending partnerships with medium/small NBFCs against a defined use of proceeds (e.g., financing 
fleet aggregators) 
c. Partnering with global DFIs to effect the development of a dedicated co-lending infrastructure to aid 
discovery and execution of co-lending deals  
d. Devising a securitization policy to play a market making role for securitization of climate assets all activities 
which unlock financing from larger banks and NBFCs who now start extending debt lines of capital to these 
medium/small NBFCs on the back of the market created by them 
112. SIDBI has actively taken up the role of creating this ecosystem enabling environment to help scale medium-/small-
sized NBFCs with dedicated climate lending towards MSMEs. 
113. Through this programme, SIDBI intends to scale this catalytic effect for medium/small scaled NBFCs across the 
country, by providing

=== PAGE 53 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 49 
 
 
 
53 https://www.sidbi.in/head/uploads/greenpathwayseseries_documents/green-series-de-risking-lending.pdf  
a. requisite concessional lines of credit to finance climate interventions within MSMEs thus creating an MSME 
driven market of climate technologies  
b. further build capacity of these organizations to deploy climate technologies within MSMEs on the back of 
SIDBI’s extensive knowledge, expertise and existing risk management/underwriting tools that can be used to 
effectively finance the Indian MSME sector  
114. For MSMEs, deployment of climate technologies and interventions are primarily a question of their ability to drive long-
term investments with large capital requirements – which most MSMEs in India, even with an understanding of the 
significance and necessity of these technologies, are unable to deploy primarily due to a lack of:  
a. Access to concessional capital at rates lower than traditional market rates (which is usually offered for general 
purpose projects in MSMEs) which makes climate investments unviable – for example, consultations with 
stakeholders across the MSME ecosystem has revealed that in absence of concessional finance, 60% of the 
customers are unlikely to purchase new EE machines and shall continue to resort to purchasing older second-
hand machines which are less efficient but cheaper.   
b. Innovative financing models and business practices that circumvent the need for large CAPEX investments (as 
is the norm with infrastructure projects) or risk guarantees to FIs while providing more attractive norms for 
MSMEs in the form of longer tenors, lower TATs, easier processing cycles, etc.     
c. On a broader sectoral level, a clear understanding of the on-ground, real world benefits of any climate 
technology especially in terms of financial benefits (including a translation of cost savings into potential 
revenue/profits) which makes the use of the technology in question attractive thus activating the 
“Neighbourhood Effect” amongst the wider segments 
115. SIDBI through this programme intends to tackle all of these challenges by creating a range of services through the 
concessional loans it offers, including:  
a. Direct lending to select MSMEs to create showcases of the real-world benefits of deploying climate 
interventions within the MSME landscape and disseminating success stories across the landscape to activate 
the “Neighbourhood Effect” 
b. Leverage wider FI network to create a much larger ecosystem of concessional lending towards MSMEs 
adopting climate technologies by leveraging the catalytic financing offered through the programme as well as 
creating new financial products that are designed specifically to meet the needs of the MSME sector 
c. Enable deployment of innovative facilities such as RESCOs, on-bill financing through DISCOMs, etc. designed 
to create large scale demand amongst MSMEs for climate technologies 
Concessionality of the funding investments:  
• 
To encourage the industry and businesses to choose energy-efficient equipment, which in many cases, is more 
expensive compared to conventional technology. Hence, there is a need to reduce the cost differences by providing 
concessional lending for energy efficient equipment. Although energy-efficient equipment may become economical on 
lifecycle cost basis, equipment buyers are most likely to decide solely based on investment cost, resulting in choosing 
cheaper, conventional non- energy-efficient equipment. Against this background, the Project aims to provide a low 
interest loan to the buyers so that total payment amount for energy efficient equipment (equipment cost and financial 
cost) will break even with the cost of acquiring conventional equipment.  
• 
It is felt that there is a need of 1% to 3% concessionality in the interest rate in order to breakeven the additional cost of 
interest of EE and RE/PV equipment with that of cheaper traditional equipment as well as the additional compliance costs 
pertaining to environment and social management framework. The price difference between conventional and efficient 
machines ranges from 25%-100%. 
• 
Similarly, for  EV there is a need of 3% to 5% concessionality in the interest rate in order to breakeven the additional cost 
of interest of EV with that of cheaper traditional vehicles as well as the additional compliance costs pertaining to 
environment and social management framework. The price difference between conventional and efficient vehicles ranges 
from 40 to 300% depending upon vehicle category53 
• 
In case of adaptation, most Indian companies are yet to completely grasp the implications of climate change and how it 
will affect them. This is particularly true for the Indian MSME sector where managing the immediate challenges of

=== PAGE 54 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 50 
 
 
everyday business remains a higher priority than addressing long term risks. At the same time, the MSME sector is also 
most vulnerable to climate risks since most within the sector lack the financial resources to endure even a single rupture 
in supply or demand, caused for instance by an extreme weather event or a new environmental or climate regulation. 
Concessional loans in this case would motivate the vulnerable MSME units to take up adaptation technologies to enhance 
climate resilience of their units.  
• 
The financial model (Annexure 3 of the Funding Proposal) clearly demonstrates that to ensure the feasibility of the project, 
concessional support is required from GCF to SIDBI to enable SIDBI to extend loan at the low interest to MSMEs.  
• 
Based on Capital Investment for each project ticket size – currently SIDBI is financing these loans from 75%-100% of 
total project cost. The loan amount typically ranges from INR 1 million to INR 500 million. The balance amount has to be 
borne by borrowers (say 0%-25% of the project cost).  
• 
The concessionality in terms of interest rate to MSMEs for their climate proposals with the help of blending other funds 
with GCF Funds is envisaged. The final prevailing concession in interest rate to each borrower would be decided based 
on credit rating of the MSME unit. This blending concept is a proven concept which has been earlier used by SIDBI. 
• 
The Interest Rate shall be calculated as follows: 
a. Determine the Accredited Entity’s average cost of funding at prevailing market rates for similar loans. 
 
b. Determine the blended funding cost for Direct and Indirect Loans provided under this Programme. 
 
 
c. Subtract the blended funding cost from the average market rate cost to determine the Direct and Indirect Loan  
     Concessionality Margin. 
 
d. The concessional interest rate to be charged to the MSMEs shall be the blended funding cost plus any 
     allowable administrative margin, ensuring the Concessionality Margin is passed on. 
 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Explain how the project/programme will successfully exit once implementation is completed, including how results and benefits 
will continue beyond the project/programme period and how the contribution to paradigm shift will be maintained.  
Include information pertaining to the longer-term ownership, project/programme exit strategy, operations and maintenance of

Provide information on additional actions to be undertaken by public and private sector or civil society as part of the 
project/programme to ensure sustainability of the results attained. 
Creating a market for long-term climate lending to MSMEs  
The programme has been designed to encourage wider Indian FI landscape to increasingly finance climate technologies and 
initiatives within the MSME sector by creating a market for climate lending for MSMEs.  
To do so, the programme shall create an enabling environment that helps FIs understand the Indian MSME sector, particularly 
its risks and the underwriting skills required to finance it effectively. A range of activities including capacity development and 
training, knowledge sharing and awareness building, provision of credit risk assessment tools, etc. will be deployed to help Indian 
FIs understand and manage the risks associated with the Indian MSME landscape. Furthermore, detailed databases containing 
lists of applicable climate-related technologies (mitigation and adaptation) will further ensure that FIs are completely aware of the 
risks and benefits of the climate technologies that they are financing. 
In addition to this enabling environment, the concessional finance offered by the programme towards capitalizing small finance 
banks, NBFCs and MFIs, is expected to create a catalytic effect and potentially leverage upto an additional USD 2,400 million 
(to be ensured on a programme basis). Successful deployment of this large corpus towards climate technologies shall effectively 
showcase the bankability of the climate interventions within the MSME sector, thereby unlocking further climate financing from 
the wider Indian Fis (including large banks and NBFCs) far beyond the lifecycle of the current programme. 
Making climate technologies attractive for MSMEs 
The programme has been designed to tackle the challenges faced by MSMEs in adopting climate technologies, by creating a 
range of services covering:

=== PAGE 55 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 51 
 
 
 
Concessional lending directly to select MSMEs to create showcases of the real-world benefits of deploying climate 
interventions within the MSME landscape and disseminating success stories across the landscape.  
 
Creating new financial products that are designed specifically to meet the needs of the MSME sector and leveraging 
wider FI network to create an ecosystem of concessional lending towards MSMEs adopting climate technologies.  
 
Enable deployment of innovative facilities such as RESCOs, on-bill financing through DISCOMs, etc. designed to 
create large scale demand amongst MSMEs for climate technologies. 
All of which, when deployed together creates an environment which is designed to activate the neighbourhood effect to its 
fullest capacity within the MSME sector, making climate technologies a very attractive tool to benefit and drive revenue 
generation/profitability within the MSME. This ensures that going forward, beyond the life of the programme, the MSME sector 
remains dedicated towards the adoption of climate technologies in the long-term.  
Finally, the activities proposed under this programme, for both financiers as well as MSMEs are designed to be sector and/or 
region agnostic which ensures that it is replicable, at scale within the country. 
Sustainability and Replicability of the programme  
The activities suggested under the programme are designed considering their sustenance beyond programme period and is as 
described below:  
a. The investment in around ~10000 climate loans under the programme would help in mainstreaming these amongst lenders 
for segments otherwise perceived as unviable or highly risky.  Also, concessional loans is being used to trigger a dormant/slow 
moving market of climate technologies especially w.r.t MSMEs. According to several studies the following is required to stir the 
MSMEs to adopt climate technologies: 
• Concessional funding which is possible through innovative financing model  
• Capacity building/training/awareness generation for both the borrowers and lenders  
• Economies of scale where implementation of a technology/ies at a large scale would lead to spill over effect. For 
example: large scale adoption of any technology would also ensure a market for its services  
• Knowledge management and e-modules would address gaps in information asymmetry 
 
Hence, sustainability and replicability will not just be attained by provisioning loans but all the other related activities of this 
programme is aimed at achieving the same. 
 
b. Also, the proposed digital integrated climate credit rating tool as one of policy instruments will further strengthen the credibility 
of MSMEs proactive in their approach towards environment and social aspect thus bringing such activities as part of their 
business priorities. This will also help the PFIs to conduct loan appraisal/ financial appraisal of MSMEs by not just considering 
the credit worthiness aspects but also how the MSMEs are performing in various environmental and social aspects.  
c. The knowledge repository developed during the project implementation will help other stakeholders including FIs and Banks, 
MSMEs etc. for taking decisions for the adoption of such clean and climate technologies and their financing, The knowledge 
management and capacity building activities under the programme will help overcome existing “Risk Averse” nature of MSMEs 
wherein they tend to “follow rather than taking a lead”. The vendor development activity will help creating knowledge about the 
latest available technologies among MSMEs, at the same time help vendors in developing their local network which is quite 
essential for the penetration of technologies in MSMEs.  
d. Training of Banker/FIs will help in appraisal of technical proposals for the proposed areas of interventions will support in 
overcoming of the perceived risk. E-modules and uploading the same will ensure that the modules are readily available for further 
trainings. Further, the banking association such as Indian Bank’s Association (IBA) will also be encouraged to confer trainings 
for all the banks thus ensuring wide scale reach. 
f. The programme will continue financing for climate change mitigation and adaptation for MSME sector beyond project duration 
to support other financial institutions in the country. This will help sustainability and replicability of the programme through entering 
into financing ecosystem of the country. This will also support other Indian financial institutions to develop standard loan products/ 
instruments for MSME climate actions. SIDBI will seek additional resources from market to replenish the financing to fulfil the 
ultimate objective of mainstreaming the climate financing in MSME sector. The pilot projects developed as part of this programme 
will further be mainstreamed to enhance climate financing by banking sector for MSMEs.

If the project/programme is expected to support capacity building and technology development/transfer, please provide a

The programme will support capacity building activities through Component 2 (Output 2.1 and Output 2.2) 
as detailed below:  
Output 2.1: Capacity built and knowledge management improved to support climate change mitigation and 
adaptation in MSMEs and PFIs. Capacities of selected MSMEs and PFIs are enhanced to rapidly scale their 
business and climate impact 
Output 2.2: Enabling environment for a low-emission and climate-resilient MSME sector 
125. The program will extend technical support to MSMEs and PFIs to address knowledge gaps on low carbon 
and climate resilient technologies. MSMEs will also be facilitated to develop bankable business proposals 
and access finance through the Fund of the project. 
  
126. Capacity building and strengthening of other financial institutions is a continuous process adopted by 
SIDBI under various programs such as PRSF wherein 15 banks/FIs/NBFCs are empanelled with SIDBI to 
support 
financing 
through 
performance 
contracting 
routes. 
While 
SIDBI 
conducts 
regular 
knowledge/capacity building programs for other banks/FIs to support them in enhancing their portfolios 
and to strengthen their capacities to appraise loans for MSMEs, focused climate specific capacity building 
for MSMEs is the need of the hour. Hence, large scale capacity building programs supporting the wider FI 
ecosystem in India to understand MSME climate financing landscape will be a critical part of the TA 
activities in this programme. Successful implementation of these activities is expected to play a key role 
towards the success of the larger programme. 
  
127. The best practices and learning of the program will be shared with larger audiences including other MSMEs 
through e-modules on a digital platform. The TA component will support in development of a repository of 
knowledge and learning established under the proposed program in digital form which will be maintained 
by SIDBI beyond project period and will be accessible by all stakeholders. 
 
128. The proposed program has been designed incorporating and building on learnings acquired by SIDBI over 
time (as showcased in preceding sections). Further, the program will build on the existing knowledge 
landscape developed by SIDBI under its ongoing schemes. 
 
129. The necessity of these activities has been determined through multiple stakeholder conversations with 
players across the climate financing ecosystems including MSMEs, PFIs, local communities etc. Basis 
their inputs, we have considered numerous TA related activities that have been included in our proposal 
which are designed to address climate concerns in the long term.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Describe the potential of the project/programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas. As 
applicable, describe the envisaged project/programme benefits for mitigation and/or adaptation. Provide the intended outcomes for 
mitigation by elaborating on how the project/programme contributes to low-emission sustainable development pathways. Provide 
the intended outcomes for adaptation by elaborating on how the project/programme contributes to increased climate-resilient 
sustainable development. Calculations should be provided as an annex. This should be consistent with section E.3 reporting 
GCF’s core indicators.  
130. The programme will potentially leverage USD 3577 million from public and private sector sources, having a 
mobilization ratio of 1:16.59 towards a dedicated investment in MSME sector for carbon emission reduction and 
promoting MSMEs for accelerating adaptation actions. Of this, 40% will address the adaptation actions and the 
remaining 60% will address mitigation efforts. The monitoring protocol shall describe the approach to obtain, record, 
compile, analyse and document monitoring data, including assumptions, references, activity data, calculation factors, 
quality management procedures and uncertainty, in a transparent manner that enables the reproduction of the 
determination of emissions by the Fund. The monitoring protocol shall also consist of a detailed, complete, and 
transparent documentation of the monitoring methodology for a specific technology.  
Mitigation 
131. Mitigation impact will be achieved through investments at scale in the RE solar, energy efficiency, and low-emission 
transport sectors. In particular, MSME’s in these sectors suffer from several financial and non-financial barriers, 
such as limited access to finance due to high perceived risk, technology and capacity gaps, limited private sector 
involvement, and low awareness of impact potential. 
 
132. The total mitigation impact of the project will be 35.34 million tCO2eq of GHG emissions over equipment lifecycle 
through the adoption of low carbon climate technologies in ~10000 climate loans in MSMEs. A summary of climate 
baseline and additionality assumptions and targeted impact is provided in the table below, with assumptions and 
calculations further detailed in Annexure 22.
