D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Paradigm shift potential is defined as ‘degree to which the proposed activity can catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or 
programme investment’. In this section, elaborate on the contribution to paradigm shift and how the proposed project/programme 
aims to contribute towards it based on the theory of change described in section B2(a). Also describe how and to what extent the 
project/programme will be able to promote or contribute to paradigm shift through the below.  
Potential for scaling up and replication  
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning 
Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment 
Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies  
Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation 
strategies and plans  
 
134. The programme will focus on MSME sector which is credit starved particularly in the climate space. The sub-
projects typically will have low investment, and high emission reduction potential. The dedicated credit and 
technical support approach of the programme will result in crowding-in of private funds and unlock the potential 
of investment for climate change projects in MSMEs. The exclusive investment in climate technologies for 
adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs will accelerate industrial decarbonization trajectory of the country through 
(a) New innovations in climate change domain; and (b) Acceleration of the market for climate technologies. 
The capacity building and training of MSMEs / Industry Associations will generate awareness among the 
MSMEs on the need of climate technologies, as well as increase the focus of banks/ FIs on climate financing. 
The support from the programme will unleash climate innovations, and thereby facilitate climate low carbon 
industrialization. The support for business approach for adaptation actions by investing in MSMEs operating in 
the ecosystem of climate adaptation technologies will upscale adaptation actions and build ecosystem for 
adaptation private sector investment in the country. As detailed below, the project's paradigm shift potential is 
anchored in its comprehensive approach to integrating sustainable practices within the MSME sector through 
scalable, replicable, and knowledge-driven initiatives that are supported by robust regulatory frameworks and 
an enabling environment for private sector participation. 
 
Potential for Scaling Up and Replication 
135. The project has potential to scale up its objectives within India's MSME sector by addressing prevalent 
challenges such as energy inefficiency, high GHG emissions, and excessive water use across sub-sectors 
such as manufacturing and services. It will introduce adaptable technologies and practices that are widely 
applicable, while leveraging SIDBI's financial structures (such as concessional loans and technical support 
funds) as scalable and replicable models for broader adoption in similar emerging subsectors and economies. 
The project’s integration of advanced, resource-efficient technologies facilitated by SIDBI’s extensive network 
will lay the basis for widespread technology transfer, ensuring dissemination and adaptation across India’s 
industrial landscape. Furthermore, the project's alignment with national policies and institutional frameworks 
enhances its replicability, with potential for replication being bolstered by a strong emphasis on capacity 
building, data-driven approaches, and demonstration effects. Enhanced partnerships with technology providers 
and policymakers, alongside alignment with global sustainability goals and India’s climate actions, position the 
project to catalyze a broad paradigm shift towards sustainability, attracting widespread support and fostering 
scalable and effective interventions across sectors. Moreover, mobilizing the Indian Banking system towards 
climate financing will trigger the market and more MSMEs will have access to climate finance for adopting 
climate and clean interventions related to mitigation and adaptation. The adoption of large scale climate 
technologies will also boost the market of service providers therefore ensuring that end of sales service for 
these technologies is not a constraint.  
 
Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Learning 
136. The project will leverage SIDBI's network to enhance MSMEs' access to sustainable practices through 
knowledge hubs that adapt global innovations to local needs and collaborative platforms that engage multiple 
stakeholders. It will promote knowledge dissemination by documenting best practices and lessons learnt, which

=== PAGE 64 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 60 
D 
 
will provide scalable templates for energy efficiency, environmental sustainability and climate adaptation in the 
MSME sector. Additionally, the project will link with national and international networks to leverage the latest 
available and innovative technologies. The documentation and dissemination of learnings through various 
platforms and collaborations with international bodies will further the project’s reach and impact, creating a 
blueprint for knowledge sharing that can be emulated in other contexts The integration of capacity-building 
programs and technical support, particularly in energy-efficient technologies and resource management 
practices, will further foster a community of practice that extends beyond the immediate project lifespan. 
Additionally, a robust Monitoring, Evaluation, and Learning framework to assess the effectiveness of 
interventions will facilitate real-time strategy adjustments. This comprehensive approach will aim to minimize 
the environmental impact of MSMEs while also bolstering their global competitiveness, aligning with the Green 
Climate Fund’s goals for sustainable, low-emission development. 
 
Contribution to the Creation of an Enabling Environment 
 
137. The project will contribute to creating an enabling environment for sustainable industrial practices, particularly 
in the MSME sector. By aligning with national priorities and frameworks such as India’s NDC targets, the project 
will solidify the institutional and policy groundwork necessary for broad-based adoption of climate technologies. 
Through concessional loans and targeted financial products, the project will build institutional capacity within 
SIDBI and local financial entities to manage climate financing effectively. Market transformation will be fostered 
by demonstrating and promoting the adoption of low-emission technologies, thus catalyzing a broader shift 
toward sustainable practices. This comprehensive approach includes capacity building for MSMEs, fostering 
collaborations among stakeholders, and utilizing robust data for informed decision-making, which supports the 
integration of sustainable development goals. These efforts collectively facilitate scalable and replicable models 
of resource efficiency and emissions reduction, positioning the project as a catalyst for widespread industrial 
and environmental reforms aligned with both national and international climate action objectives. 
 
Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies 
 
National Climate Change Strategies and Priorities targeting MSMEs  
 
138. The program is in line with the regulatory and policy frameworks governing the financial, environment, energy 
conservation and promotion of MSME sectors. Micro, Small & Medium Enterprises Development Act 2006, an 
exclusive legislation for micro, small and medium enterprises states under its Section.9 on Measures for 
promotion and development that the Central Government may, from time to time, for the purposes of facilitating 
the promotion and development and enhancing the competitiveness of micro, small and medium enterprises, 
particularly of the micro and small enterprises, by way of development of skill in the employees, management 
and entrepreneurs, provisioning for technological upgradation, marketing assistance or infrastructure facilities 
and cluster development of such enterprises with a view to strengthening backward and forward linkages, 
specify, by notification, such programmes, guidelines or instructions, as it may deem fit. The Act under 
Section.10 on Credit Facility67 states that the policies and practices in respect of credit to the micro, small and 
medium enterprises shall be progressive, and such as may be specified in the guidelines or instructions issued 
by the Reserve Bank, from time to time, to ensure timely and smooth flow of credit to such enterprises, minimise 
the incidence of sickness among and enhance the competitiveness of such enterprises. 
 
139. Government of India has been implementing overarching framework of National Action Plan on Climate Change 
(NAPCC) with its eight missions and State specific Action Plans on Climate Change targeting climate change 
mitigation and adaptation efforts. Under its National Mission on Energy Efficiency, Government of India is 
implementing Perform, Achieve and Track (PAT) scheme to achieve energy efficiency in selected Designated 
Consumers targeting only major high energy-consuming industries. The Performance Linked Incentive Scheme 
is promoting co-investment model for raising blended capital to finance start-ups for agriculture & rural 
enterprise relevant for farm produce value chain. MSME sector is also one of the priority sectors in national 
financial inclusion68 Strategy of Government of India/ RBI which aims to ensure access to appropriate financial 
products and services needed by vulnerable groups such as weaker sections and low-income groups at an 
affordable cost by mainstream institutional players. The Zero Effect, Zero Defect, Make in India Campaign also 
focuses on to the Small and medium-sized enterprises. In line with ambitious climate targets for 2030 and net-

zero pathways for 2070, India has also co-launched Leadership Group for Industry Transition (LeadIT)69 and 
Industry Deep Decarbonization Initiative70 in the last three years to facilitate climate finance and technology 
transfer for industry decarbonization and energy efficiency measures. However, these mainstream schemes 
and programs of the government in areas of energy efficiency and productivity of the industries with emission 
reduction as co-benefits often primarily target large industries. The proposed program will bridge the national 
priorities of enhancing the competitiveness of MSMEs and economy wide emission reduction strategies, by 
focusing on providing low-cost finance and support for adoption of low carbon technologies.   
 
140. Govt. of India has carried out various pilot projects in association with Bilateral / Multilateral agencies including 
the World Bank, UNDP, UNIDO, etc. for enhancement of awareness and capacity building of MSMEs along 
with identification of energy savings potential and implementation challenges. SIDBI is also part of many of 
these projects as a development financial institution working with MSMEs. These projects have been 
successful in demonstrating the potential for GHG emission reductions and resource optimization including 
water, etc. on pilot scale and highlighted the need of customized solutions including technology support 
available at concessional rates for their uptake in MSMEs. The ongoing GCF investments in India are not 
targeted for the needs of MSMEs but may have spillover technology transfer impacts.  
 
141. On adaptation side, the Government of India has adopted various sectoral approaches to address the climate 
vulnerabilities and enhance resilience of communities and the ecosystem. Five out of eight missions under 
NAPCC address sectoral adaptation actions in agriculture, water, forests, Himalayan region, and sustainable 
habitat. For example: The main objective of National Water Mission is the conservation of water, minimising 
wastage and ensuring equitable distribution both across and within states. The private sector including MSME 
is an important partner for achieving the Mission’s objectives, both because it is a major consumer of water 
and because it is an important source of innovation for water efficient processes and technologies. While water 
scarcity and rising water prices already challenge Indian society and business, climate change is likely to put 
additional pressure on water supply. Enhancing water efficiency and sustainably managing water withdrawal 
therefore are key components of a business adaptation strategy. The FMAP programme will target financing 
the MSMEs in water stressed regions to install water efficient technologies in their units so as to reduce their 
dependence on groundwater. Similarly, The National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change 
(NMSKCC) seeks to address a number of gaps in existing research in climate change impacts on India. The 
programme will contribute to a better availability of regionalized data on climate impacts on MSMEs, adaptation 
interventions specific to MSMEs, key preconditions for MSMEs to proactively and successfully implement the 
adaptation interventions etc. The National Mission on Sustainable Habitat seeks to strengthen the ability of 
habitats to adapt to climate change by improving the resilience of infrastructure, strengthening disaster 
management and improving warning systems for extreme weather events. The programme aims to finance the 
MSMEs who would either manufacture/supply/service climate resilient infrastructures like amphibious houses, 
climate proofing/insulation products which can enhance the resilience of habitats and building against climate 
hazards. However, there is huge gap in demand and supply of finance to be invested in adaptation actions. 
The gap in adaptation finance also needs to be addressed by engaging private sector investment with 
innovative business models, enterprises, products and services to accelerate adaptation actions in the country 
to reach its required goals. 
 
142. The project will contribute to regulatory framework and policies by demonstrating sustainable practices within 
India's MSME sector. It will provide data to advocate for stringent environmental standards and enhance policy 
through insights gained from implementing advanced technologies focused on energy efficiency, climate house 
gas reduction, and water conservation. These efforts will support India’s commitments under international 
accords like the Paris Agreement and align with national actions plans on climate change, reinforcing the 
regulatory basis for sustainability. Additionally, the project will introduce innovative financing models that 
incentivize adoption of cleaner technologies and serve as examples for institutional frameworks for climate 
finance, ultimately fostering comprehensive integration of environmental considerations into regulatory and 
financial governance. This integration will not only promote a shift towards sustainable development but will 
also ensure enhanced compliance mechanisms and stakeholder involvement in policy development, making 
environmental policies effective and broadly accepted across the industry. Through these multifaceted 
contributions, the project sets a foundational precedent for future sustainability initiatives within the regulatory 
landscape.

Additional national legislation and priorities that the programme supplements  
143. The Energy Conservation (EC) Act, 2001, accommodates productive utilization of energy and its preservation 
for matters associated therewith or incidental thereto. It was instituted by the Parliament in the Fifty-second 
Year of the Republic of India and was enacted on 29th September 2001 but came into practice from 1st march, 
2002.  
 
144. Environment Protection Act, 1986, and as amended thereof, relates to the protection and improvement of the 
human environment and the prevention of hazards to human beings, other living creatures, plants and property. 
The Act is an “umbrella” legislation designed to provide a framework for central government coordination of the 
activities of various central and state authorities established under previous laws. 
 
145. In addition to the above, this programme is also aligned to the resolve of the Government of India to achieve 
the following: 
 
• 
Reach 500GW non-fossil energy capacity by 2030. 
• 
50 per cent of its energy requirements from renewable energy by 2030. 
• 
Reduction of total projected carbon emissions by one billion tonnes from now to 2030. 
• 
Reduction of the carbon intensity of the economy by 45 per cent by 2030, over 2005 levels. 
• 
Achieving the target of net zero emissions by 2070. 
 
146. Further, in the very recent G20 Delhi declaration, the leaders unanimously accepted the recommendation of 
the Sustainable Finance Working Group (SFWG) to scale up climate financing through innovating finance 
models such as blended finance, to leverage private finance and multilateral climate funds.

such as improved working conditions, reduce negative health impacts due to reduction in fugitive

Co-benefits: 
• 
Job Creation / Employment opportunities through low-cost climate resilient financial assistance - There 
are studies to corroborate the fact that investments in climate energy (EE and RE) help in the growth of the 
economy as well as generate jobs 74. The employment generation per million$ of investment in climate energy in 
India was evaluated to be at 116.1 direct jobs in Energy Efficiency and 215 direct jobs in Renewable Energy75. 
(1) This Programme will cover ~10000 climate loans in MSMEs and will impact around 10.80 million lives indirectly.  
(2) Partnerships with technical experts / ESCOs to bring in much needed expertise through joint ventures and will 
also create new job avenues in service segment. 
(3) Further policy measures (a) Digital Integrated Climate Credit rating tool; (b) Assessment and recommendation 
for domestic carbon trading scheme for MSME sector, will create more jobs. 
• 
Environmental and climate benefits - Energy efficiency in MSMEs can play a major role in reducing local air 
pollution, particularly outdoor concentrations of pollutants such as sulphur dioxide, particulate matter, unburned 
hydrocarbons and nitrogen oxides. In doing so, it can lead to a range of associated economic, environmental and 
health benefits. The Project will enable expected saving of more than 2.75 million MWh energy and ~ 1,458,641 
million litres of water saving through the adoption of improved technologies in MSMEs.  
• 
Better productivity & Quality – Advanced EE machines improves competitiveness, product quality, material 
efficiency and enhance demand in the market. Studies indicate that the value of the productivity and operational 
benefits derived can be up to 2.5 times the value of the energy savings (IEA, 2014b)76. 
• 
Improved bottom line ‘profitability’ - Cost-effective investments in energy efficiency generate energy savings 
that have a direct impact on the profitability of MSMEs and provide net positive earnings in the long run. Energy 
cost reductions can free up resources that can be directed at more productive activities, such as expansion or 
developing new product lines or services. 
• 
Reducing Credit Risk - Energy efficiency can also help mitigate risks for MSMEs. While risk is inherent in all 
business functions and activities, MSMEs are more vulnerable than large companies to changing conditions in 
credit markets, given their limited financial base. MSMEs often face more severe credit conditions than large 
enterprises. Banks may not be as motivated to engage with MSMEs, as the transaction costs of smaller loans are 
proportionately higher. Many banks are uneasy about the risks involved or fail to see quality or resilience in the 
small business segment. At the same time, most MSMEs do not have enough collateral to provide a strong 
alternative repayment source. MSMEs in many countries also pay more per unit of energy than larger companies 
and are hence more vulnerable to price increases. Despite a dip in energy prices across many countries, energy 
costs are increasingly restricting MSME profitability. MSMEs are also more sensitive to energy supply disruptions 
as they may not have back-up systems or on-site generation.  
• 
Future Entrepreneurs ‘Innovation’ - MSMEs are important for climate growth as drivers of eco-innovation and 
key players in emerging climate industries. New and young companies are particularly important for radical climate 
innovations, as they often exploit technological or commercial opportunities that have been neglected by more 
established enterprises or even challenge the business models of existing companies. 
• 
Women Empowerment- Various TA activities including capacity building and financial literacy of women 
workers/civil society representatives will be conducted so that they can become an independent entrepreneur. On 
the other hand, women entrepreneurs will be attracted for credit incentives in terms of Interest Subventions. These

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Describe the scale and intensity of vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups and elaborate how the project/programme 
addresses the issue (e.g. the level of exposure to climate risks for beneficiary country and groups, overall income level, etc.). 
Describe how the project/programme addresses the following needs:  
•

• 
Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population

Country Needs 
India is entering a dynamic phase in energy development post-Covid19, according to the India 
Energy Outlook 2021 report. It highlights the imminent surge in appliance, air conditioning, and 
vehicle purchases. Over the next two decades, to meet electricity demand, India must expand 
its power system to the size of the European Union's. Key to economic growth will be 
affordable, clean, and reliable energy. This requires strong policies, technological 
advancements, and increased clean energy investments. Plans include quadrupling renewable 
electricity by 2030, doubling natural gas use, enhancing energy efficiency and infrastructure, 
boosting coal production, and reducing oil import costs77. As the world's fourth-largest climate 
house gas emitter78, India's vulnerability to climate change was emphasized in the 2015 Paris 
pledge, given its large population, agricultural reliance, extensive coastal areas, and fragile 
Himalayan region79. A World Bank study80 suggests that unchecked emission growth could 
impact 600 million people. Urban migration and population increases could significantly raise 
overall emissions, necessitating large investments to mitigate climate change effects like 
altered rainfall, frequent droughts, groundwater depletion, and rising sea levels, which threaten 
agriculture and food security. District-level vulnerability reports81 by the Council on Energy, 
Environment, and Water (CEEW) show that over 80% of India's population resides in districts 
highly susceptible to extreme hydro-meteorological disasters. Southern India is notably 
vulnerable, followed by eastern, western, northern, north-eastern, and central zones. Several 
states face significant risks from floods, droughts, and cyclones.  India has committed to 
reducing emissions intensity by 45% by 2030 from 2005 levels under the Paris Agreement. 
This could lead to 90% emissions increase from 2014 levels by 2030, yet India’s efforts are 
aligned with the global 2°C warming limit, marking it as one of the few countries on track to 
meet its climate targets. Meeting these targets will require an estimated $2.5 trillion 82 , 
spotlighting the investment gap with current climate finance flows significantly below the 
needed $170 billion annually. This underscores the importance of innovative financing and 
technologies in advancing India’s climate goals.

Needs of MSMEs 
Meeting the India’s aspirations related to climate change mitigation and adaptation through it’s 
INDC and NAPCC requires prolonged approach and resource including easy and affordable 
finance to support its efforts. These efforts have been supported by various National and 
International agencies e.g., GEF, CTF, GiZ, ADB, The World Bank, JICA, KfW, AFD etc. by 
means of technical and financial assistance. The focus of these assistance has been diversified 
and not specific to MSMEs only. Further, many of these have focused on Climate Mitigation 
only and do not include Adaptation measures. Further, the cost of funds from these agencies 
is on the higher side presently, making such projects unviable. 
 
Specific needs of MSMEs in terms of Climate Change Mitigation: 
• 
Reduction of Climate house Gas Emissions: MSMEs are significant contributors to 
carbon emissions, particularly the energy-intensive subsectors. There is a critical need to 
adopt low-carbon technologies and improve energy efficiency. 
• 
Technology Upgrade: Many MSMEs use outdated technologies that are less efficient and 
more polluting. Upgrading these technologies is essential for reducing energy consumption 
and associated emissions. 
• 
Access to Climate Financing: MSMEs often face barriers in accessing finance for climate 
upgrades. There is a need for financial products tailored to the unique challenges and risks 
of the MSME sector. 
• 
Capacity Building and Awareness: MSMEs require training and awareness programs on 
the benefits of energy efficiency and information on available technologies and financing 
options. 
• 
Policy Support: Supportive policies and incentives are needed to drive the adoption of 
climate technologies and practices in the MSME sector. 
 
Climate Change Adaptation Needs: 
• 
Risk Assessment and Management: MSMEs need tools and support to assess, manage 
and forecast climate risks, particularly those related to natural hazards like water scarcity, 
floods, and extreme weather events. 
• 
Infrastructure Resilience: Investments are necessary to make infrastructure more 
resilient against climate impacts, which includes upgrading facilities and adopting resilient 
construction practices. 
• 
Diversification of Water Sources: Given the dependency on monsoon rains, there is a 
need for MSMEs to diversify their water sources and increase their storage capacities to 
buffer against droughts and irregular rainfall. 
• 
Enhanced Information Access: MSMEs need better access to climate forecasts and 
market information to make informed decisions regarding crop planting, water use, and 
other critical business operations. 
• 
Community and Ecosystem Resilience: Programs that enhance the overall resilience of 
the communities and ecosystems in which MSMEs operate are crucial. This includes 
protecting local water sources and ecosystems that support agriculture and other MSME 
activities. 
Further MSMEs can contribute towards the manufacturing/servicing of adaptation 
technologies, especially those which are appropriate and locally suited to the climatic 
vulnerable regions of Himalayas, coastal and eastern belt in the sectors of agriculture, water 
resources, disaster risks, agroforestry and services. The tentative list of technologies are 
outlined below: 
• 
Weather forecasting and early warning systems to identify the climate related natural 
hazards in advance and to prevent loss in lives and livelihood  
• 
Software application- Consumer software applications and enterprise software 
applications on mobile devices concerning climate related services.

147. The Government of India has been implementing overarching framework of National Action Plan on Climate Change 
(NAPCC) with its eight missions and State specific Action Plans on Climate Change targeting climate change 
mitigation and adaptation efforts. Under its National Mission on Energy Efficiency, the Government of India is 
implementing Perform, Achieve and Track (PAT) scheme to achieve energy efficiency in selected Designated 
Consumers targeting only major high energy consuming industries. The Performance Linked Incentive Scheme is 
promoting co-investment model for raising blended capital to finance start-ups for agriculture & rural enterprise 
relevant for farm produce value chain. MSME sector is also one of the priority sectors in national financial inclusion 
Strategy of Government of India/ RBI which aims to ensure access to appropriate financial products and services 
needed by vulnerable groups such as weaker sections and low-income groups at an affordable cost by mainstream 
institutional players. The Zero Effect Zero Defect, Make-in-India Campaign also focuses on to the Small and 
medium-sized enterprises. India’s climate actions, economic development and financial inclusion strategies are 
aligned with the proposed project’s focus on MSME low carbon development pathways and accelerating climate 
adaptation actions. 
 
148. In line with ambitious climate targets for 2030 and net-zero pathways for 2070, India has also co-launched 
Leadership Group for Industry Transition (LeadIT) and Industry Deep Decarbonization Initiative in the last three 
years to facilitate climate finance and technology transfer for industry decarbonization and energy efficiency 
measures. However, these mainstream schemes and programs of the government in areas of energy efficiency 
and productivity of the industries with emission reduction as co-benefits and often primarily targeting large 
industries. The proposed program will bridge the national priorities of enhancing the competitiveness of MSMEs 
and economy wide emission reduction strategies, by focusing on providing low-cost finance and support for adoption 
of low carbon technologies.

149. The proposed program is a great priority of the NDA and is aligned with several priorities included in India’s NDC83, 
including: i) to put forward and further propagate a healthy and sustainable way of living based on traditions and 
values of conservation and moderation, including through a mass movement for ‘LiFE’– ‘Lifestyle for Environment’ 
as a key to combating climate change; ii) to adopt a climate friendly and a cleaner path than the one followed 
hitherto by others at corresponding level of economic development; iii) to reduce Emissions Intensity of its GDP by 
45 percent by 2030, from 2005 level; iv) to achieve about 50% cumulative electric power installed capacity from 
non-fossil fuel-based energy resources by 2030, with the help of transfer of technology and low-cost international 
finance including from Green Climate Fund (GCF); v) to create an additional carbon sink of 2.5 to 3 billion tonnes 
of CO2 equivalent through additional forest and tree cover by 2030; vi) to better adapt to climate change by 
enhancing investments in development programmes in sectors vulnerable to climate change, particularly 
agriculture, water resources, Himalayan region, coastal regions, health and disaster management; vii) to mobilize 
domestic and new & additional funds from developed countries to implement the above mitigation and adaptation 
actions in view of the resource required and the resource gap; and viii) to build capacities, create domestic 
framework and international architecture for quick diffusion of cutting-edge climate technology in India and for joint 
collaborative R&D for such future technologies. 
 
150. India’s NDC is ambitious, and it is a significant contribution towards achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement. 
Environmentally sustainable, low carbon initiatives are underpinning all key sectors of the Indian economy. This 
update to India’s existing NDC is a step forward towards our long-term goal of reaching net-zero by 2070. To align 
with the NDC aims, the project is focusing on financing clean energy generation sources like solar power capacity 
of around 1.62 MWh/Yr which will not only provide clean energy to MSMEs but also reduce their power bills. 
Currently power generation cost on solar rooftop plants is around INR 3-4 per unit (kWh) which is almost 40-50% 
less than the average cost of conventional power.  
 
151. SIDBI, being the government’s DFI, is consulted on all matters related to the development and financing of MSMEs. 
Therefore, it is uniquely positioned to implement effective measures for the development of MSMEs. The 
organization is also consulted by MoEFCC which is the NDA for GCF on specific matters related to climate financing 
of MSMEs, hence it has a strong say in policy consultation of Government of India. 
 
152. Several stakeholder consultations have taken place in the development of this program to ensure strong country 
ownership. These consultations have resulted in the following points to be considered:

Accelerating the MSMEs to adopt climate technologies require incentivization and innovative financing models

• 
A consultation conducted with NBFCs has shown an increasing interest among NBFCs in lending to MSMEs for 
adopting climate interventions.  
• 
Continued handholding in the form of technical assistance is required to address the gap of information symmetry 
among MSMEs with regards to climate technologies.

153. Funding climate technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as a risky business. While the MSMEs can borrow 
from other private PFIs, the concessionality provided under this programme will drive the adoption of climate 
technologies in MSMEs. In addition, funding of climate technologies in remote/rural areas in the country is 
particularly challenging. To address this, the programme’s key feature will be reaching MSMEs in rural areas 
through indirect lending through PFIs who have wider presence. Thus, the programme will not crowd out private 
sector investment as it is targeting MSMEs that do not have access to alternative funding. 
 
154. The entire programme is structured by taking learnings from SIDBI’s previous programs related to Renewable 
Energy and Cleaner Production.  
1. The climate finance portfolio of SIDBI grew 16 times in FY 2023 which is expected to lead to energy savings of 
330 GWh per annum, and GHG emission reduction of 0.23 million tCO2 eq per annum in Indian MSMEs. 
2. In FY 2023-2024, financing of MSMEs for RECP has led to Electricity Savings of > 1.3 billion units annually with 
equivalent GHG Emission Reduction of 1.07 million tCO2 eq./Yr. 
3. The list of 900+ mitigation technologies financed by SIDBI through its various programs is attached as annexure. 
Thus, the best available technologies and practices have been considered and applied by using SIDBI’s past 
successes as models for this programme. 
 
Funding multiplier effect and Estimated cost per tCO2e 
155. GCF contribution by way of loan and TA Grant aggregating to USD 215.6 million (USD 200 million as loan, and 
USD 15.6 million as TA Grant) shall have a multiplier effect and leveraging a total investment of USD 3577 million 
in 5 years (SIDBI – USD 800 million; NBFC/PFIs – USD 2400 million; MSME – USD 377 million) for climate 
mitigation and adaptation measures, thereby improving the efficiency and effectiveness of each dollar invested by 
GCF. 
 
Co-financing Ratio of GCF loan to SIDBI’s loan 1:4 
 
Estimated cost per tCO2eq.  
USD 120 million (GCF contribution) / 35.34 million tCO2 eq. = 3.39 USD / tCO2 eq. 
 
Water Savings 
156. Through the programme, there will be substantial water savings through Adaptation measures by MSMEs, 
amounting to ~ 1,458,641 million litres of water (lifetime). 
 
GCF mobilization mechanism 
 
Mobilization Ratio of 1:16.59 
157. Based on current estimations, the GCF fund of USD 215.6 million will help SIDBI to channelize a total investment 
of USD 3577 million for Mitigation and Adaptation measures resulting a mobilization rate of 1:16.59. Co-Financing 
of USD 800 million from SIDBI, USD 2400 million as loan from NBFCs/PFIs and another USD 377 million will be 
mobilized as investments by MSMEs (around 10% will be invested by MSMEs before disbursements of these loans).  
 
158. SIDBI will lend around 20% of the loans directly and another 80% of the loans will be financed indirectly through 
PFIs from the USD 1000 million FMAP facility.  SIDBI will provide technical handholding support to the selected 
Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs)to design and implement climate lending products within their asset 
portfolio, in line with the climate financial instruments of SIDBI. SIDBI will select the PFIs based on their asset 
portfolio and experience. A detailed selection criteria of PFIs is provided in the term sheet and operations manual. 
Further list of PFI, SFBs and NBFCs with whom SIDBI is working is attached as Additional supporting documents 
2.

E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK 
This section refers to the project/programme’s logical framework in accordance with the GCF’s Integrated Results Management Framework to which the project/programme 
contributes as a whole, including in respect of any co-financing.  
E.1. Project/Programme Focus 
Please indicate whether this proposal is for a mitigation or adaptation project/programme. For cross-cutting proposals, select both. 
☒ Reduced emissions (mitigation) 
☒ Increased resilience (adaptation) 
E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages) 
This section of the logical framework is meant to help a project/programme monitor and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in section D.2 above by applying three 
assessment dimensions - scale, replicability, and sustainability.  
 
Accordingly, for each assessment dimension (see the definition per assessment in the accompanying guidance note), describe the current state (baseline) and the potential scenario 
(target) and rate the current state (baseline) by using the three-point-scale rating (low, medium, and high) provided in the guidance note. Also describe how the project/programme will 
contribute to that shift/ transformation under respective assessment dimensions (scale, replicability and sustainability). In doing so, please refer to section B.2(a) (theory of change).   
 
The programme will exclusively focus on MSMEs which have poor credit access, low investment, and high emission reduction potential. The dedicated credit and 
technical support approach of the programme will result in crowding-in of private funds and unlock the potential of investment for climate change projects in MSMEs. 
The exclusive investment in climate friendly Clean Technologies in MSMEs will accelerate industrial decarbonization trajectory of the country through (a) New innovations 
in climate change domain; and (b) Acceleration of the market for climate technologies. The capacity building and training of MSMEs / Industry Associations will increase 
climate risk resilience, as well as increase the focus of banks/ FIs on green financing. The support from the programme to new and young companies and entrepreneurs 
who often exploit technological or commercial opportunities that have been neglected by more established enterprises or even challenge the business models of existing 
companies will unleash green innovations, and thereby facilitate green low carbon industrialization. The support for business approach for adaptation actions by investing 
in MSMEs operating in the ecosystem of climate adaptation technologies will upscale adaptation actions and build ecosystem for adaptation private sector investment 
in the country.

According to one study, the

will require to use energy

This will also lead to both 
mitigation and adaptation 
against climate effects.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Monitoring and verification (M&V) for verification of project indicators will be undertaken once in two years, starting from the 3rd year onwards, on a sample basis. 
Indicators tracked under M&V activities will include implementations of sub-projects, energy savings, environmental benefits e. g. CO2 emission reductions, and 
other co-benefits at programme level.  
 
As an Accredited Entity, SIDBI will be responsible to Oversee, Supervise, Manage and Monitor the overall implementation of the programme and periodically submit 
progress reports to the GCF as agreed in the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and the Accreditation Management Agreement (AMA).  Executing Entity headed 
by the Project Director consists of experts shall be responsible for the execution of day-to-day activities of the programme for direct loans as well as for the loan 
sectioned through PFIs and report to the AE about the progress of the FMAP. Experts placed at Executing Entity (EE) will be responsible for M&V of sub projects 
sanctioned directly and through PFIs. EE will ensure in house M&V as well as M&E through independent agencies. 
 
Additionally, a Management Information System (MIS) will be developed to assist in project tracking, including dashboards for loan cases and online regular 
monitoring of proposals and related outcomes indicators. AE will collate the data and will further report to NDA and GCF.  
 
Moreover, an Impact Evaluation at the end of the project will be Undertaken. Lastly, as part of the project monitoring process, internal financial audits will be 
conducted annually. Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of Climate Impacts- Mitigation and Adaptation will be achieved through the use of energy 
efficient technologies by MSMEs.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)  
Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, 
prohibited practices, and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the 
proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.  
 
For probability: High has significant probability, Medium has moderate probability, Low has negligible probability 
For impact: High has significant impact, Medium has moderate impact, Low has negligible impact 
Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as well as 
fraudulent, coercive, collusive, and obstructive practices 
Selected Risk Factor 1 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.  
The technical risk may be owing to lack of understanding among the end beneficiaries regarding a particular 
Resource Efficient and Cleaner Production (RECP) technology which may hinder its adoption and may affect market 
demand for these technologies. The operational risk may be related to after end of sale services wherein servicing of 
these equipment/technologies may be hindered by lack of service providers in the market. In addition, MSMEs may 
be unable to invest 10%, resulting in weak commercial appetite from MSMEs in the FMAP facility. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk 
occurring? If so, to what level?  
• Technical Assistance component includes various activities such as awareness creation, capacity building of various 
stakeholders to motivate industries and enabling them to make an informed decision.  
• Further, the programme envisages providing end to end handholding support to participating units. The approach 
has been very fruitful during the implementation of similar programmes in the past by SIDBI. 
• The proposed activities will also support “development of mentors” (Cluster champions or stalwarts) and will also 
perform various cluster development activities under “Project GRiT (Climate  inclusiviTy)” managed by SIDBI. 
The operational risks related to end of sale services will be mitigated by the market itself. It is understood that end of 
sale services is difficult to achieve for limited number of technologies, however, due to large scale technologies 
funded under this programme, there is bound to be spillover effect in the market which would incentivise service 
providers to scale up their operations in the market. 
Selected Risk Factor 2 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Credit 
High 
Medium 
Description 
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.  
The risk perceived under this category relates to the MSMEs those may become non performing over the period of 
time due to various factors including market crunch, loss of revenue, or any other situation beyond their control. 
Credit risk is the possibility of a loss happening due to a borrower's failure to repay a loan or to satisfy contractual 
obligations which may result in the MSMEs becoming non performing over the period of time due to various factors 
including market crunch, loss of revenue, or any other situation beyond their control. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk 
occurring? If so, to what level?

=== PAGE 92 ===

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 88 
 
 
 
 
In line with the RBI guidelines, SIDBI has developed a detailed assessment process for risk measurement following 
which investment decisions are taken. Some of the Risk Mitigation Measures are –  
• 
First charge over all assets created under the programme.  
• 
Personal guarantee of promoter(s) 
• 
Corporate Guarantee of group companies, if available and feasible 
• 
Any other security as offered by the borrower / other unencumbered assets of the Borrower, to meet asset 
coverage norms.  
• 
PRSF / CGTMSE / NCGTC cover may be obtained, wherever felt necessary. 
• 
Further, as per SIDBI’s annual report for FY22-23, the Gross NPA and Net NPA of the Bank stood at nominal 
0.01% and 0.00% during the year. There was no NPA reported in the climate lending portfolio of SIDBI. The 
low rate of NPA is owing to various risk management policies of the organization, rating tools, usage of block 
chain for security information services, focused monitoring of potential units that might defer etc.   
• 
SIDBI commits USD 800 million, through co-financing commitment letter and documented in the term sheet. 
 
Selected Risk Factor 3 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Forex 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.  
Dollar value may change with respect to time. Foreign exchange risk is the possibility for an entity to be affected by a 
variation in the exchange rate between its local currency and the currency used in a transaction with a foreign 
country.  
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk 
occurring? If so, to what level?  
The funds requested from GCF will be in USD while SIDBI will lend in INR. SIDBI will be managing the currency risks 
through appropriate hedging mechanisms and may charge risk premium to the end borrowers. The indicative annual 
hedging cost is taken as 2.85 percent per annum and is indicated in assumptions given in Annexure 3- Financial 
model.  
 
Selected Risk Factor 4 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
ML/FT 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.  
Banks and other financial institutions may be used as a channel for Money Laundering (ML)/ Terrorist Financing (TF) 
hence there is an associated risk related to ML/FT 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability 
of risk occurring? If so, to what level?  
SIDBI has a well formulated policy to prevent ML/FT with well laid out procedure to conduct risk assessment which is 
attached as Annexure. Yes, the mitigation measure can well reduce the possibility of a risk originating from ML/FT. a) 
SIDBI shall carry out ‘Money Laundering (ML) and Terrorist Financing (TF) Risk Assessment’ exercise periodically to 
identify, assess and take effective measures to mitigate its money laundering and terrorist financing risk for clients, 
countries or geographic areas, products, services, transactions or delivery channels, etc. The assessment process 
should consider all the relevant risk factors before determining the level of overall risk and the appropriate level and 
type of mitigation to be applied. While preparing the internal risk assessment, SIDBI shall take cognizance of the 
overall sector-specific vulnerabilities, if any, that the regulator/supervisor may share with SIDBI from time to time. (b) 
The risk assessment by SIDBI shall be properly documented and be proportionate to the nature, size, geographical 
presence, complexity of activities/structure, etc. of SIDBI. Further, the periodicity of risk assessment exercise shall be

=== PAGE 93 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 89 
 
 
 
 
determined by the Board or any other Committee of the Boards of SIDBI to which power in this regard has been 
delegated, in alignment with the outcome of the risk assessment exercise. However, it should be reviewed at least 
annually. (c) The outcome of the exercise shall be put up to the Board or Audit Committee of the Board and should be 
available to competent authorities and self-regulating bodies. (d) SIDBI shall apply a Risk Based Approach (RBA) for 
mitigation and management of the identified risk and would have Board approved policies, controls and procedures in 
this regard. Bank shall implement a Customer Due Diligence exercise, having regard to the ML/FT risks identified and 
the size of business. Further, SIDBI shall monitor the implementation of the controls and enhance them if necessary. 
 
Selected Risk Factor 5 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Other 
Low 
Low 
Description 
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.  
 
Security risk may be related to law and order in any jurisdiction, it may also be due to international territorial disputes. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk 
occurring? If so, to what level?  
In this programme, though we will be focusing on Himalayan region for our adaptation interventions, not all areas 
within Himalayan region have security risk. Also, the industrial units to be financed are located in regions with low or 
minimal security risks with the law and order enforcement being managed by the state and central government. So, 
mitigations related to security risks (if any) are managed by the respective states and central government. The 
programme will not target areas which are affected by international territorial disputes/issues.

F G. POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
Provide the environmental and social risk category assigned to the proposal as a result of screening and the rationale 
for assigning such category. Present also the environmental and social assessment and management instruments 
developed for the proposal (for example, Environment and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), Environment and Social 
Management Framework (ESMF), Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), Environmental and Social 
Management System (ESMS), environmental and social audits, etc.). Provide a summary of the main outcomes of these 
instruments. Present the key environmental and social risks and impacts and the measures on how the 
project/programme will avoid, minimize and mitigate negative impacts at each stage (e.g. preparation, implementation 
and operation), in accordance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguard (ESS) standards. If the proposed project 
or programme involves investments through financial intermediations, describe the due diligence and management 
plans by the Executing Entities (EEs) and the oversight and supervision arrangements. Describe the capacity of the EEs 
to implement the ESMP and ESMF and arrangements for compliance monitoring, supervision and reporting. Include a 
description of the project/programme-level grievance redress mechanism, a summary of the extent of multi-stakeholder 
consultations undertaken for the project/programme, the plan of the Accredited Entity (AE) and EEs to continue to 
engage the stakeholders throughout project implementation, and the manner and timing of disclosure of the applicable 
safeguards reports following the requirements of the GCF Information Disclosure Policy and Environmental and Social 
Policy. 
 
Describe any potential impacts on indigenous peoples and the measures to address these impacts including the 
development of an Indigenous Peoples Plan and the process for meaningful consultation leading to free, prior and 
informed consent, pursuant to the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy.  
 
Attach the appropriate assessment and management instruments or other applicable studies, depending on the 
environmental and social risk category as annex 6.  
 
Environmental and social risk categorization:  
The Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) is prepared referring to policies, act, rules of the government 
of India and Green Climate Fund (GCF) of Project/Program Financing.  
The existing national policies and legislations, international frameworks on environment, social and energy efficiency 
issues along with the ESMP of Accredited Entity (SIDBI) and GCF’s environmental and Social Safeguard Policies were 
reviewed for the ESS categorization of the proposal. Further, the ESMS has been aligned with IFC’s Performance 
Standards. The potential adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts related to the Programme activities are 
individually or cumulatively generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through adequate policy 
implementation and workplace standards. Further, the initial screening based on Exclusion List will ensure that Category 
A/I1 projects are screened out and will not be financed under this programme. The activities and sub-activities under this 
programme therefore would be classified as a Category ‘B’ under the GCF Risk Categories. However, the program will 
be categorized as Medium level of intermediation, or I2 according to GCF guidelines as financial intermediation will be 
involved in the investment process 
 
Compliance with National Legislation:  
As a responsible financier, SIDBI has adopted various institutional mechanisms to ensure its compliance with the relevant 
environmental and social laws/norms. Under this, SIDBI has: 
• 
Adopted an Environmental and Social Management Policy (ESMP) which ensures that it’s initiatives are aimed at 
reducing environmental and social risks. The ESMP outlines SIDBI’s engagement with PFIs in the case of indirect 
financing.  
• 
Adopted the Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace as per the Sexual 
Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace Act, 2013 
• 
Adopted a Customer Grievance Redressal Policy to ensure prompt and efficient service to its customers. 
 
These detailed policies are placed in Annexure 1, Attachment 2 & 3 respectively at the end of Annexure 6.  
 
In compliance with GCF’s ESMS framework, the ESMS framework for this programme aims to achieve the following 
outcomes/meet performance standards: 
1. List of projects supported/Screening: Based on the risk categorization, SIDBI’s ESMP identifies the 
type/nature of projects to be supported.

G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 91 
 
 
 
 
2. Identify and assess environmental & social risks: Projects are assessed for their potential environmental and 
social impacts. This includes both negative impacts that need to be mitigated and potential benefits that can be 
enhanced.  
 
3. Manage & Monitor Risk, Reporting & Compliance: Based on the assessments, management plans are 
developed to address the risks through various measures including mitigation strategies, monitoring plans, and 
stakeholder engagement activities. Further, it ensures compliance with national regulations and international 
standards, and reporting on environmental and social performance. 
 
4. Grievance Redressal: This also includes a Grievance Redressal Mechanism to ensure all grievances are 
addressed in line with applicable policies and standards. 
 
5. Public Disclosure and Consultation: SIDBI’s through its website, will publicly disclose relevant reports in 
compliance with its Information Disclosure policy. The results of the preliminary environmental and social 
screening, and draft environmental and social assessment, shall be made available for public consultations. 
 
6. Zero Tolerance of SEAH:  SIDBI has ZERO TOLERANCE towards any sexually harassment in any form. SIDBI 
has adopted the Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace as per the 
Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace Act, 2013. 
 
7. Indigenous People: The COP decision adopting the Paris Agreement recognized the need to strengthen 
practices and efforts of local communities and Indigenous Peoples related to addressing and responding to 
climate change and operationalized the local communities and Indigenous Peoples platform to help do this. 
However, given that MSMEs are restricted to areas of industrial clusters/special economic zones, SIDBI’s project 
is not anticipated to have impacts on indigenous people, their lands, culture, livelihood or way of life. 
 
Details on each of the above elements of the ESMS framework are shared below: 
 
Project Screening 
SIDBI itself, or the PFIs that are engaged, will screen the projects to ensure that the proposed projects are not in the 
Negative list/ Exclusion list prepared by SIDBI as per their ESMP (Appendix I of Annexure 1 of the ESMS (annexure 
6)). The negative list includes projects/ programmes involving harmful or exploitative form of child labour, production or 
trade in weapons or munitions, production or trade in alcoholic beverages and tobacco, etc. The screening based on 
Exclusion List will ensure that Category A/I1 projects are screened out and will not be financed under this programme.  
 
Identification & assessment of E&S risks 
Further to the initial screening of the projects, SIDBI or the PFIs are required to screen the potential risks and 
environmental and social compliance of the project through document verification, field visits, and environmental and 
social checklist (Refer Appendix II of Annexure 1 of the ESMS (Annexure 6)). Further, each project is evaluated for its 
E&S risk using a proprietary ESG rating tool. This allows SIDBI to characterize the risk being considered while financing 
and accordingly plan for the mitigation.  
 
 
Manage & Monitor Risk, Reporting & Compliance 
SIDBI’s ESMP (Refer Section 9.2, 9.3, 9.4 of Annexure 1 of the ESMS (Annexure 6)) states that it is the responsibility 
of the PFIs/Host entity to address all environmental and social risks identified during the screening of environmental and 
social risks. The implementing entities’ programme reports shall include a section on status of implementation of any 
environmental and social management plan, including those measures required to avoid, minimize, or mitigate the risks.  
The reports shall also include, if necessary, a description of any corrective actions that are deemed necessary. 
Accordingly, the mid- term and terminal evaluation reports shall also include an evaluation of Programme performance 
with respect to environmental and social risks. 
The formats and periodicity of submitting such reports for effective monitoring is decided during the commencement of 
the Programme. 
 
Grievance Redressal Mechanism 
SIDBI has a comprehensive 3-tier Grievance Redressal Mechanism (GRM) (Refer Attachment 3 of ESMS Annexure 
6). It has put in place a web- enabled registration form for complaints/ grievances, whereby the project affected 
stakeholders can log their grievances.

=== PAGE 96 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 92 
 
 
 
 
SIDBI further mandates that the implementing agencies/ PFIs shall also have in place an effective GRM that provides for 
stakeholders affected by the projects supported by SIDBI. Such a mechanism shall be accessible, transparent, fair, and 
effective for receiving and addressing complaints about potential or actual environmental or social harm caused by any 
such project. 
 
Public Disclosure & Consultation 
SIDBI mandates that the PFIs identify stakeholders as early as possible in planning any programme. The results of the 
preliminary environmental and social screening, and draft environmental and social assessment, shall be made available 
for public consultations. 
Further, the scope and frequency of stakeholder engagement depends on the nature and scale of the programme and its 
potential environmental and social risks.  
Additionally, SIDBI’s existing ESMP states that SIDBI, through its website, will publicly disclose relevant reports in 
compliance with its Information Disclosure policy. 
 
Further, the Annexure on Stakeholder Engagement (Annexure 7 of Funding Proposal) has details on the plan to consult 
stakeholders at a regular frequency. It also has key findings from extensive stakeholder consultations conducted by SIDBI.  
 
Zero tolerance of SEAH 
SIDBI has adopted the Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace as per the 
Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace Act, 2013. The Act aspires to ensure 
women’s right to workplace equality, free from sexual harassment through compliance with the mentioned three elements 
viz. “Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal.” 
SIDBI has ZERO TOLERANCE towards any sexually harassment in any form.  
To give effect to the principle of zero-tolerance, FMAP recognizes the importance of its financed activities to prevent and 
respond effectively to SEAH in a survivor-centered and gender-responsive way.  
SIDBI will further ensure that the implementing agencies/ financial institutions shall also have in place an effective SEAH 
guidelines, norms acts in place as per requirement. 
 
Indigenous People 
Given that MSMEs are restricted to areas of industrial clusters/special economic zones, SIDBI's project is not anticipated 
to have adverse impacts on indigenous people, their lands, culture, livelihood or way of life.  
 
However, SIDBI through its PFIs will ensure sub-projects through FMAP, include measures to (a) avoid potentially adverse 
effects on the Indigenous Peoples’ communities, or (b) when avoidance is not feasible, minimize, mitigate, or compensate 
for such effects. The sub-projects are also designed to ensure that the Indigenous Peoples receive social and economic 
benefits that are culturally appropriate and gender and inter-generationally inclusive. 
 
An Indigenous People Framework (IPPF) has been developed (Refer Attachment 6 of the Annexure on ESMS) to 
mitigate any potential risk arising out of the activities of the programme that may have adverse impacts on indigenous 
people. Further, an Indigenous People Plan (IPP) will be developed for all projects affecting indigenous communities and 
Indigenous Peoples where impacts are identified, whether they are direct or indirect, negative or positive, if applicable 
with necessary disclosure for IPPs. 
 
Resettlement Action Plan: 
In case of greenfield projects where there is any likelihood of compulsory land acquisition and involuntary resettlement, a 
resettlement action plan has been developed which is aimed at the following: 
 
(a). To identify the risks at the sub-project level which may lead to compulsory land acquisition, involuntary resettlement 
affecting physical and/or economic displacement of people (loss of  structures, livelihoods and access to common 
resources 
(b). To mitigate the identified risks associated with compulsory land acquisition and involuntary resettlement and to 
check the adequacy and implementation of the mitigation measures 
(c). To develop a monitoring framework to check the adequacy and implementation of mitigate measures.

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
Provide a summary of the gender assessment and project/programme-level gender action plan that is aligned with the 
objectives of GCF’s Gender Policy. Confirm a gender assessment and action plan exists describing the process used to 
develop both documents. Provide information on the key findings (who is vulnerable and why) and key 
recommendations (how to address the vulnerability identified) of the gender assessment. Indicate if stakeholder 
consultations have taken place and describe the key inputs integrated into the action plan, including: how addressing 
the vulnerability will ensure equal participation and benefits from funds investment; key gender-related results to be 
expected from the project/programme with targets; implementation arrangements that the AE has put in place to ensure 
activities are implemented and expected outcomes will be achieved, monitored and evaluated. 
 
Provide the full gender assessment and project-level gender action plan as annex 8.  
 
 
Approach 
In preparing the Gender Assessment & Action Plan for the Programme, following approaches have been leveraged: 
1. Baseline assessment of participation of women in the MSME sector in India as entrepreneurs 
a. The literature reviewed from secondary sources to understand the baseline situation and the broad scope 
for EE applications in the sector considering gender dimensions. In this regard, relevant reports, about 
national policies and guidelines (environmental, social, gender equality), historical studies and research 
on Energy Efficiency (EE), international best practices, potential EE interventions etc. considering gender 
equity were analysed.

=== PAGE 98 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 94 
 
 
 
 
b. This assessment has been done based on SIDBI’s existing initiatives to promote women 
entrepreneurship and based on research studies supported by SIDBI. 
 
2. Assessment of challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in the MSME sector 
a. Stakeholder consultation i.e. “Strengthening Green Financing and Mainstreaming Gender” organised 
under the banner ‘Green Indian Financial System (GIFS)85’ – Greening of Finance by Women (Grow)86 
Initiatives, founded by SIDBI, Shakti Sustainable Energy Foundation (SSEF) and French Development 
Agency (AFD) has identified the key challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in the MSME sector 
 
3. Review of SIDBI’s existing capabilities to promote women entrepreneurship/gender equality 
a. This assessment has been done based on a review of SIDBI’s existing initiatives to promote women 
entrepreneurship and participation of women in the MSME sector.  
b. Further, SIDBI’s gender policies have been reviewed in line with GCF’s gender policies 
 
Key Findings & Assessment 
• 
Gender Parity in Workforce: Only ~24% of the workforce in MSMEs is women. This is lower than the national 
labour force participation rates for females (~28.7% in 2023). This suggests that MSMEs have a scope to improve 
their gender parity in the workforce. 
• 
Gender Parity in Ownership: As per the statistical data provided on the Udyam Registration Portal (“URP”) 
under the Ministry of MSME87, women owned MSMEs constitute 20.37 per cent of the total number of registered 
MSMEs 
 
• 
Following are some of the key challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in India.  
 
• 
Not Sustainable: ~73% women-led enterprises have faced a reduction in revenues post COVID-19. Most 
female entrepreneurs are concentrated in sectors and industries highly affected by the pandemic and 
digitization like micro and small businesses in non-professional services, such as tailor shop owners, 
dressmakers, shopkeepers and street vendors, arts and handicrafts vendors, and beauty-parlor owners, 
among others. This points to the fact that women led businesses are not financially 
sustainable/resilient enough to recover from external shocks.  
• 
Not Autonomous: In ~20% cases, the business-related decisions are taken by the respective spouses and 
not the women entrepreneur running the business. Often, women do not truly own or control many women-
led enterprises. They depend considerably on men for key business decisions, procurement of raw 
materials, pricing information, and market access. 
• 
Not Formal: ~95.6% of the women entrepreneurs in India are informal and not registered with relevant 
national/state institutions. A high level of informality harms the growth and resilience of their enterprises and 
their entrepreneurial rights. The level of effort involved in running such enterprises go unaccounted for and 
expose women to occupational health hazards. Further, informality leads to challenges in accessing 
finance, market, etc.   
• 
Not sufficient employment generating: ~95% of women proprietors run with less than six workers, most 
being own-account or single person enterprises. Only ~1.9% proprietors run with six to 10 employees. This 
points to the fact that women entrepreneurs often lack skills in scaling businesses i.e. in areas such 
as business management, market access, access to finance, etc. 
• 
Lack of awareness and skills on green jobs and technologies:  Women in Indian MSMEs have been 
confined to low-paying, low-skilled jobs. The ongoing transitions present them with an opportunity to realign 
their skill sets and adjust to the evolving nature of work. Digital & Green Technologies proficiency is one of 
the most important skills that women need to acquire to keep pace with the ongoing transitions. This will 
enable them to enter traditionally male-dominated sectors that rely heavily on digital technologies, such as 
healthcare services, automobiles, and more advanced roles within the textiles sector with green technologies.

Alignment of the Project with GCF Gender Assessment Guideline  
• 
SIDBI as an AE shall take the necessary measures to ensure that proposed projects, as well as proposed activities 
under the FMAP, submitted to GCF for financing are aligned with national policies and priorities on gender.  
• 
The project activities shall be framed around principals of GCF and will ensure the following issues in the gender 
action plan:    
 
  
Gender Action Plan 
 
Based on the programme design, the broad aim is to ensure adequate representation for women as stakeholder and 
beneficiary under component (1) supporting affordable financing of mitigation and adaptation activities; targeted technical 
support under component (2), for capacity development, awareness generation, etc., as well as in M&E frameworks; and 
leveraging financing mechanisms for greater and more equitable outreach and coverage in the MSME sector going ahead. 
A stated endeavor of the programme is to secure 80:20 coverage (i.e. 20% financing towards women), broadly in line with 
the selected baselines for women’s ownership and labour force participation. In the longer term, the aim is to contribute 
to a paradigm shift in terms of women’s capacity and participation in ownership/ leadership/ management and technical 
roles/workforce participation in, as well as other benefits from climate mitigation and adaption activities taken up in the 
MSME sector in India. This will ensure that the current challenges on women in MSMEs, especially in the area of climate, 
will be addressed and thus the landscape of women-led MSMEs in India will be significantly transformed.  
Following are the outcomes that the program aims to achieve: 
• 
Number of women led MSMEs: Increase the number of women owned MSMEs through necessary interventions 
and ensure enhanced participation of women workforce in MSMEs. 
• 
Business Sustainability through improve access of finance: Make women led enterprises/MSMEs more 
sustainable & resilient to external shocks, especially climate shocks by providing access to affordable finance for 
mitigation and adaption technologies. Services and technologies provided by the project/program shall be 
available and accessible to both women and men.

=== PAGE 100 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.12.0 | PAGE 96 
 
 
 
 
• 
Autonomy: Increased skilling for women entrepreneurs to ensure their businesses are more climate resilient by 
offering handholding support on the adoption of better technology. Encourage women entrepreneurship in energy 
efficiency, renewable, electric vehicles and other identified sectors 
• 
Formalization: Increased formalization of women-led MSMEs, leading to enhanced access to credit.  
• 
Employability & Employment Generation: Ensuring the opportunities of creating more employment from 
Women own MSMEs by capacity building on climate finance and awareness on energy efficient technology. The 
measures will also be taken to equip the women workforce for green jobs helping in just transition. 
• 
Encouraging gender-focused practices among MSMEs: The project would encourage those MSMEs with 
women-centric workplace or are willing to create the one. These will include having a gender action plan, separate 
rest spaces, better lighting etc. 
 
A detailed gender action plan outlining the baseline assessment, investment amounts, M&E processes, etc. has been 
outlined in Annexure 8.

159. In line with the Govt. of India Procurement guidelines, SIDBI has an established Procurement Manual which is 
approved by the Board (enclosed). The manual provides guidance on the following (including but not limited to):

ii. procurement methods/approach for Goods, Works and Services  
iii. thresholds/ limits under various methods of procurement 
iv. Mandatory procurement through Govt e-marketplace (GeM) which is an electronic platform by GoI for transparency

Commission (KVIC) etc.

categorized duly addressing the potential risk and to maintain transparency in the process. Further SIDBI has an 
established mechanism for financial and administrative review and approval powers delegated to the Competent

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for 
all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme 
funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the 
redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of 
the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, 
segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence. 
☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity 
is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
•

accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and

redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.
