GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 107 
 
 
 
 
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP241 
Proposal name: 
Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in Indian 
MSMEs 
Accredited entity: 
Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) 
Country/(ies): 
India 
Project/programme size: 
Large 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Overview 
1. 
The Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Project (FMAP) aims to accelerate the adop-
tion of low-emission and climate-resilient practices among micro, small and medium-sized en-
terprises (MSMEs) in India while promoting the greening of the country’s financial sector. Led 
by Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) as the accredited entity (AE) and execut-
ing entity (EE), the programme focuses on financing resource-constrained sectors. A key benefit 
of FMAP is its emphasis on financing adaptation projects, which have so far seen limited uptake 
by both public and private financial institutions. In addition, the programme seeks to boost the 
effectiveness of primary financial institutions (PFIs), including small finance banks (SFBs), mi-
crofinance institutions (MFIs) and non-banking financial companies (NBFCs), by enabling them 
to build a track record that supports scaling up climate finance in India. 
2. 
FMAP consists of two components. The first is a USD 1 billion lending facility managed 
by SIDBI, capitalized by an USD 800 million contribution from SIDBI and an additional USD 200 
million from the GCF. These funds will support direct lending (20 per cent) to MSMEs for cli-
mate-aligned initiatives and indirect financing (80 per cent) of MSMEs through PFI partners. 
The indirect financing will be channelled to MSMEs via PFIs, which are required to co-finance 
three times the amount received, mobilizing an additional USD 2.4 billion. This three times com-
mitment is measured on a portfolio basis rather than at the level of the individual loans. Fur-
thermore, participating MSMEs are expected to contribute equity ranging from 10 to 25 per cent 
of the total loan for eligible projects. The concessionality provided by the GCF funds enables 
FMAP to adopt a flexible approach (e.g. in terms of grace periods and tenors) in structuring sub-
loans and to offer concessional capital costs to incentivize uptake among MSMEs. 
3. 
The second component is a USD 16 million grant facility to be implemented by SIDBI. 
These grants will support MSMEs and PFIs in greening their operations by identifying the tech-
nical needs of MSMEs, promoting knowledge-sharing, building capacity among stakeholders and 
contributing to policy initiatives. SIDBI will prioritize conducting technology needs assessments 
to identify key solutions for MSMEs and capacity-building for PFIs, and will implement due dili-
gence on their loan books through a dedicated project management unit. Leveraging its experi-
ence in knowledge-sharing and capacity-building, SIDBI will lead training sessions and work-
shops to raise awareness and address barriers. The grant component is sized at approximately 
1.6 per cent of the loan facility component. 
4. 
The objective of FMAP is to address market gaps in access to finance for resource-con-
strained sectors, particularly in adaptation, by providing concessional lending and technical as-
sistance. By working through PFIs in addition to direct lending, the programme seeks to support

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 108 
 
 
 
 
the greening of the Indian financial sector. In addition, FMAP aims to induce positive market sig-
nals on the commercial viability of climate projects and attract local financial institutions and 
private sector finance. 
1.2 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
           Scale: Medium to high 
5. 
India is a major emitter of greenhouse gases (GHGs). Given its rapid economic develop-
ment and substantial population growth, it is imperative for India to pursue a development path 
that leads to a low-carbon, more resilient future. According to the third national communication 
and initial adaptation communication submitted by India under the Convention in December 
2023, India’s net national GHG emissions in 2019 amounted to 2.6 billion tonnes of carbon diox-
ide equivalent (t CO2 eq), reflecting a 4.56 per cent increase from the 2016 level and a 115 per 
cent increase since 1994. CO2 remains the main GHG, accounting for 79 per cent of total emis-
sions in 2019, primarily due to increased fossil fuel combustion, and rising aluminium and ce-
ment production. 
6. 
The FMAP funding proposal explains that India’s energy-intensive MSME subsectors are 
substantial contributors of GHG emissions. However, MSMEs face several barriers to becoming 
more climate-aligned, include their use of conventional processes and technologies, coupled 
with unskilled or semi-skilled workers, leading to higher resource consumption and associated 
waste generation and emissions. There are over 63 million MSMEs in India, employing about 
110 million people and accounting for about 45 per cent to the country’s manufacturing output. 
MSMEs consume about 25 per cent of the total energy used by the industrial sector in India, rep-
resenting a significant opportunity to reduce resource consumption, including electricity, fossil 
fuels and water, in a cost-effective manner. FMAP aims to support this transition by improving 
access to finance and facilitating MSME access to greener technologies. 
7. 
Considering the above, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) takes a posi-
tive view of FMAP’s main goal of sensitizing MSMEs towards adopting green and cleaner pro-
duction technologies, and developing and deploying adaptation technologies. The iTAP also con-
curs with the programme’s aim to encourage banks and financial institutions to develop dedi-
cated green instruments and lend with increased confidence to meet MSMEs’ demand for adopt-
ing and developing the necessary green technologies. Transitioning MSMEs in India to lower-
carbon, more resilient pathways could be a crucial contributor to helping India meet its climate 
goals. 
8. 
FMAP will provide loans to MSMEs targeting the renewable energy, energy efficiency 
and low-emission transport sectors. In addition, it will support adaptation by helping MSMEs in 
the water-intensive and agriculture sectors to implement climate solutions in their businesses, 
thereby increasing resilience and adaptive capacity against climate change. Adaptation invest-
ments will be directed to highly vulnerable regions such as coastal areas, the Himalayas and the 
eastern belt of India. According to the funding proposal, FMAP is expected to avoid 43.86 million 
t CO2 eq and benefit 10.3 million people. The iTAP notes that estimating GHG reductions over 
such a large programme and long tenor (13 years) is challenging due to uncertainties in the 
composition of underlying loans. It is therefore difficult to put very much stock on specific pro-
jections in the context of FMAP; however, the iTAP acknowledges that the targeted loan areas 
(e.g. energy efficiency, low-emission transport, renewables) coupled with climate-aligned 
screening mechanisms mean that there is the potential for significant impact. Importantly, the 
sheer scale of India and the role of MSMEs in the economy support an argument that the overall 
impact will be positive from a climate standpoint. 
9. 
Moreover, FMAP is expected to mobilize USD 3.5 billion from public and private sector 
sources, with co-financing at a ratio of 1:18 towards dedicated investments in MSMEs for GHG 
emission reductions and accelerating MSME adaptation actions. Of this, the funding proposal es-
timates that 40 per cent of the funding will address adaptation actions, and the remaining 60

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 109 
 
 
 
 
per cent will focus on mitigation efforts. The iTAP agrees that the potential mobilization impact 
could be significant, and the potential to redirect the lending of PFIs in India towards climate-
aligned interventions is a valuable aspect of this funding proposal.  
10. 
The iTAP also concurs that the well-sized and tailored use of grants alongside commer-
cial lending can help to develop a pipeline for the lending facility and, in turn, increase confi-
dence that MSMEs will take up loans and effect operational changes that have beneficial climate 
outcomes. Moreover, the grant component has the potential to accelerate the greening of the lo-
cal financial sector as envisaged in the funding proposal, which is critical to the programme’s 
success.  
11. 
The iTAP considers that the programme is well-positioned to mitigate barriers to ac-
cessing finance, by directly supporting MSMEs through green lending at concessional rates 
while incentivizing local financial institutions through wholesale lending. Direct lending will tar-
get medium-sized enterprises, while indirect lending will support micro and small enterprises, 
ensuring complementarity between both channels. For indirect lending, SIDBI will work with 
eligible local financial institutions, MFIs, NBFCs and SFBs. This approach could allow SIDBI to 
reach a broader range of MSMEs through these institutions’ networks and help them increase 
their capacity to extend green loans for implementing or developing climate solutions. This in-
clusive structure addresses the immediate need for finance availability. 
12. 
FMAP intends not only to increase the availability of finance for climate-related loans to 
MSME but also to use concessionality to accelerate the transition to climate-friendly technolo-
gies. The AE has made a credible case for the levels of concessionality needed to shift PFIs to-
wards climate-aligned sectors and, more importantly, to encourage MSMEs to adopt technology 
changes that enhance their resilience to climate impacts and reduce GHG emissions. However, 
the iTAP notes a concern over how levels of concessionality will be determined over time. The 
iTAP recommends that SIDBI regularly evaluate and adjust concessionality on a sector-by-sec-
tor basis to avoid over-subsidization, which could generate windfall gains for some PFIs while 
crowding out other players.  
13. 
In addition, the iTAP has some qualms concerning the risk that a substantial portion of 
FMAP funding could end up refinancing existing PFI portfolio loans instead of financing new and 
additional MSME efforts to adopt climate-friendly technology. There is very little climate-di-
rected utility in using GCF concessionality to refinance mitigation efforts that have already been 
funded commercially. SIDBI has indicated that it may wish to fund some portfolio refinance in 
the early stages of the programme as a way to attract PFI partners and facilitate their co-financ-
ing capacity. The iTAP is willing to accept this argument but strongly recommends that the Sec-
retariat and SIDBI develop safeguards to ensure that the bulk of concessional funding is truly for 
additional projects. The iTAP encourages SIDBI to develop a plan for FMAP that minimizes port-
folio refinancing over time on a trajectory to zero over the programme’s tenure. 
14. 
Notwithstanding the above concerns, the iTAP regards FMAP’s impact potential as me-
dium-high. The significant potential for mobilization of substantial local finance into a high cli-
mate impact sector has strongly influenced this assessment.  
 
1.3 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
  
                    Scale: High 
15. 
The funding proposal convincingly demonstrates that MSMEs in India are crucial to the 
local economy and that any significant shift in India’s climate trajectory must address their GHG 
emissions and vulnerability to climate impacts. The proposal also effectively argues that a key 
barrier to climate action is the lack of access to affordable finance. FMAP will provide conces-
sional loans to MSMEs with limited credit access, enabling them to adopt and develop climate 
solutions. By enhancing the capacity of MSMEs and financial institutions for green financing

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 110 
 
 
 
 
practices, FMAP seeks to build institutional capacity within SIDBI and local financial entities to 
manage climate financing effectively. Market transformation will be driven by demonstrating 
and promoting low-emission technologies, thereby catalysing a broader shift towards sustaina-
ble practices. 
16. 
The iTAP views favourably the effort to pull the existing financial system into climate 
lending, noting that the most effective approach is to adapt current systems to align better with 
climate goals. FMAP recognizes that MSMEs in India already engage in financial practices, albeit 
inadequately scaled, which can be steered towards a greener future through a mix of co-finance, 
concessionality and technical support. This is a pragmatic approach and avoids the pitfall of 
one-off innovations that often plague climate finance thinking. The iTAP also notes that the GCF 
paradigm shift potential is defined as the “degree to which the proposed activity can catalyse 
impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment”. By creating conditions that incen-
tivize Indian PFIs to finance climate technologies for MSMEs, it is expected that PFIs will de-
velop a comfort level in these sectors. This should spur additional private capital flows from the 
Indian financial system into the sector, scaling up climate finance and accelerating the country’s 
decarbonization efforts. Potential for replication and overall market uptake is a key factor in 
achieving a paradigm shift. 
17. 
SIDBI has demonstrated its past success in providing local financial institutions with 
technical support and concessional loans. This track record gives the iTAP some confidence in 
the AE’s ability to scale and replicate models for addressing energy inefficiency and high GHG 
emissions across various MSME sectors. FMAP’s capacity-building initiatives are expected to in-
crease awareness of available climate solutions among MSMEs and help participating PFIs to en-
hance their ability to extend climate financing. The programme will promote knowledge dissem-
ination by documenting best practices and lessons learned, providing scalable templates for en-
ergy efficiency, environmental sustainability and climate adaptation in the MSME sector. 
18. 
The iTAP also notes favourably that FMAP will focus on MSMEs with poor credit access, 
low investment potential and high emission reduction potential. Investing exclusively in cli-
mate-friendly technologies for MSMEs will have the added benefit of accelerating the growth of 
the product and service providers essential to India’s decarbonization trajectory. The paradigm 
shift benefits extend beyond direct interventions at the MSME level or getting PFIs comfortable 
with climate lending; they also support the expansion of the ecosystem of climate-friendly solu-
tions in India. The scale of lending and concessional support has the potential to drive demand 
for low-carbon technologies and services (and climate adaptive interventions), which in turn 
could spur greater supply and investment into providers of these products and services. 
19. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the potential for FMAP to effect paradigm shift as high.  
1.4 
Sustainable development potential                         Scale: Medium to high 
20. 
The FMAP funding proposal estimates that the programme will directly reduce GHG 
emissions and benefit 10.3 million people through approximately 10,000 green loans to MSMEs 
over its 13-year lifespan. These loans will facilitate the adoption of climate technologies, which 
are expected to help MSMEs save over 100,000 GWh of energy and 1,241 million cubic metres of 
water over the life time of the technologies and equipment. In addition to the anticipated cli-
mate benefits, the proposal projects ancillary environmental benefits, such as cleaner air 
through reduced air pollution. For instance, energy efficiency improvements implemented by 
MSMEs can significantly reduce local air pollution, particularly outdoor concentrations of pollu-
tants such as sulphur dioxide, particulate matter, unburned hydrocarbons and nitrogen oxides. 
21. 
The funding proposal credibly demonstrates FMAP’s potential to contribute to India’s 
sustainable development beyond mitigation and adaptation impacts, as follows: 
22. 
Economic co-benefits:

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 111 
 
 
 
 
(a) 
The proposed climate financing is expected to stimulate economic activity and create 
over 400,000 jobs. Low investment costs for implementing green technologies are antic-
ipated to directly enhance the profitability of MSMEs through increased productivity 
and product quality;  
(b) 
The programme will also target innovative entrepreneurs to drive MSME growth, posi-
tively impacting India’s gross domestic product, given the sector’s economic signifi-
cance; and 
(c) 
Cost-effective investments in energy efficiency will generate energy savings that directly 
improve the profitability of MSMEs and provide net positive earnings in the long run. 
Reduced energy costs can free up resources for more productive activities, such as ex-
pansion or developing new product lines or services; and 
23. 
Social and gender co-benefits:  
(a) 
The adoption of climate technologies and best practices will support increased access to 
affordable clean energy and water-efficient technologies, improving the health and well-
being of the Indian population; and  
(b) 
The programme will empower women and ensure their adequate representation as 
stakeholders and beneficiaries. The funding proposal specifies that 20 per cent of the to-
tal annual FMAP lending will be allocated to women. 
24. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the sustainable development potential of FMAP as medium-high.  
1.5 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
           Scale: Medium to high 
25. 
India is experiencing rapid economic growth and entering a crucial phase of energy de-
velopment, which will shape its emissions profile for many years to come. This pivotal moment 
affects both the supply and demand sides of energy. Economic growth is driving increased de-
mand for air conditioning, vehicle purchases and other symbols of rising affluence. Over the 
next two decades, to meet electricity demand, India must expand its power system to a scale 
comparable with that of the European Union. 
26. 
It is essential that this economic growth is fuelled by clean, reliable energy. To achieve 
this, climate-aligned technologies need to be adopted across the economy, particularly by 
MSMEs. MSMEs are significant contributors to GHG emissions (over 110 million t CO2 eq in 
2018), especially in energy-intensive subsectors. Achieving a low-carbon future for India will 
necessitate rapid adoption of low-carbon technologies and improved energy efficiency by 
MSMEs. However, MSMEs often face barriers in accessing finance for green upgrades. There is a 
pressing need for financial products tailored to the unique challenges and risks of the MSME 
sector. 
27. 
Under the Paris Agreement India has committed to reducing emissions intensity by 45 
per cent by 2030 compared with the 2005 level. Meeting these targets will require an estimated 
USD 2.5 trillion, highlighting the significant investment gap, with current green finance flows 
well below the USD 170 billion needed annually. This underscores the critical importance of in-
novative financing and technologies in advancing India’s climate goals. 
28. 
India is also highly vulnerable to climate change, as emphasized in its nationally deter-
mined contribution (NDC), due to its large population, agricultural dependence, extensive 
coastal areas and fragile Himalayan region. India ranks seventh on the Climate Risk Index and 
thirty-first on the INFORM Risk Index, both of which measure climate change vulnerability. The 
funding proposal demonstrates that the MSME sector is particularly vulnerable to extreme 
weather events such as floods, droughts and cyclones, especially MSMEs dependent on agricul-
ture and located in vulnerable regions. This sector faces a number of barriers, such as limited

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 112 
 
 
 
 
access to technology, limited capacity to adopt new technologies and lack of affordable financing 
options. 
29. 
In India, MSMEs need tools and support to assess, manage and forecast climate risks, 
particularly those related to natural hazards (e.g. water scarcity, floods and extreme weather 
events). Investments are needed to make infrastructure more resilient to climate impacts, in-
cluding upgrading facilities and adopting resilient construction practices. 
30. 
The funding proposal describes how FMAP aims to alleviate financing barriers for 
MSMEs’ mitigation and adaptation interventions by providing long-term concessional lending. 
In addition, technical assistance will be provided to MSMEs to increase awareness of available 
climate solutions, addressing market gaps and delivering climate impact projects that are un-
likely to be implemented without FMAP. 
31. 
The iTAP rates the needs of the recipient as medium-high.  
1.6 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
           Scale: Medium to high 
32. 
The FMAP aligns with the implementation of India’s NDC and is consistent with national 
adaptation plans and the national climate strategy. The focus on sectors such as renewable en-
ergy, energy efficiency, transportation, water and agriculture align with high-priority areas 
identified in India’s NDC. The iTAP considers the proposed programme’s emphasis on MSME 
low-carbon development pathways and accelerating climate adaptation efforts to be in harmony 
with India’s climate actions, economic development and financial inclusion strategies. 
33. 
India has issued a non-objection letter for FMAP. In addition, the AE (SIDBI) is an agency 
of the Government of India, with over 30 years of experience working with Indian MSMEs and 
local financial institutions. These factors collectively indicate strong government engagement 
and national ownership. 
34. 
The iTAP finds it commendable that SIDBI will contribute a substantial portion (80 per 
cent) of the finance in the lending facility from its own balance sheet, demonstrating confidence 
in the programme’s potential to achieve its desired outcomes. However, it is disappointing that 
SIDBI’s financial commitment is limited to the loan facility and does not extend to the grant 
component for capacity-building. While SIDBI intends to contribute in-kind assistance (person-
nel) to some capacity-building efforts, the lack of any SIDBI budget at stake in the grant compo-
nent increases the risk of quality control issues over time. When grant facilities rely solely on 
external funding there is a higher risk that managers prioritize fund deployment over ensuring 
the funds achieve the best impact outcomes. 
35. 
The iTAP also notes positively that FMAP builds on SIDBI’s past experiences with direct 
lending to MSMEs and collaboration with PFIs to promote MSMEs’ adoption of green technolo-
gies. This includes providing concessional loans, partnering with NBFCs and offering technical 
assistance to bridge the information gap. We observe approvingly that SIDBI has incorporated 
several lessons learned from these past efforts into FMAP, particularly the requirement for PFIs 
to co-lend alongside FMAP. The iTAP also appreciates that the FMAP development process in-
cluded stakeholder consultations with both potential borrowers and the banking community in 
India. 
36. 
The iTAP considers FMAP to be aligned with host country priorities and assesses in-
volvement on the part of India to be significant. Overall, country ownership of the funding pro-
posal is rated as medium-high.  
1.7 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
           Scale: Medium to high

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 113 
 
 
 
 
37. 
iTAP concurs that fFocusing on transitioning MSMEs to a low-carbon and more resilient 
pathway is an effective strategy, given the overall size of the Indian economy, the central role of 
MSMEs and the rapid changes occurring in India. The proposal’s aim to deliver high-impact miti-
gation and adaptation projects by enhancing the capacity of local financial institutions to pro-
vide climate loans to MSMEs is viewed by iTAP as positive, and greening the existing financial 
systems is an efficient and effective approach. Moreover, FMAP plans to fund green technologies 
in remote/rural areas of India, which can be particularly challenging. To address this, the pro-
gramme’s key feature will be reaching MSMEs in rural areas through indirect lending via local 
financial institutions with a wider presence. This inclusive structure is crucial for developing the 
capacity and sustainability of climate financing in India. 
38. 
Challenges may arise in ensuring that underlying loans deliver the expected climate im-
pacts. This will require the AE to maintain strict screening criteria for its direct loans and effec-
tive oversight of its PFI partners. The iTAP assessed the criteria and processes described in the 
funding proposal and notes that they meet a minimal standard of acceptability but could be im-
proved. In particular, standards are only as effective as their implementation. In this case, SIDBI 
serving as both AE and EE dilutes one of the usual checks on misapplication that the GCF relies 
on, namely the AE acting as the GCF’s fiduciary eyes and ears. The iTAP encourages the Secretar-
iat to consider requesting additional safeguards to ensure that GCF funds and concessionality 
are used effectively, minimizing the risk of merely supporting PFI balance sheets or financing 
interventions without a strong climate rationale. 
39. 
The USD 200 million senior loan provided by GCF will be blended with SIDBI’s USD 800 
million and further leveraged by 16 times through participating local financial institutions, sup-
plemented by USD 377 million in equity at the MSME level, and this is viewed as positive by the 
iTAP. Specifically, the funding proposal states that GCF funding will help SIDBI channel a total 
investment of approximately USD 3.5 billion for mitigation and adaptation measures, resulting 
in a mobilization rate of 1:20.71. This represents significant co-finance by multiple partners, in-
cluding the MSMEs themselves. This structure aligns the interests of all parties with those of the 
GCF. By requiring financial inputs from all relevant parties, FMAP has the potential to scale up 
green financing for MSMEs, delivering climate impact projects. In addition, partnering with local 
financial institutions will leverage existing banking relationships to improve scaling and speed 
of deployment. 
40. 
Funding green technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as risky. While MSMEs can 
borrow from other private financial institutions, the concessionality provided under this pro-
gramme will drive the adoption of green technologies in MSMEs. The GCF funds will enable 
FMAP to offer concessionality as needed, depending on the relevant MSME sector and technol-
ogy. This flexible approach allows lenders to provide financing that is fit for purpose. However, 
there is a risk that over the long tenor of FMAP, the provided concessionality may not keep up 
with market needs. The iTAP encourages the Secretariat and the AE to explore ways to ensure 
that GCF concessionality does not end up over-subsidizing sectors as technologies become more 
accepted and additional financing entrants join the market. As the AE and EE are the same en-
tity, the iTAP suggests having an external process to periodically evaluate the levels of conces-
sionality and the additionality of FMAP funding. This would help mitigate the risk of crowding 
out other funders, or of PFIs capturing windfall profits without corresponding climate benefits. 
41. 
The iTAP agrees on the effectiveness of combining a grant component with the lending 
programme to provide technical assistance and capacity-building for both MSMEs and partici-
pating financial institutions, and the grant programme is well-sized and focused on providing 
technology assessments for MSMEs. These assessments can increase awareness of climate tech-
nologies and dispel outdated perceptions about the costs and effectiveness of many clean tech-
nologies. Capacity support for participating financial institutions, particularly underserved 
NBFCs, MFIs and SFBs, is also crucial in increasing their ability to extend climate finance.

=== PAGE 114 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 114 
 
 
 
 
42. 
Overall, the iTAP rates the efficiency and effectiveness of this programme as medium-
high.  
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
43. 
The iTAP considers that the FMAP programme aligns closely with the GCF private sector 
strategy and overall mission of supporting developing countries to pursue growth pathways 
that are aligned with a low-carbon and resilient future for the planet.  
44. 
If executed well, FMAP will facilitate access to capital for vulnerable MSMEs and create 
incentives for these businesses to adopt climate-aligned technologies and processes. Im-
portantly, FMAP will also aim to green the existing financial system by building capacity and es-
tablishing a track record for PFIs in financing mitigation and adaptation investments among the 
most vulnerable economic segments. In doing so, FMAP should mobilize large-scale funds from 
these local financial institutions by de-risking private sector investments and supporting the 
greening of the financial system. 
45. 
The iTAP considers that the concessional loans and technical assistance provided 
through FMAP can play a pivotal role in reducing the costs and risks – both real and perceived – 
associated with investments in climate change mitigation and adaptation. SIDBI has long been a 
market maker for MSMEs in India and has comprehensive access to the MSME landscape, in-
cluding those not significantly serviced by traditional lenders. The iTAP notes that SIDBI is well-
positioned to be catalytic across MSME segments for both mitigation and adaptation activities. 
46. 
Significantly, the commitment of GCF funding will be complemented by substantial fi-
nancial support from SIDBI, signalling that this programme is well-suited for the Indian context. 
The programme also incorporates co-finance obligations from PFIs and the MSMEs themselves 
to ensure proper buy-in from all key actors. At the heart of FMAP is the recognition that ad-
dressing the climate finance challenge in larger and middle-income countries is best achieved by 
catalysing a green shift in local financial markets, rather than relying on cross-border funding. 
Local finance can operate more efficiently due to a better understanding of localized implemen-
tation risks, legal frameworks and market challenges, and doing all this in the right currency. In 
any investment analysis, the journey from perceived risk to real risk understanding is much 
shorter for local financial actors.  
47. 
However, the iTAP has noted some areas of concern, primarily related to the operation-
alization of FMAP over time. The FMAP concept is strong, but it relies heavily on SIDBI’s capac-
ity to underwrite loans and oversee participating PFIs’ underlying loans to achieve the target 
mitigation and adaptation impacts. Areas of FMAP that can be strengthened are noted through-
out this assessment. FMAP may face challenges associated with pipeline risks and MSMEs’ will-
ingness to adopt climate-resilient technologies and solutions. In such circumstances, there could 
be a temptation to loosen climate screening standards or oversight of PFI portfolios. It is im-
portant to consider additional safeguards in the operational plan to mitigate this risk and up-
hold the climate imperative of FMAP. 
48. 
Considering the foregoing considerations, the iTAP recommends that the Board approve 
this funding proposal.

GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01

Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical 
Advisory Panel's assessment (FP241) 
Proposal name: 
Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in Indian 
MSMEs 
Accredited entity: 
Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) 
Country/(ies): 
India 
Project/Programme size: 
Large  
 
AE duly notes the comments of iTAP and appreciates the efforts of the team in reviewing the 
Funding Proposal package in detail. The comprehensive feedback would help the AE in further 
strengthening the proposal and programme. It may be noted by the iTAP that the Funding 
Proposal has undergone significant revisions as suggested by the secretariat and most of the 
concerns/feedback/recommendation given by the iTAP members has already been factored in 
the FP package. However, AE acknowledges the valuable feedback and would like to give below 
clause wise explanation on the comments by iTAP. 
Impact potential 
iTAPs feedback (Point 8)-: FMAP will provide loans to MSMEs targeting the renewable 
energy, energy efficiency and low-emission transport sectors. In addition, it will support 
adaptation by helping MSMEs in the water-intensive and agriculture sectors to implement 
climate solutions in their businesses, thereby increasing resilience and adaptive capacity 
against climate change. Adaptation investments will be directed to highly vulnerable regions 
such as coastal areas, the Himalayas and the eastern belt of India. According to the funding 
proposal, FMAP is expected to avoid 43.86 million t CO2 eq and benefit 10.3 million people. 
The iTAP notes that estimating GHG reductions over such a large programme and long tenor 
(13 years) is challenging due to uncertainties in the composition of underlying loans. It is 
therefore difficult to put very much stock on specific projections in the context of FMAP; 
however, the iTAP acknowledges that the targeted loan areas (e.g. energy efficiency, low-
emission transport, renewables) coupled with climate-aligned screening mechanisms mean 
that there is the potential for significant impact. Importantly, the sheer scale of India and the 
role of MSMEs in the economy support an argument that the overall impact will be positive 
from a climate standpoint. 
 
SIDBI Response: 
While estimating the GHG emissions, AE has considered the following:  
(i) 
AE has used the approved methodologies (CDM and SEC) for calculation 
for energy savings and GHG emissions of different sub-sectors. 
(ii) 
AE has experience of working in the area of Energy Efficiency in MSME 
since 2005-06. A technology list comprises of 891 clean, green, energy 
efficient technologies have been developed with the support of technical 
expert agencies, Bureau of Energy Efficiency etc., AE has used the data 
from the projects assisted in the past, by AE for the baseline calculations.

=== PAGE 116 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 116 
 
 
 
However, considering the various limitations as well as vast heterogeneity of the MSME 
sector, AE agrees with the observations of the iTAP that although realistic estimations of GHG 
emissions is difficult for such a long tenor but the programme has a huge potential for 
significant impact on the most vulnerable segment i.e MSMEs.   
 
iTAPs feedback (Point 12)- FMAP intends not only to increase the availability of finance for 
climate-related loans to MSME but also to use concessionality to accelerate the transition to 
climate-friendly technologies. The AE has made a credible case for the levels of 
concessionality needed to shift PFIs towards climate-aligned sectors and, more importantly, 
to encourage MSMEs to adopt technology changes that enhance their resilience to climate 
impacts and reduce GHG emissions. However, the iTAP notes a concern over how levels of 
concessionality will be determined over time. The iTAP recommends that SIDBI regularly 
evaluate and adjust concessionality on a sector-by-sector basis to avoid over-subsidization, 
which could generate windfall gains for some PFIs while crowding out other players.  
 
SIDBI Response-  
The concerns regarding determination of levels of concessionality and passing of the same 
over a period is duly noted and the explanation is as follows:  
(a). The concessionality provided by GCF on blended basis shall be passed on to the 
borrowers (MSMEs as well as PFIs) by AE by way of reducing the spread by atleast an amount 
equal to the concessionality worked out on blended basis towards each tranche of drawal 
from GCF. Further, AE shall suitably bind the PFIs to pass on the concessionality on a blended 
basis to their end borrowers. 
(b). AE duly notes the suggestions of iTAP regarding the necessity to evaluate and adjust 
concessionality to avoid over-subsidization. In this regard, AE submits that all the sub-loans 
will undergo credit rating and the effective interest rate for the end borrower would be the 
interest rate based on the credit rating of the borrower. The credit rating parameters include 
various factors on obligor and facility, as well as sectoral outlook. 
(c). The programme has also inbuilt suitable checks and balances which includes M&E on 
sample basis of sub-loans, periodical reporting of the climate benefits, pricing and passing on 
the concessionality to the ultimate borrowers etc., to avoid over-subsidisation or PFIs getting 
windfall profits without any climate benefits. The programme proposes to work not with one 
two PFIs but with around 20 to 25 PFIs and it is expected that over the period gradually there 
will be sufficient competition among these PFIs for climate financing. This would obviate the 
risk of windfall profits to the PFIs and shall ensure further benefits to the ultimate borrowers 
i.e. MSMEs.  
 
iTAPs feedback (Point 13)- In addition, the iTAP has some qualms concerning the risk that a 
substantial portion of FMAP funding could end up refinancing existing PFI portfolio loans 
instead of financing new and additional MSME efforts to adopt climate-friendly technology. 
There is very little climate-directed utility in using GCF concessionality to refinance 
mitigation efforts that have already been funded commercially. SIDBI has indicated that it 
may wish to fund some portfolio refinance in the early stages of the programme as a way to 
attract PFI partners and facilitate their co-financing capacity. The iTAP is willing to accept this 
argument but strongly recommends that the Secretariat and SIDBI develop safeguards to 
ensure that the bulk of concessional funding is truly for additional projects. The iTAP

=== PAGE 117 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 117 
 
 
 
encourages SIDBI to develop a plan for FMAP that minimizes portfolio refinancing over time 
on a trajectory to zero over the programme’s tenure. 
 
SIDBI Response- AE wish to state that iTAP concerns are addressed in the revised Funding 
Proposal and the Term Sheet. Further, AE would like to supplement with the following w.r.t 
refinancing of existing portfolio: 
(A) The programme will not cover any sub-loans prior to the date of effectiveness 
of FAA.  
(B) The Programme will offer onlending/ refinance support against eligible assets 
created by the empanelled PFIs and originated no longer than six months 
prior to approval from the Executing Entity for financing in accordance with 
FAA. The concerned PFI would be required to submit (for Refinance) all the 
details, copy of documentation, etc. to satisfy AE that the conditions stipulated 
in the term sheet at the time of sanction have been fully complied with.  
The above will ensure that only new/additional projects (which were not already support by 
PFIs before launch of the programme) are covered under the programme. 
 
Paradigm shift potential 
AE acknowledges the positive feedback from iTAP on the paradigm shift potential of the 
FMAP proposal.  
 
Sustainable development potential 
AE acknowledges the positive feedback from iTAP on the sustainable development potential 
of the FMAP proposal. 
 
Needs of the recipient 
AE duly notes and appreciates that iTAP recognises the objectives of the FMAP programme in 
alleviating the financing barriers of MSMEs in adopting mitigation and adaptation 
interventions.  
 
Country ownership 
iTAP feedback (Point 34)- The iTAP finds it commendable that SIDBI will contribute a 
substantial portion (80 per cent) of the finance in the lending facility from its own balance 
sheet, demonstrating confidence in the programme’s potential to achieve its desired 
outcomes. However, it is disappointing that SIDBI’s financial commitment is limited to 
the loan facility and does not extend to the grant component for capacity-building. 
While SIDBI intends to contribute in-kind assistance (personnel) to some capacity-
building efforts, the lack of any SIDBI budget at stake in the grant component increases 
the risk of quality control issues over time. When grant facilities rely solely on external

=== PAGE 118 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 118 
 
 
 
funding there is a higher risk that managers prioritize fund deployment over ensuring 
the funds achieve the best impact outcomes. 
 
SIDBI Response- It is worth noting that iTAP acknowledges that the FMAP aligns with the 
implementation of India’s NDC and is consistent with national adaptation plans and the 
national climate strategy. AE appreciates the positive feedback received in this section.  
In this regard, AE wish to submit the following: 
(i) 
AE is ensuring 16X leverage of the funds contributed by the GCF. 
(ii) 
AE is taking the credit risk on NBFCs, SFBs, MFIs as well as the MSMEs. 
GCF is taking credit risk on SIDBI which is a AAA rated quasi sovereign 
entity.  
(iii) 
It may be noted that the TA supported by GCF has been estimated on a highly 
conservative basis and it is not even 2% of the total cost i.e. USD 400 Mn (around 
10% of total climate financing expected, USD 4 Bn) which will be incurred by the 
EE (SIDBI) as well as the PFIs towards manpower and administrative costs 
towards deployment of the climate loans. 
 
As regards comment on prioritising fund deployment of the donor, AE may like to mention 
again that AE has managed several technical assistance(s) which were part of a project 
involving LOC for being channelised and TA for capacity building. There has been only 
counterpart contribution in-kind (from SIDBI) and still AE has achieved the intended 
outcome set by the donor(s). 
 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
iTAP feedback (Point 38)-  Challenges may arise in ensuring that underlying loans deliver 
the expected climate impacts. This will require the AE to maintain strict screening criteria for 
its direct loans and effective oversight of its PFI partners. The iTAP assessed the criteria and 
processes described in the funding proposal and notes that they meet a minimal standard of 
acceptability but could be improved. In particular, standards are only as effective as their 
implementation. In this case, SIDBI serving as both AE and EE dilutes one of the usual checks 
on misapplication that the GCF relies on, namely the AE acting as the GCF’s fiduciary eyes and 
ears. The iTAP encourages the Secretariat to consider requesting additional safeguards to 
ensure that GCF funds and concessionality are used effectively, minimizing the risk of merely 
supporting PFI balance sheets or financing interventions without a strong climate rationale. 
 
SIDBI Response- SIDBI duly acknowledges iTAP’s comment in maintaining strict screening 
criteria. In this regard, we would like to highlight the Funding Proposal has been revised to 
accommodate the following: 
1. A comprehensive eligibility criteria and investment process for the mitigation 
and adaptation technologies that would be financed has been laid down and 
the compliance of the same shall be ensured through a robust MRV 
mechanism for both direct as well as indirect lending.  
 
2. The revised Funding Proposal also has details of the loan approval process in 
which at the very initial stage, due diligence related to technical viability, 
climate impact, ESS due diligence would be checked by dedicated professional

=== PAGE 119 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 119 
 
 
 
at the branch level and at the PMU level. For the PFIs, the required check to 
factor in climate criteria is placed at the very first stage of the loan approval 
which details out the condition for empanelment which necessitates that the 
PFIs which have interest in climate financing and are willing to adopt new 
systems processes would be empanelled.  
 
SIDBI’s total balance sheet size is around USD 57 billion (FY 2024). Out of the above around 
90% approximately USD 53.4 billion is towards providing on-lending/ Refinance support to 
50 Banks, 78 NBFCs, 41 SFB/MFIs. This depicts SIDBI’s strong relationship/ operating 
capabilities with Financial Institutions. Further, with regard to climate financing, SIDBI has 
been actively pursuing energy efficiency financing in MSMEs since 2005 -06 and have 
channelised around USD 2 billion to over 16,000+ MSMEs.  The present outstanding of direct 
green loans is USD 850 million as on March 31, 2024. Hence, AE has strong capacity to 
underwrite loans as well as to encourage MSMEs and the PFIs to participate in the 
programme. 
 
AE again reassure the achievements of envisaged impact of the programme through our 
development and credit plus engagement. This is validated by AE’s continued top ranking on 
multilateral supported programmes. It may be mentioned that in many such multilaterals 
programmes, SIDBI has been playing both the roles i.e AE as well as EE. 
 
iTAP feedback (Point 40)-  Funding green technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as 
risky. While MSMEs can borrow from other private financial institutions, the concessionality 
provided under this programme will drive the adoption of green technologies in MSMEs. The 
GCF funds will enable FMAP to offer concessionality as needed, depending on the relevant 
MSME sector and technology. This flexible approach allows lenders to provide financing that 
is fit for purpose. However, there is a risk that over the long tenor of FMAP, the provided 
concessionality may not keep up with market needs. The iTAP encourages the Secretariat and 
the AE to explore ways to ensure that GCF concessionality does not end up over-subsidizing 
sectors as technologies become more accepted and additional financing entrants join the 
market. As the AE and EE are the same entity, the iTAP suggests having an external process to 
periodically evaluate the levels of concessionality and the additionality of FMAP funding. This 
would help mitigate the risk of crowding out other funders, or of PFIs capturing windfall 
profits without corresponding climate benefits. 
 
SIDBI Response- The programme has been designed on the blended finance concept to offer 
concessional loans to MSMEs either directly or indirectly through PFIs (NBFCs/SFBs/MFIs). 
The programme envisages a leveraging of more than 16x of the GCF support by way of 
concessional loan. The programme shall create an ecosystem for financing climate 
technologies in order to ensure that results are achieved at a scale. Through this, the 
programme intends to encourage the PFIs to create an ecosystem of climate financing which 
would allow them to lend to the MSMEs with an increased understanding of the associated 
risks and underwriting capabilities required. SIDBI’s direct lending to the MSMEs sets an 
example for the PFIs to follow to successfully lend to the MSMEs for adopting low emission 
climate resilient technologies.

=== PAGE 120 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 120 
 
 
 
This will be achieved by : 
(i). Offering concessional finance to MSMEs directly encouraging them to adopt relevant 
climate resilient technologies and interventions. However, at the same time minimum energy 
savings of 10% will be ensured on a sub-loans basis and 20% at the programme level.  
(ii). Offering concessional lines of capital and blended financial products to FIs which will 
help in reducing their risk of lending towards MSMEs thus encouraging them to offer 
additional financing. 
 
The programme has also inbuilt suitable checks and balances which includes M&E on sample 
basis of sub-loans, periodical reporting of the climate benefits and passing on the 
concessionality to the ultimate borrowers etc., to avoid over-subsidisation or PFIs getting 
windfall profits without any climate benefits. The programme proposes to work not with one 
two PFIs but with around 20 to 25 PFIs and it is expected that over the period gradually there 
will be sufficient competition among these PFIs for climate financing. This would obviate the 
risk of windfall profits to the PFIs and shall ensure further benefits to the ultimate borrowers 
i.e. MSMEs.  
 
As regard independent evaluation, it may be mentioned that the programme has suitable 
provisions for interim and final evaluations through independent third-party agencies. 
 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel: 
 
iTAPs feedback (Point 47)- However, the iTAP has noted some areas of concern, primarily 
related to the operationalization of FMAP over time. The FMAP concept is strong, but it relies 
heavily on SIDBI’s capacity to underwrite loans and oversee participating PFIs’ underlying 
loans to achieve the target mitigation and adaptation impacts. Areas of FMAP that can be 
strengthened are noted throughout this assessment. FMAP may face challenges associated 
with pipeline risks and MSMEs’ willingness to adopt climate-resilient technologies and 
solutions. In such circumstances, there could be a temptation to loosen climate screening 
standards or oversight of PFI portfolios. It is important to consider additional safeguards in 
the operational plan to mitigate this risk and uphold the climate imperative of FMAP. 
 
SIDBI response- SIDBI submits the following: 
1. SIDBI’s total balance sheet size is around USD 57 billion (FY 2024). Out of the above 
around 90% approximately USD 53.4 billion is towards providing on-lending/ 
Refinance support to 50 Banks, 78 NBFCs, 41 SFB/MFIs. This depicts SIDBI’s strong 
relationship/ operating capabilities with Financial Institutions. Further, with regard 
to climate financing, SIDBI has been actively pursuing energy efficiency financing in 
MSMEs since 2005 -06 and have channelised around USD 2 billion to over 16,000+ 
MSMEs.  The present outstanding of direct green loans is USD 850 million as on March 
31, 2024. Hence, AE has strong capacity to underwrite loans as well as to encourage 
MSMEs and the PFIs to participate in the programme. 
 
2. It may be mentioned that there are more than 63 million MSMEs in India and the 
programme is targeting only around 10,000 MSMEs. Notwithstanding the above, we

=== PAGE 121 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.16/Rev.01 
Page 121 
 
 
 
fully concur with the views of the iTAP regarding willingness of MSMEs to adopt 
climate -resilient technologies and solutions.  Accordingly, the programme envisages 
to provide suitable technical handholding, capacity building, awareness as well as 
other TA activities to encourage MSMEs to participate in the programme. The 
concessionality will also play a major role in encouraging MSMEs to adopt climate- 
resilient technologies.

1. 
GENDER ANALYSIS & ASSESSMENT STUDY 
1.1 
INTRODUCTION 
 
In the past few years, India has been increasingly faced with the challenge of mitigating 
the detriments of and adapting to the adverse impact of climate change and balancing 
the same with the need of economic growth. While the ever-increasing demand of 
energy resources, energy systems and infrastructure, concomitant with the needs of 
a vast and rapidly developing country, climate related vulnerabilities are now in 
desperate need of mitigation and adaptation actions.  
Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (“MSMEs”), forming a vital part of the Indian 
economy and playing a critical role in the sector of employment generation in the 
country, today face considerable operational challenges, energy costs being one of 
the key causes. Challenges such as insufficient infrastructure, limited access to credit 
and investments, as well as limited financial, technical knowledge and capacities 
further render MSMEs vulnerable to adverse impacts of climate change. 
It is in this context that SIDBI, India proposes to address such deficiencies, through a 
programme named ‘Financing Mitigation & Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in 
MSMEs’, that is aimed at facilitating MSMEs to become more climate resilient, 
sustainable and competitive in the long run.” (“Programme”) This programme will be 
upscale ongoing projects, through affordable and accessible finance and technical 
support for projects pertaining to climate change mitigation and adaptation 
projects.  
Among other aspects, the design of the programme takes into consideration, the 
existing gender issues, and concerns, in conformity with international norms and 
national priorities.  
 
1.1.1 RATIONALE OF THE ASSESSMENT 
 
The Gender Assessment will help to understand relationship between men and 
women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative 
to each other. 
 
The objective of the present document is to showcase findings basis a Gender 
Assessment Study and formulate a Gender Action Plan for the Programme, with 
respect to its’ targeted sectors. The projects in MSMEs under this Programme 
would 
be 
related 
to 
mitigation 
specific 
interventions 
such 
as 
Energy 
Efficiency/Technology upgradation, Electric Mobility, Renewable Energy (including 
Solar). Further, the interventions will also include adaptation specific interventions

=== PAGE 126 ===
2 
 
across a variety of themes including Climate Smart technologies, Water, Wastewater, 
Innovative Technologies etc. 
The scope of work under the Programme entails, conducting a gender assessment 
study, preparing a gender action plan, as well identifying the gender impact and 
benefits accruing from the said programme.  
 
1.1.2 METHODOLOGY NOTES 
The assessment and the action plan under the Programme is prepared in alignment 
with the Green Climate Fund (“GCF”) Gender Policy, in consonance with the 
instructions provided by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate 
Change(“UNFCCC”), the Sustainable Development Goals (“SDGs”) and other related 
international agreements and normative frameworks. The exercise of acknowledging 
disproportionate gender impact, aids in examining the inter-sectional constraints and 
issues prevalent in the national and sectoral context; and thereby aims to incorporate 
a course to enhance the ‘agency’ of women in the change process (in contributing to 
mitigation and adaptation activities, enhancing resilience and well-being, supporting 
livelihoods in volume and diversity, etc).1 
 
It is to be noted that GCF has placed gender perspectives as a “key element of its’ 
programming architecture” and deems it to Be an “essential decision-making element 
for the deployment of its resources” towards a thoroughly “gender-responsive” 
approach for its projects/ programmes. 
 
In preparing the Gender Action Plan for the Programme, following approaches have 
been leveraged: 
1. Baseline assessment of participation of women in the MSME sector in 
India as entrepreneurs 
a. The literature reviewed from secondary sources to understand the 
baseline situation and the broad scope for EE applications in the sector 
considering gender dimensions. In this regard, relevant reports, about 
national policies and guidelines (environmental, social, gender equality), 
historical studies and research on Energy Efficiency (EE), international 
best practices, potential EE interventions etc. considering gender equity 
were analysed. SIDBI’s Gender Policy attached at Appendix-II. 
b. This assessment has been done based on SIDBI’s existing initiatives to 
promote women entrepreneurship and based on research studies 
supported by SIDBI. 
c. SIDBI in collaboration with Dun and Bradstreet introduced a quarterly 
Sustainability Perception Index (SIDBI - D&B SPeX)2 - the green pulse 
 
1 UNWomen and Green Climate Fund, (2017), Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects, available at

2 https://www.sidbi.in/spex

indicator. SPeX is a pioneering effort to assess the sustainability 
perceptions of MSMEs across various industries and locations. The 
index is designed to provide a comprehensive overview of the

2. Assessment of challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in the MSME 
sector 
a. Stakeholder consultation i.e. “Strengthening Green Financing and

gender policies 
 
The above assessments feed into the Gender Action Plan proposed for the 
Programme and the specific outcomes from the Gender Action Plan for the 
Programme.  
 
1.2 
BACKGROUND AND CONTEXT: GENDER IN INDIA 
 
Gender and Employment in India – Data Trends 
There remains no doubt in the growing potential of women in terms of their economic 
participation. It is to be noted that the difference between the overall literacy rate for 
adult males and females aged 15 and above have significantly reduced, with the 
current participation of women estimated at 65.79 per cent of the total female 
population, against the male population comprising of 82.37 per cent.  
 
However, it is pertinent to note that there was a significant disproportionate gender 
impact because of the COVID - 19 pandemic, in sectors of labour, employment, 
financial inclusion and with reference to other socio-economic considerations. In the 
 
3 https://www.gifsinitiative.in/ 
4 https://grownetwork.in/

=== PAGE 128 ===
4 
 
global context, the WEF Gender Gap Report 20215(“WEF Report 2021") highlighted 
the following pointers: 
1. 5 per cent of all employed women lost their jobs, compared with 3.9 per cent of 
employed men (ILO projection)  
2. marked decline of women’s hiring into leadership roles across multiple industries6 
3. increase in care work for women. 
4. automation, digitalization as compounding factors in ‘labour market disruption’ – 
with implications for ‘gender parity in further of jobs’ and ‘occupational gender 
segregation  
 
The WEF Report 2021 further identifies the increased likeliness of gender gaps in 
sectors that require disruptive technical skills. Further, in fields where women are 
under-represented, they are likely to face discrimination and other institutional or social 
barriers in job transition, particularly in emerging areas of growth potential, with 
widening disparities in access to resources and capacities.  
In case of India, reports furnished by UN Women (2021)7 and Centre for Sustainable 
Employment, Azim Premji University (2021)8 point to certain alarming trends, that 
threaten the national and international investments in gender empowerment and long-
term gains in this regard. The latter recognizes the impact of the pandemic, rendering 
a large section of the workforce without access to employment or social protection. 
These reports also identify the increased informality and decline in earnings for 
majority of workers, with women and younger workers disproportionately affected, 
because of the pandemic. 
For women, it is observed that wage inequality and the burden of unpaid care has 
pushed more women out of employment falling into depths of poverty, with women 
losing jobs during COVID-19, and therein engaging more in unpaid care work, both in 
India and globally.  
 
This section attempts to provide an overview of gender related workforce allocation 
vis a vis the MSME sector in India. Given the scope of the present study, the focal 
point of this sections is to furnish national level data, in respect to the participation of 
women as workforce in the economy and the current scenario of women workforce 
and ownership vis a vis the MSME sector.

• Based on type & scale of organization13: Most women-owned enterprises in

enterprises decreases. There is a need to increase the share of women-owned

1.4 CHALLENGES RELATED TO WOMEN ENTREPRENEURSHIP IN 
MSME SECTOR IN INDIA 
 
The MSME sector in India contributes to ~30% of India’s GDP and employs around 
110 million people. In India, about 89% of the women-owned enterprises operate all 
year round, 9%are seasonal, and the remaining 2% operate casually, which are 
neither perennial nor seasonal18. 
 
The stakeholder consultation highlights the efforts needed to support the greening of 
Indian financial systems and the role of MSMEs in initiatives scaling up of renewables, 
energy efficiency, electric vehicles and sustainable business. The event brings 
together stakeholders from different sectors to share learnings and drive sustainable 
practices. The consultation also focuses on promoting women's participation in the 
green and climate finance sector. Women entrepreneurs share their experiences and 
insights in sustainable business, highlighting the challenges they face and the potential 
solutions. The experts also deliberated on challenges faced by sustainable businesses 
in scaling their products, the need for government support, and the importance of user-
friendly technology and clear communication for making sustainable technologies 
commercially viable. 
 
Following are some of the key challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in India.  
 
• Not Sustainable: ~73% women-led enterprises have faced a reduction in 
revenues post COVID-19. Most female entrepreneurs are concentrated in 
sectors and industries highly affected by the pandemic and digitization like 
micro and small businesses in non-professional services, such as tailor shop 
owners, dressmakers, shopkeepers and street vendors, arts and handicrafts 
vendors, and beauty-parlor owners, among others. This points to the fact that 
women led businesses are not financially sustainable/resilient enough to 
recover from external shocks.  
• Not Autonomous: In ~20% cases, the business-related decisions are taken 
by the respective spouses and not the women entrepreneur running the 
business. Often, women do not truly own or control many women-led 
enterprises. They depend considerably on men for key business 
decisions, procurement of raw materials, pricing information, and market 
access. 
• Not Formal: ~95.6% of the women entrepreneurs in India are informal and not 
registered with relevant national/state institutions. A high level of informality 
harms the growth and resilience of their enterprises and their entrepreneurial 
rights. The level of effort involved in running such enterprises go unaccounted 
 
18 Niti Aayog – “Decoding support to women entrepreneurs in india” - 2023

=== PAGE 133 ===
9 
 
for and expose women to occupational health hazards. Further, informality 
leads to challenges in accessing finance, market, etc.   
• Not sufficient employment generating: ~95% of women proprietors run with 
less than six workers, most being own-account or single person enterprises. 
Only ~1.9% proprietors run with six to 10 employees. This points to the fact 
that women entrepreneurs often lack skills in scaling businesses i.e. in 
areas such as business management, market access, access to finance, 
etc. 
• Lack of awareness and skills on green jobs and technologies:  Women in 
Indian MSMEs have been confined to low-paying, low-skilled jobs. The ongoing 
transitions present them with an opportunity to realign their skill sets and adjust 
to the evolving nature of work. Digital & Green Technologies proficiency is one 
of the most important skills that women need to acquire to keep pace with the 
ongoing transitions. This will enable them to enter traditionally male-dominated 
sectors that rely heavily on digital technologies, such as healthcare services, 
automobiles, and more advanced roles within the textiles sector with green 
technologies.  
 
Currently, there is limited evidence on how climate change affects women in the 
MSME sector. The impact of these disruptions presents both a challenge and an 
opportunity for mainstreaming gender inclusion and empowerment through the 
proposed project.  
 
1.5 
GENDER LENS ON MSMES AND CLIMATE (MITIGATION AND 
ADAPTATION) 
 
The role of women entrepreneurs in driving inclusive economic growth cannot be ruled 
out. Women entrepreneurs not only generate income for themselves and their families 
but also reinvest in their communities, thereby creating a ripple effect of positive 
socioeconomic impact. By fostering entrepreneurship opportunities for women, we can 
reduce gender disparities in employment and help alleviate poverty. Targeted 
interventions to address the specific needs of women in different regions and sectors, 
especially in clean energy and climate-resilient agriculture, would be the best next 
step. 
 
Women entrepreneurs are not just agents of change; they are the architects of a 
sustainable future. To create a more inclusive, equitable, and sustainable future for 
generations to come, women-led endeavours must be supported. Further, an enabling 
environment should be created for women to flourish and share their learning’s and 
insights while tackling climate change.  
 
A recent report for “improving women’s productivity and incomes” through 
energy/ climate initiatives has also been conducted in 2021 by the Council on

=== PAGE 134 ===
10 
 
Energy, Environment and Water.19 Citing a direct link or contribution to SDGs 5, 7 and 
8, women’s engagement as producers, customers, and suppliers in this area is seen 
to have potential for expanding markets and achieving scale. 
 
Opportunities: 
 
In order to address women’s specific and systemic vulnerabilities, there is a need for 
collective efforts that support women in the sector.  
• There is a need for national and state level governments to ensure effective 
skill training in addition to training provided by the employer.  
• Drawing lessons from past experiences: Women are also more susceptible 
to economic fluctuations and other shocks that affect the MSME sector. For 
Instance, during the first lockdown in 2020, only 7 per cent of men lost their 
jobs, compared to 47 per cent of women who lost their jobs and did not return 
to work by the end of the year. In the informal sector, women fared even worse. 
Between March and April 2021, rural Indian women in informal jobs accounted 
for 80 per cent of job losses.” 
 “Indian women also spend more time doing unpaid care work at home 
than men. On an average, they spend 9.8 times more time than men on 
unpaid domestic chores and 4.5 hours a day caring for children, elders 
and the sick. During the pandemic, their share of unpaid care work grew 
by nearly 30 per cent.” Centre for Sustainable Employment, State of 
Working India (2021)  
 “More girls than boys were left out of school during the pandemic and 65 
per cent of parents surveyed were reluctant to continue the education of 
girls and resorting to child marriages to save costs. This can create an 
entire generation of young women without education and employment 
opportunities.” UN Women (2021) 
 
As women-led businesses continue to recover from the pandemic-induced 
shocks, there is an emerging need to adopt cleaner energy technologies, 
digitalization, and other transitions in the value chain. As these transitions 
necessitate newer and more specialized skills, they further compound the 
existing challenges that women in MSMEs currently face. 
 
• New skills and training for women in green jobs: Women are more inclined 
to embrace technologies and mechanisms that offer cost and energy savings, 
leading to increased cash flows. It is imperative to develop suitable business 
cases and models to promote the adoption of renewable technologies, such as 
solar energy and biogas. There is, therefore, a need to make such innovations 
 
19 Improving Women’s Productivity and Incomes Through Clean Energy in India Sasmita Patnaik, Shaily Jha, and Tanvi Jain, CEEW Report 
July 2021, https://www.ceew.in/sites/default/files/Improving-Womens-Incomes-and-Productivity-through-Clean-Energy-in-India.pdf

=== PAGE 135 ===
11 
 
accessible to female entrepreneurs through training and research provisions. It 
is crucial to educate women about the long-term implications of climate change 
for their businesses and highlight the potential opportunities arising from the 
adoption of clean energy and climate-resilient practices. Additionally, on-the-
job training programs should be designed to develop relevant skill sets among 
female workers. These programs should include modules on financial 
management, digital and information technology, leadership, and management. 
This approach will not only support female workers in making informed choices 
when selecting from various career paths but also contribute to their 
professional development and decision-making capabilities.  
• Establishing Institutional support: Women workers and entrepreneurs may 
find themselves in the minority as they strive to enter roles and sectors 
traditionally dominated by men. Ensuring that women are not left behind during 
the low-carbon transition and placing them at the forefront of climate adaptation 
and resilience require comprehensive support at all levels. Creating 
opportunities for women requires innovative strategies and policies that 
effectively address social, cultural, and economic biases. The formation of 
networks of female workers that provide mutual support and training for new 
and multiple roles is vital. Even as women enter sectors such as aviation, 
engineering, and construction, they require targeted support and skills for 
technical roles where they are currently underrepresented.  
• Gender-responsive financing for MSMEs: Access to credit is a primary 
barrier for women in MSMEs. Improving access to formal and affordable 
financial services can serve as a catalyst in supporting women during the 
transition to clean energy and climate change. The financial inclusion of women 
can lead to social and economic empowerment by improving access to a range 
of financial products, including savings, credit, insurance, and payment 
services, and by building financial awareness. Most women-owned MSMEs 
operate as unregistered enterprises and continue to face issues in accessing 
formal financing to meet their business requirements. The credit needs of 
women entrepreneurs remain largely unmet owing to social, economic, cultural, 
and legal factors. 
• Promote decent and dignified workplaces for women: Business 
sustainability involves striking a balance between social, economic, and 
environmental considerations. From a gender perspective, this implies 
providing conducive and safe working conditions, access to resources, training, 
and capacity building for female workers. To ensure comprehensive support for 
women across different life stages, including marriage and motherhood, there 
is a need for innovative and flexible mechanisms that facilitate and encourage 
an effective return to work. 
 
The current mechanisms for accessing climate funds offer ad hoc solutions. There is 
a need to move towards systems approaches that provide financial assistance and

=== PAGE 136 ===
12 
 
offer support to entrepreneurs in sectors undergoing multiple transitions, such as low-
carbon and technological transitions. It is crucial to consider systemic benefits and 
institutional mechanisms for a just transition. 
 
In this regard, encouraging women entrepreneurs in the “green” economy represents 
an opportunity to make women-led enterprises more resilient and sustainable, thus 
enabling a “just” transition through FMAP. 
 
1.6 
INITIATIVES TAKEN BY GOVERNMENT TO PROMOTE & SUPPORT 
WOMEN ENTREPRENEURSHIP IN MSME SECTOR IN INDIA 
 
There are several Gender focused legal regulations in India, that aim to address the 
vulnerabilities and cultural biases associated with gender, through the provision of 
certain measures that support a safe and equal professional platform for women. 
These regulations are presented below.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The G20 New Delhi Leaders’ Declaration 2023 focuses on “Enhancing Economic 
and Social Empowerment,” “Bridging the Gender Digital Divide,” “Driving Gender 
Inclusive Climate Action,” “Securing Women’s Food Security, Nutrition, and Well-
Being, Creation of a Working Group on the Empowerment of Women. 
 
The Government of India had launched programmes like Beti Padhao, Beti Bachao 
campaign (launched in 2015) for improving the Gender Parity Index (GPI) 20  by 
promoting survival, protection and education of girl child. The schemes aim to address 
the issue of declining Child Sex Ratio through a mass campaign targeted at changing 
social mind set and creating awareness about the criticality of the issue. Furthermore, 
Mahila E-Haat has been launched by the Government in 2016 for promoting women 
entrepreneurs, with the objective to provide an e-marketing platform by leveraging 
technology 
for 
showcasing 
product 
made/manufactured/sold 
by 
women 
entrepreneurs/SHGs/NGOs. 
 
20 Gender Parity Index (GPI) in education is a valuable indicator which reflects the discrimination against girls in

provides for payment of equal 
remuneration to men and women 
workers for the same work of similar 
nature without any discrimination. In 
order to ensure social security to the 
workers including women in the

Further, The Ministry of MSME, Government of India has taken a number of steps 
to promote and support women-owned MSMEs21 
1. 
Special drives have been undertaken for registration of women owned MSMEs 
under the URP. 
2. 
In order to endorse women entrepreneurs, the Public Procurement Policy was 
amended in 2018 mandating Central Ministries/Departments/ Undertakings to 
procure at least 3 per cent of the annual procurement from women-owned 
MSMEs. 
3. 
To further support women entrepreneurs under the Credit Guarantee Scheme 
for MSMEs, the following two provisions have been introduced for w.e.f.

b. 10 per cent additional Guarantee coverage of up to 85 per cent, as against 
the 75per cent for other entrepreneurs. 
4. 
In order to encourage entrepreneurship among women, the Ministry of MSME 
has implemented the ‘Skill Upgradation & Mahila Coir Yojana’ under ‘Coir Vikas 
Yojana’, which is an exclusive training program aimed at the skill development 
of women artisans engaged in the Coir sector. 
5. 
The Ministry of MSME has also implemented Prime Minister’s Employment 
Generation Programme (“PMEGP”), which is a major credit-linked subsidy 
programme aimed at generating self-employment opportunities through 
establishment of micro enterprises in the non-farm sector.  
6. 
A higher subsidy is being provided to beneficiaries belonging to special 
categories such as Scheduled Caste/Scheduled Tribe/OBC /minorities/women/

7. 
For purposes of encouraging participation of women entrepreneurs in trade 
fairs under the Procurement & Marketing Support Scheme, a higher subsidy is 
provided for women owned MSMEs. 
8. 
“SAMARTH” initiative was launched to support women owned MSMEs with the 
objective of providing Skill Development and Market Development Assistance 
to women and to train more than 7,500 women candidates from rural and sub-
urban areas. Aspiring and existing women entrepreneurs have been 
provisioned with 20 per cent seats in free Skill Development Programs 
organized under Skill Development Schemes of the Ministry of MSME; 20 per 
cent of MSME Business Delegations for domestic & international exhibitions 
under the schemes for Marketing Assistance implemented by the Ministry of 
MSME; and 20 per cent discount on annual processing fee on National Small 
Industries Corporation’s Scheme.
